# Daily Summary: 2025-05-30

### Scalable Complexity Control Facilitates Reasoning Ability of LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23013v1)
- **Authors**: Liangkai Hang, Junjie Yao, Zhiwei Bai, Tianyi Chen, Yang Chen, Rongjie Diao, Hezhou Li, Pengxiao Lin, Zhiwei Wang, Cheng Xu, Zhongwang Zhang, Zhangchen Zhou, Zhiyu Li, Zehao Lin, Kai Chen, Feiyu Xiong, Yaoyu Zhang, Weinan E, Hongkang Yang, Zhi-Qin John Xu
- **Abstract**: The reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs) has been rapidly advancing in recent years, attracting interest in more fundamental approaches that can reliably enhance their generalizability. This work demonstrates that model complexity control, conveniently implementable by adjusting the initialization rate and weight decay coefficient, improves the scaling law of LLMs consistently over varying model sizes and data sizes. This gain is further illustrated by comparing the benchmark performance of 2.4B models pretrained on 1T tokens with different complexity hyperparameters. Instead of fixing the initialization std, we found that a constant initialization rate (the exponent of std) enables the scaling law to descend faster in both model and data sizes. These results indicate that complexity control is a promising direction for the continual advancement of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of model complexity control on the reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors argue that by carefully adjusting the initialization rate and weight decay (parameters affecting model complexity), they can improve the scaling laws of LLMs – meaning better performance with increasing model and data size.  They demonstrate this empirically by training LLMs (up to 2.4B parameters) on large datasets (up to 1T tokens) using different complexity hyperparameters. Their results indicate that a constant initialization rate (expressed as an exponent of the standard deviation related to network width) leads to faster descent in the scaling law, suggesting improved performance and sample efficiency compared to traditional initialization methods.  The paper also offers some theoretical analysis and examines the internal representations of the models to provide insights into how complexity control affects learning and reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of controlling model complexity isn't entirely new. Techniques like weight decay and regularization have long been used for this purpose.  Previous works also explored the impact of initialization schemes. However, the paper's novelty lies in:

    *   **Systematic investigation:**  It provides a more thorough and systematic exploration of how *simultaneously* tuning initialization rates and weight decay affects the scaling behavior and downstream performance of LLMs.  Prior work often focused on one or the other, or on training stability rather than comprehensive reasoning benchmarks.
    *   **Focus on reasoning:**  The explicit focus on reasoning ability as the primary outcome is valuable. While generalizability has been a goal, the paper frames it specifically in the context of enhancing LLM reasoning.
    *   **Scaling law perspective:** The paper examines complexity control within the context of scaling laws, connecting it directly to the question of how to make LLMs learn more efficiently with larger data and model sizes. The finding about constant initialization rate as the correct invariant for scaling is interesting.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because:

    *   **Practical implications:** Complexity control strategies outlined are relatively simple to implement (adjusting initialization parameters and weight decay), making them practically relevant for LLM pre-training.
    *   **Improved performance:** The empirical results showing performance gains across a range of benchmarks (specifically +4.6% for 0.9B model and +3.4% for 2.4B) lend strong support to the effectiveness of the approach. The consistent improvement in performance with small complexity across various tasks indicates a practical way to improve current LLMs.
    *   **Theoretical insights:** The theoretical analysis, while heuristic, offers possible explanations for the observed benefits, linking complexity control to the types of functions learned and the structure of the network.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited model sizes:** The largest model size (2.4B) is modest by current standards. The trends observed need to be validated on much larger models to confirm their scalability.
    *   **Heuristic analysis:** The theoretical analysis is insightful but still relatively high-level and relies on some strong assumptions. More rigorous theoretical treatment would be beneficial.
    *   **Data dependence:** The superior performance of smaller complexity models is potentially reliant on the high quality of the pre-training dataset. The observations regarding different complexity models should be analyzed in datasets with different noise levels and sizes.
    *   **Black box:** It's not fully clear *why* the specific initialization rate works as well as it does. Further probing into the resulting learned parameters and representations could be helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the pre-training practices of LLMs. If the results hold at larger scales, it could lead to more efficient and better-performing models by providing a principled way to tune complexity during pre-training.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7**.

*   The paper addresses a crucial problem in LLM research: how to improve reasoning and generalization.
*   The methodology is sound, involving systematic experimentation across different model sizes, data sizes, and a range of benchmark tasks.
*   The empirical results are compelling, showing consistent performance improvements with the proposed complexity control strategies.
*   The theoretical analysis offers plausible explanations for the observed benefits.
*   The main limitations are the relatively small model size and the dependence on a specific initialization approach. These leave open questions about the true generalizability of the method at scale.

Despite these limitations, the paper makes a meaningful contribution by providing a systematic exploration of complexity control in LLMs, demonstrating its potential to improve reasoning abilities, and offering practical guidelines for pre-training. The theoretical insights, while not definitive, are a valuable step towards understanding why these techniques work. A higher score would require more definitive evidence of scalability and a more rigorous theoretical foundation.

### Detecting Stealthy Backdoor Samples based on Intra-class Distance for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23015v1)
- **Authors**: Jinwen Chen, Hainan Zhang, Fei Sun, Qinnan Zhang, Sijia Wen, Ziwei Wang, Zhiming Zheng
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning LLMs with datasets containing stealthy backdoors from publishers poses security risks to downstream applications. Mainstream detection methods either identify poisoned samples by analyzing the prediction probability of poisoned classification models or rely on the rewriting model to eliminate the stealthy triggers. However, the former cannot be applied to generation tasks, while the latter may degrade generation performance and introduce new triggers. Therefore, efficiently eliminating stealthy poisoned samples for LLMs remains an urgent problem. We observe that after applying TF-IDF clustering to the sample response, there are notable differences in the intra-class distances between clean and poisoned samples. Poisoned samples tend to cluster closely because of their specific malicious outputs, whereas clean samples are more scattered due to their more varied responses. Thus, in this paper, we propose a stealthy backdoor sample detection method based on Reference-Filtration and Tfidf-Clustering mechanisms (RFTC). Specifically, we first compare the sample response with the reference model's outputs and consider the sample suspicious if there's a significant discrepancy. And then we perform TF-IDF clustering on these suspicious samples to identify the true poisoned samples based on the intra-class distance. Experiments on two machine translation datasets and one QA dataset demonstrate that RFTC outperforms baselines in backdoor detection and model performance. Further analysis of different reference models also confirms the effectiveness of our Reference-Filtration.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of detecting stealthy backdoor samples in datasets used to fine-tune Large Language Models (LLMs). It proposes a novel method called Reference-Filtration and Tfidf-Clustering (RFTC). RFTC first filters out suspicious samples by comparing their responses to those of a smaller, task-specific "reference" model. Samples with significant discrepancies are deemed suspicious. Then, it performs TF-IDF clustering on these suspicious samples, identifying true poisoned samples based on the intra-class distance of their responses. The intuition is that poisoned samples will cluster more tightly due to their specific malicious outputs, while clean samples will be more dispersed.  Experiments on machine translation and QA datasets demonstrate that RFTC outperforms baselines in backdoor detection rate, model performance, and computational efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in combining reference model filtering with TF-IDF clustering based on response analysis, specifically for *generation* tasks.  While reference models have been used in related areas, the combination with TF-IDF clustering on the *response* itself, rather than the input or model parameters, is a distinct contribution, especially in the context of stealthy backdoors. The observation that poisoned *outputs* cluster more tightly is valuable. Previous work has concentrated on word trigger or input characteristics.  However, reference model filtering, by itself, is not entirely novel, and TF-IDF clustering is a relatively standard technique. The integration is the key element of novelty.
*   **Significance:**  The problem of backdoor attacks in LLMs is undeniably significant, posing serious security risks.  A method that effectively detects stealthy backdoors *without* significantly degrading performance is valuable. The claim of improved computational efficiency is also important, as many existing defense mechanisms are computationally expensive, hindering their practical application. The empirical results appear promising, showing improvements over established baselines.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a critical and timely problem in LLM security.
    *   The proposed RFTC method offers a novel combination of techniques tailored for generation tasks.
    *   The paper presents a clear and logical justification for the RFTC approach.
    *   Experiments demonstrate improved performance over baselines across different datasets and attack types.
    *   The analysis of reference model selection is insightful.
    *   Computational efficiency claim is important for LLM-scale models.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the idea of clustering on the *response* is novel, the individual components (reference filtering, TF-IDF clustering) are well-established.
    *   The reliance on a "reference" model introduces a dependency. While the paper explores different reference model sizes, the selection of a *suitable* reference model for a given task may still present a challenge in practice.  A more detailed discussion of robustness to reference model limitations (e.g., biases) would be beneficial.
    *   The experimental section samples 10,000 examples. While this is a decent sample size, it may not be fully representative of performance on larger, real-world datasets. Scaling the approach to full datasets might reveal limitations not apparent in the smaller experiments.
    *   The performance increase from the ROUGE-1 metric seems moderate. More detailed result analysis would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the method proves robust and scalable in more realistic scenarios, it could become a valuable tool for dataset curation and LLM security. The idea of focusing on output clustering to identify poisoned samples may inspire other related research directions.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a useful and somewhat novel contribution to an important area. The method is well-motivated and has strong experimental support relative to baselines. The weakness lies in the novelty not being groundbreaking, rather an integration, and certain experimental limitations.

### Sensitivity of DC Network Representation for GIC Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23016v1)
- **Authors**: Aniruddh Mishra, Arthur K. Barnes, Jose E. Tabarez, Adam Mate
- **Abstract**: Geomagnetic disturbances are a threat to the reliability and security of our national critical energy infrastructures. These events specifically result in geomagnetically induced currents, which can cause damage to transformers due to magnetic saturation. In order to mitigate these effects, blocker devices must be placed in optimal locations. Finding this placement requires a dc representation of the ac transmission lines, which this paper discusses. Different decisions in this process, including the method of representing the blocking devices, result in significant variations to the power loss calculations. To analyze these effects, we conclude the paper by comparing the losses on a sample network with different modeling implementations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the sensitivity of DC network representations to variations in modeling choices, specifically for Geomagnetically Induced Current (GIC) analysis and mitigation. It focuses on the impact of different modeling decisions related to GIC blockers (line series capacitors, transformer neutral blockers, and substation ground approximations) on power loss calculations. The paper analyzes these effects using a case study and compares power losses across different blocking configurations, uniform and non-uniform electric field models, and with different mitigation strategies.  The analysis utilizes PowerModelsGMD.jl to generate and analyze the DC networks and PowerWorld Simulator for validation and specific blocker implementations. The paper concludes that different modeling approaches can significantly affect results, highlighting the importance of careful consideration in GIC mitigation planning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic exploration of the *sensitivity* of GIC power loss calculations to different *modeling choices* for blocking devices. While individual models for GIC, transformers, and blockers exist, the direct comparative analysis of how specific modeling decisions alter the outcome of power loss calculations and, therefore, mitigation strategies, is less common. Existing literature often focuses on a single modeling approach or on sensitivity to *network parameters* (line resistance, grounding), but not explicitly on the sensitivity to the representation of the *mitigation devices* themselves.

**Significance:**  The significance is moderate. GIC mitigation is a crucial area for power grid resilience.  By highlighting the importance of accurate blocker representation, the paper contributes to a more informed approach to GIC mitigation planning. If mitigation strategies are based on inaccurate power loss estimates, the placement and types of blockers could be sub-optimal, leading to inadequate protection or unnecessary investment. The paper's finding that uniform electric fields may mask certain system vulnerabilities has practical implications for grid operators who rely on simplified analysis for preliminary assessments. This can lead to better allocation of resources for mitigation efforts by pointing out where the models and assumptions lead to the most difference in the results.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and motivation for the study.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The investigation covers a reasonable range of modeling options and scenarios.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The findings have direct implications for GIC mitigation strategies.
*   **Reproducible Research:** The use of PowerModelsGMD.jl and the availability of the test case online contributes to reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The case study is relatively small. While insightful, it is limited in generalizability to very large-scale, real-world power grids.
*   **Depth of Analysis:** While the paper covers several factors, it could benefit from a deeper dive into the *reasons* behind the observed sensitivities. For example, a more detailed analysis of how specific network topologies or transformer configurations interact with different blocker representations to influence power loss calculations could strengthen the findings. While the paper notes this is due to implicit circuits, quantifying these connections in terms of admittances and power flows can aid the reader in understanding and further extending the work.
*   **Lack of Quantitative Validation:** The paper uses PowerWorld Simulator and theoretical considerations for validation but lacks comparison to real-world GIC event data (which is admittedly difficult to obtain) or to a higher-fidelity EMTP-type simulation for a more comprehensive comparison.
*   **Presentation:** The paper suffers from minor presentational issues, such as unnecessary repetition.

**Impact:**

The paper's impact will likely be in prompting more careful consideration of modeling choices in GIC analysis. It contributes to the ongoing effort to improve the accuracy and reliability of GIC mitigation planning, but it is not a groundbreaking contribution that will fundamentally reshape the field.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6**. Here's the rationale:

*   **+1 for Novelty:** The focus on *sensitivity* to *modeling assumptions* for *mitigation devices* is more novel than simply applying existing GIC models.
*   **+1 for Practical Relevance:** The findings directly influence how GIC mitigation is planned and implemented, which is beneficial.
*   **+1 for Clarity:** The problem is well-defined and the analysis is clearly presented.
*   **+1 for Reproducibility:** Sharing code and test data is essential for others to build upon the work.
*   **-1 for Limited Scope:** The small case study limits the generalizability of the findings. The conclusions are valid within the specific constraints of the test network, but further analysis would need to be done before extrapolating the results to large national-scale power systems.
*   **-1 for Lack of Quantitative Validation:** Validation primarily relies on theoretical considerations. Real-world data or more detailed simulation comparisons would bolster confidence in the results.

### Stairway to Success: Zero-Shot Floor-Aware Object-Goal Navigation via LLM-Driven Coarse-to-Fine Exploration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23019v1)
- **Authors**: Zeying Gong, Rong Li, Tianshuai Hu, Ronghe Qiu, Lingdong Kong, Lingfeng Zhang, Yiyi Ding, Leying Zhang, Junwei Liang
- **Abstract**: Object-Goal Navigation (OGN) remains challenging in real-world, multi-floor environments and under open-vocabulary object descriptions. We observe that most episodes in widely used benchmarks such as HM3D and MP3D involve multi-floor buildings, with many requiring explicit floor transitions. However, existing methods are often limited to single-floor settings or predefined object categories. To address these limitations, we tackle two key challenges: (1) efficient cross-level planning and (2) zero-shot object-goal navigation (ZS-OGN), where agents must interpret novel object descriptions without prior exposure. We propose ASCENT, a framework that combines a Multi-Floor Spatial Abstraction module for hierarchical semantic mapping and a Coarse-to-Fine Frontier Reasoning module leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) for context-aware exploration, without requiring additional training on new object semantics or locomotion data. Our method outperforms state-of-the-art ZS-OGN approaches on HM3D and MP3D benchmarks while enabling efficient multi-floor navigation. We further validate its practicality through real-world deployment on a quadruped robot, achieving successful object exploration across unseen floors.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper "Stairway to Success: Zero-Shot Floor-Aware Object-Goal Navigation via LLM-Driven Coarse-to-Fine Exploration" introduces ASCENT, a novel framework for zero-shot object-goal navigation (ZS-OGN) in multi-floor environments. The method leverages a Multi-Floor Spatial Abstraction module to create a hierarchical semantic map and a Coarse-to-Fine Frontier Reasoning module that uses large language models (LLMs) to guide exploration. ASCENT addresses the limitations of existing methods that struggle with cross-level planning and open-vocabulary object descriptions. The framework's performance is evaluated on HM3D and MP3D benchmarks, demonstrating state-of-the-art results and enabling efficient multi-floor navigation. The practicality of ASCENT is further validated through real-world deployment on a quadruped robot.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a compelling approach to a challenging problem in embodied AI: zero-shot object-goal navigation in complex, multi-floor environments. While some components build on existing ideas, the combination of these elements and the overall system design demonstrate notable novelty and significance.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Critical Gap:** The paper directly addresses the limitations of existing OGN and ZS-OGN methods regarding multi-floor navigation. The analysis of HM3D and MP3D datasets effectively highlights the importance of this aspect.

*   **Multi-Floor Spatial Abstraction:** The proposed module is a significant contribution, enabling efficient cross-level planning by combining intra-floor and inter-floor relationships into a unified representation. This is a clear improvement over existing approaches that often neglect or inadequately handle floor transitions.

*   **Coarse-to-Fine Reasoning:** The use of LLMs for context-aware exploration, combined with a value map for prioritizing frontiers, is a clever way to integrate semantic understanding and environmental priors. This module enhances navigation efficiency and decision interpretability compared to methods relying solely on local matching or frequent LLM calls.

*   **State-of-the-Art Performance:** The experimental results on HM3D and MP3D benchmarks demonstrate that ASCENT achieves state-of-the-art performance in ZS-OGN, with substantial improvements in SR and SPL compared to existing methods like MFNP and VLFM.

*   **Real-World Validation:** The deployment on a quadruped robot and demonstration of successful object exploration across unseen floors adds credibility and highlights the practical relevance of the approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited LLM Use** While the coarse-to-fine frontier reasoning module using LLMs appears effective, some might argue that the LLM's role could be expanded further to more fully integrate semantic understanding into the entire navigation process. The current implementation has a few calls.

*   **Dependence on Prior Knowledge:** Although the authors acknowledge the limitations of relying on dataset-derived priors, the system's performance is still affected by mismatches between training and testing environments. The prior is more of a crutch here; an LLM is capable of making the same decision without prior, so the utility is not high.

*   **Room for architectural improvements:** The paper itself already admits to limitations that come as a result of some design choices. Given that a limitation is admitted, the novelty is slightly reduced by it as it is more apparent that the design decisions have not been thoroughly considered.

*   **Limited analysis of error conditions:** The paper indicates certain types of errors the framework is more susceptible to without any concrete solutions provided, suggesting an incomplete research process to the problem.

**Overall Significance:**

The paper presents a well-designed and effective solution for zero-shot object-goal navigation in multi-floor environments. The proposed framework addresses several critical limitations of existing methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks. The real-world validation further strengthens the significance of the work. While there are some weaknesses, the contributions of this paper are substantial and have the potential to significantly influence the field of embodied AI and robotics.

### AgentAlign: Navigating Safety Alignment in the Shift from Informative to Agentic Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23020v1)
- **Authors**: Jinchuan Zhang, Lu Yin, Yan Zhou, Songlin Hu
- **Abstract**: The acquisition of agentic capabilities has transformed LLMs from "knowledge providers" to "action executors", a trend that while expanding LLMs' capability boundaries, significantly increases their susceptibility to malicious use. Previous work has shown that current LLM-based agents execute numerous malicious tasks even without being attacked, indicating a deficiency in agentic use safety alignment during the post-training phase. To address this gap, we propose AgentAlign, a novel framework that leverages abstract behavior chains as a medium for safety alignment data synthesis. By instantiating these behavior chains in simulated environments with diverse tool instances, our framework enables the generation of highly authentic and executable instructions while capturing complex multi-step dynamics. The framework further ensures model utility by proportionally synthesizing benign instructions through non-malicious interpretations of behavior chains, precisely calibrating the boundary between helpfulness and harmlessness. Evaluation results on AgentHarm demonstrate that fine-tuning three families of open-source models using our method substantially improves their safety (35.8% to 79.5% improvement) while minimally impacting or even positively enhancing their helpfulness, outperforming various prompting methods. The dataset and code have both been open-sourced.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "AgentAlign: Navigating Safety Alignment in the Shift from Informative to Agentic Large Language Models" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the emerging safety risks associated with the increasing agentic capabilities of large language models (LLMs).  As LLMs transition from providing information to executing actions through tools, their potential for malicious use significantly increases. The authors propose AgentAlign, a novel framework for safety alignment that leverages abstract behavior chains as a medium for synthesizing safety alignment data.  AgentAlign instantiates these behavior chains in simulated environments with diverse tool instances, generating authentic and executable instructions. The framework also ensures utility by proportionally synthesizing benign instructions, balancing helpfulness and harmlessness. Experimental results on the AgentHarm benchmark demonstrate that fine-tuning open-source models with AgentAlign substantially improves their safety, while minimally impacting or even enhancing their helpfulness, outperforming various prompting methods. The authors release their dataset and code.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:

*   **Focus on Agentic Safety:**  The explicit focus on the safety challenges presented *specifically* by the agentic capabilities of LLMs is well-justified and timely.  While previous work addressed LLM safety in general, this paper targets the unique risks introduced when LLMs can take actions in the world via tools.
*   **Abstract Behavior Chains:** The use of abstract behavior chains as an intermediate representation for synthesizing both harmful and benign agentic instructions is a clever and potentially powerful technique.  This approach allows for the systematic generation of diverse and realistic scenarios.
*   **Simulated Environment:** The creation of a simulated environment with diverse tool instances is crucial for generating executable instructions. This grounding in practicality differentiates this work from previous approaches that might generate less-realistic instructions.
*   **Utility Preservation:**  The proportional synthesis of benign instructions using the same behavior chains is a significant contribution, aiming to address the common problem of over-alignment leading to reduced utility.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from:

*   **Addressing a Critical Gap:** It directly tackles a crucial gap in LLM safety alignment: the lack of specific alignment strategies for agentic LLMs. This is particularly relevant as these models become more prevalent.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results on AgentHarm demonstrate a substantial improvement in safety across different model families, providing compelling evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.  The positive impact or minimal impact on helpfulness is an important outcome.
*   **Open-Sourcing:** The release of the AgentAlign dataset and code promotes reproducibility and facilitates further research in this area.
*   **Balanced Approach:** The framework's deliberate design to maintain a balance between harmlessness and helpfulness addresses a practical problem hindering widespread adoption of aligned models.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of agentic LLM safety.
*   **Novel approach:** The abstract behavior chain and simulated environment approach are innovative.
*   **Rigorous evaluation:** The experimental setup is well-defined, and the results are statistically significant.
*   **Practical considerations:** The paper focuses on generating executable instructions and balancing safety and utility.
*   **Reproducible research:** Data and code are released to the public.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simulated Environment Limitations:**  The realism of the simulated environment is a potential limitation.  While the authors strived for authenticity, there will inevitably be differences between simulated and real-world tool interactions.  The impact of these differences on the generalizability of the safety alignment remains to be seen.
*   **Reliance on LLMs for instruction generation/evaluation:** The approach depends on LLMs for synthesizing instructions and evaluation, which may introduce biases or inaccuracies. Although quality control steps exist, these issues cannot be fully eliminated.
*   **Scalability to more complex tasks:** The paper focuses on tasks within a limited scope of tool usage. How well AgentAlign scales to scenarios involving more complex reasoning and tool interactions needs further exploration.
*   **Potentially limited impact:** It may not be straightforward to incorporate into the training pipeline of large foundation models due to the extra computation needed to simulate environment and the reliance on LLMs for instruction synthesis/evaluation.

**Justification of Score:**

This is a strong paper that makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM safety.  The focus on agentic safety, the novel framework, and the empirical validation are all commendable. While the limitations associated with simulated environments and reliance on LLMs for instruction/evaluation exist, the proposed approach represents a significant step forward in addressing a critical emerging challenge. The clear presentation and reproducibility enhance the paper's impact. However, some of the weaknesses mentioned above prevents the paper from receiving an exceptional score.

### Context Robust Knowledge Editing for Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23026v1)
- **Authors**: Haewon Park, Gyubin Choi, Minjun Kim, Yohan Jo
- **Abstract**: Knowledge editing (KE) methods offer an efficient way to modify knowledge in large language models. Current KE evaluations typically assess editing success by considering only the edited knowledge without any preceding contexts. In real-world applications, however, preceding contexts often trigger the retrieval of the original knowledge and undermine the intended edit. To address this issue, we develop CHED -- a benchmark designed to evaluate the context robustness of KE methods. Evaluations on CHED show that they often fail when preceding contexts are present. To mitigate this shortcoming, we introduce CoRE, a KE method designed to strengthen context robustness by minimizing context-sensitive variance in hidden states of the model for edited knowledge. This method not only improves the editing success rate in situations where a preceding context is present but also preserves the overall capabilities of the model. We provide an in-depth analysis of the differing impacts of preceding contexts when introduced as user utterances versus assistant responses, and we dissect attention-score patterns to assess how specific tokens influence editing success.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of current knowledge editing (KE) methods in language models, which primarily evaluate editing success in isolation without considering preceding contexts. The authors argue that in real-world scenarios, context significantly impacts the model's ability to recall the edited knowledge. To address this, they introduce CHED, a new benchmark dataset designed to evaluate context robustness in KE. CHED consists of fact triplets with prefixes crafted to be semantically relevant and distracting. The paper also proposes CoRE, a novel KE method that aims to enhance context robustness by minimizing the variance of hidden states for the edited knowledge across different prefix contexts. Evaluations demonstrate that CoRE improves editing success in the presence of challenging contexts and preserves the overall capabilities of the model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper identifies a crucial gap in the evaluation of knowledge editing techniques, highlighting the importance of context robustness, which is often overlooked in existing benchmarks. This is a significant step forward and addresses a vital practical limitation of KE methods. The CHED dataset is a novel contribution, specifically designed to stress-test KE methods in contextual settings. The proposed CoRE method, regularizing hidden state variance, presents a new direction for achieving context-robust knowledge editing.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have implications for the deployment of KE methods in real-world applications, as they emphasize the need to account for contextual effects. The introduction of CHED provides a standardized way to assess and compare the context robustness of different KE techniques. The performance gains of CoRE over existing methods further highlight its potential as a practical and effective approach. The analysis of user vs. assistant contexts and the impact of hop words deepens our understanding of contextual effects on knowledge recall in LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Formulation:** Clearly articulates the problem of context-dependent KE failure and provides compelling examples.
    *   **Dataset Creation:** Meticulously constructs a challenging and realistic benchmark (CHED) using Wikidata, carefully considering distractiveness and relevance of prefix contexts.
    *   **Methodological Soundness:** Proposes a theoretically grounded and empirically validated method (CoRE) for enhancing context robustness, leveraging hidden state regularization.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Performs extensive experiments and analyses, providing valuable insights into the behavior of KE methods under different contexts.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on MEMIT:** The CoRE method builds upon the locate-then-edit paradigm, specifically MEMIT. Although justified by its scalability, exploring other editing paradigms may offer broader insights.
    *   **Context Construction:** The method focuses on automatically generated prefix contexts. While useful, they might lack the nuance and complexity of human-generated conversations.
    *   **Dataset Size**: CHED while novel, still relies on a relatively limited subset of counterfact. Scale up dataset size could further highlight the challenge of context robustness.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the development and evaluation of future KE methods, pushing the field towards more practical and robust techniques. CHED is expected to become a valuable resource for benchmarking context robustness in KE, and CoRE offers a promising approach for mitigating contextual effects.

**Justification:**

The paper makes several important contributions to the field of knowledge editing. It provides a valuable new benchmark dataset in CHED, highlights the practical significance of context robustness, and introduces an effective technique for enhancing context robustness. The weaknesses related to paradigm reliance and dataset size are not insignificant, but the overall impact of the paper is substantial. The work addresses an underexplored and important limitation of knowledge editing methods, contributing to practical and robust deployment of these techniques in the real world.

### Case-Based Reasoning Enhances the Predictive Power of LLMs in Drug-Drug Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23034v1)
- **Authors**: Guangyi Liu, Yongqi Zhang, Xunyuan Liu, Quanming Yao
- **Abstract**: Drug-drug interaction (DDI) prediction is critical for treatment safety. While large language models (LLMs) show promise in pharmaceutical tasks, their effectiveness in DDI prediction remains challenging. Inspired by the well-established clinical practice where physicians routinely reference similar historical cases to guide their decisions through case-based reasoning (CBR), we propose CBR-DDI, a novel framework that distills pharmacological principles from historical cases to improve LLM reasoning for DDI tasks. CBR-DDI constructs a knowledge repository by leveraging LLMs to extract pharmacological insights and graph neural networks (GNNs) to model drug associations. A hybrid retrieval mechanism and dual-layer knowledge-enhanced prompting allow LLMs to effectively retrieve and reuse relevant cases. We further introduce a representative sampling strategy for dynamic case refinement. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CBR-DDI achieves state-of-the-art performance, with a significant 28.7% accuracy improvement over both popular LLMs and CBR baseline, while maintaining high interpretability and flexibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper "Case-Based Reasoning Enhances the Predictive Power of LLMs in Drug-Drug Interaction" introduces CBR-DDI, a novel framework for improving the prediction of drug-drug interactions (DDIs) by leveraging case-based reasoning (CBR) with large language models (LLMs). CBR-DDI constructs a knowledge repository of historical DDI cases, where each case is enriched with pharmacological insights extracted by an LLM and structured drug associations modeled by a graph neural network (GNN). The framework employs a hybrid retrieval mechanism and dual-layer knowledge-enhanced prompting to enable LLMs to retrieve and reuse relevant cases for DDI prediction.  The authors also introduce a representative sampling strategy for dynamic case refinement. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, with a significant accuracy improvement compared to baseline LLMs and a naive CBR approach.  The method also maintains high interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining CBR with LLMs for DDI prediction possesses novelty. The use of LLMs to extract pharmacological insights *and* GNNs to model drug associations in a structured way, forming a comprehensive case representation, is a significant contribution. The hybrid retrieval mechanism and dual-layer prompting strategies enhance the system's ability to leverage the knowledge repository effectively. The dynamic case refinement approach also shows an effort to improve scalability.

*   **Significance:** Accurate DDI prediction is a critical task in pharmacology and healthcare. The paper's ability to significantly improve DDI prediction accuracy, particularly in scenarios involving new drugs where data is scarce, makes a significant contribution. The improvement over both base LLMs and naive CBR baselines highlights the value of the proposed hybrid approach. Furthermore, interpretability is a crucial factor in clinical decision-making, and CBR-DDI's ability to provide explainable predictions is a strong advantage.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:**  The integration of LLMs, GNNs, and CBR provides a well-rounded framework that addresses key challenges in DDI prediction.
    *   **Interpretability:** The incorporation of interaction mechanisms extracted by the LLM improves the interpretability of the predictions, a critical aspect in clinical settings.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents substantial experimental results across multiple benchmarks, demonstrating significant performance gains.
    *   **Addressing Cold-Start Problem:** By leveraging existing cases, CBR-DDI offers a way to improve predictions for new drugs with limited interaction data.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:**  While dynamic case refinement addresses scalability to an extent, the computational cost associated with LLM inference and GNN training remains a potential limitation. This isn't thoroughly discussed.
    *   **Reliance on External Knowledge:** The performance of the framework heavily relies on the quality and completeness of the biomedical KG. The paper could address how the model handles missing or inaccurate knowledge.
    *   **Text-Based Approach:** The exclusive reliance on textual information, without considering drug molecular structures, constrains the model's capacity for detailed interaction analysis at the molecular level. The authors also do not include details on how the method addresses hallucinated text from the LLM.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies for some components are brief. More thorough ablation studies would better illustrate the contribution of each component. For instance, a deeper exploration of different components or design choices within each major part of the framework.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach of combining LLMs and CBR has the potential to influence research in other areas of biomedical knowledge discovery, beyond just DDI prediction. It could inspire new methods for leveraging LLMs to reason with structured knowledge in other domains, such as drug repurposing, personalized medicine, or disease diagnosis.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the integrated approach, the significance of the improved DDI prediction accuracy, particularly in cold-start scenarios, and the strong experimental results, the paper represents a noteworthy contribution to the field. However, the limitations regarding computational cost, reliance on external knowledge, and the purely text-based nature of the approach prevent it from achieving a higher score. In this context, a fair and critical evaluation leads to the following score:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving Multilingual Social Media Insights: Aspect-based Comment Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23037v1)
- **Authors**: Longyin Zhang, Bowei Zou, Ai Ti Aw
- **Abstract**: The inherent nature of social media posts, characterized by the freedom of language use with a disjointed array of diverse opinions and topics, poses significant challenges to downstream NLP tasks such as comment clustering, comment summarization, and social media opinion analysis. To address this, we propose a granular level of identifying and generating aspect terms from individual comments to guide model attention. Specifically, we leverage multilingual large language models with supervised fine-tuning for comment aspect term generation (CAT-G), further aligning the model's predictions with human expectations through DPO. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method in enhancing the comprehension of social media discourse on two NLP tasks. Moreover, this paper contributes the first multilingual CAT-G test set on English, Chinese, Malay, and Bahasa Indonesian. As LLM capabilities vary among languages, this test set allows for a comparative analysis of performance across languages with varying levels of LLM proficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to improve multilingual social media analysis by focusing on Comment Aspect Term Generation (CATG).  The authors argue that social media comments are often noisy and contain language mismatches, hindering tasks like comment clustering and opinion mining. To address this, they propose extracting aspect terms from individual comments to guide model attention, essentially acting as a form of noise cancellation.  Key contributions include:

*   A multilingual CATG test dataset in English, Chinese, Malay, and Bahasa Indonesian.
*   Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) for CATG, further refined using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to align model outputs with human expert annotations.
*   Integrating the CATG system into downstream monolingual and cross-lingual comment clustering tasks, demonstrating performance improvements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of a multilingual CATG dataset is a valuable contribution. While aspect term extraction is not a new task in general, applying it specifically to noisy social media comments across multiple languages, with a focus on Southeast Asian languages, adds a unique angle. The application of DPO to align LLM outputs with human annotations in this context is also a worthwhile exploration. The focus on SEA languages is interesting, as it addresses a gap in resources for low-resource languages.
*   **Significance:** The demonstrated improvements in comment clustering (ComC) using the CATG-augmented approach highlight the practical value of the proposed method. By improving the representation of comments, the authors facilitate more accurate downstream analysis.  The work has the potential to improve social media monitoring and analysis efforts, especially in multilingual settings. This can have implications for understanding public opinion, identifying misinformation, and managing online discourse.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The multilingual dataset is a key strength, filling a gap in existing resources.
    *   The use of DPO for aligning LLM outputs with human annotations addresses a common problem of LLM-generated text diverging from human preferences.
    *   The integration of CATG into a downstream task provides concrete evidence of its effectiveness.
    * The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    * The limitations section acknowledges that a significant performance gap persists between the model and human annotations.
    *   The reliance on a relatively small, noisy dataset of online comments means the generalizability of the approach may be limited. It's unclear how well it would perform on other domains or with different types of social media data.
    *   The impact of biases in the pre-training data or the fine-tuning data on the model's outputs is not fully explored, although they acknowledge this in the risks section. Further analysis on identifying and mitigating potential biases may be warranted.

* **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper provides a useful dataset for multilingual aspect-based comment analysis in SEA languages. The approach is sound and well-explained. However, the benefits are modest and incremental, as the performance improvements from CATG are limited. Also, there is no comparison with other techniques for handling noise and cross-linguistic issues in social media data. Given these points, the significance and impact are not transformative.

**Score: 6**

The paper presents a valuable dataset and a well-executed methodology, but the limited performance improvements and the lack of comparison to other baselines somewhat diminish its overall impact. It's a solid contribution, but not a groundbreaking one.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### EL4NER: Ensemble Learning for Named Entity Recognition via Multiple Small-Parameter Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23038v1)
- **Authors**: Yuzhen Xiao, Jiahe Song, Yongxin Xu, Ruizhe Zhang, Yiqi Xiao, Xin Lu, Runchuan Zhu, Bowen Jiang, Junfeng Zhao
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) technique based on Large Language Models (LLMs) has gained prominence in Named Entity Recognition (NER) tasks for its lower computing resource consumption, less manual labeling overhead, and stronger generalizability. Nevertheless, most ICL-based NER methods depend on large-parameter LLMs: the open-source models demand substantial computational resources for deployment and inference, while the closed-source ones incur high API costs, raise data-privacy concerns, and hinder community collaboration. To address this question, we propose an Ensemble Learning Method for Named Entity Recognition (EL4NER), which aims at aggregating the ICL outputs of multiple open-source, small-parameter LLMs to enhance overall performance in NER tasks at less deployment and inference cost. Specifically, our method comprises three key components. First, we design a task decomposition-based pipeline that facilitates deep, multi-stage ensemble learning. Second, we introduce a novel span-level sentence similarity algorithm to establish an ICL demonstration retrieval mechanism better suited for NER tasks. Third, we incorporate a self-validation mechanism to mitigate the noise introduced during the ensemble process. We evaluated EL4NER on multiple widely adopted NER datasets from diverse domains. Our experimental results indicate that EL4NER surpasses most closed-source, large-parameter LLM-based methods at a lower parameter cost and even attains state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance among ICL-based methods on certain datasets. These results show the parameter efficiency of EL4NER and underscore the feasibility of employing open-source, small-parameter LLMs within the ICL paradigm for NER tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "EL4NER: Ensemble Learning for Named Entity Recognition via Multiple Small-Parameter Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EL4NER, an ensemble learning method designed to improve Named Entity Recognition (NER) performance using multiple open-source, small-parameter Large Language Models (LLMs).  EL4NER aims to overcome the limitations of relying on large, closed-source LLMs (high API costs, data privacy issues) or computationally expensive open-source large models.  It employs a task decomposition-based pipeline (span extraction and classification), a span-level sentence similarity algorithm for In-Context Learning (ICL) demonstration retrieval, and a self-validation mechanism to reduce noise. The authors demonstrate that EL4NER can achieve competitive or even state-of-the-art performance on various NER datasets compared to methods utilizing larger LLMs, while significantly reducing computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core idea of using ensemble learning with small LLMs for NER is interesting and addresses a practical problem: reducing the computational burden and cost associated with large LLMs. The specific contributions – the task decomposition pipeline, span-level similarity for retrieval, and self-validation – represent incremental but valuable improvements over existing ICL-based NER methods.  While ensemble learning itself isn't new, its application to NER in this particular way, leveraging ICL with smaller models, has some novelty. The focus on a more parameter-efficient approach is welcome. The specific implementation details are novel but not truly ground-breaking.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to make NER more accessible and practical. Reducing computational costs and dependency on proprietary LLMs is a crucial step for wider adoption, especially in resource-constrained environments. The experimental results, showing competitive performance with smaller models, strengthen this argument. The demonstration of improved results while leveraging existing open-source projects promotes a more collaborative environment. However, the improvements, while significant, are not always consistent across datasets.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Practical Problem Addressed:**  The paper directly tackles the real-world challenge of deploying NER systems in scenarios with limited resources or concerns about using closed-source APIs.
    * **Well-Defined Approach:** EL4NER's components are clearly described and well-motivated, offering a logical and coherent pipeline.
    * **Detailed Experimental Evaluation:** The experiments are thorough and comprehensive, using diverse datasets and ablation studies to assess the contribution of each component.  The comparison against strong baselines is also a plus.
    * **Focus on Open-Source and Parameter Efficiency:** It emphasizes the use of open-source models and a smaller parameter count, promoting greater accessibility.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvements:** While the individual components contribute to the overall performance, they are not entirely novel concepts in isolation.  The paper primarily combines and adapts existing techniques within a specific NER framework.
    * **Dataset Dependency:** The performance gains of EL4NER are not consistent across all datasets.  While it achieves SOTA on some, its performance on others is only competitive. This raises questions about the generalizability of the approach and the tuning required for new datasets.
    * **Limited Analysis of Specific LLM strengths:** While the method uses an ensemble of LLMs, it would benefit from analysis of why each LLM works on different aspects, and how the fusion works. 
    * **Black Box Nature:** LLMs can be black boxes, making it hard to know precisely *why* the ensemble approach helps. Greater insight into this would improve the work.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research by encouraging exploration of more parameter-efficient methods for NER and other NLP tasks.  It could also inspire further development of ensemble learning techniques specifically tailored to the characteristics of smaller LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid and well-executed approach to a relevant problem, demonstrating that competitive NER performance can be achieved with smaller, open-source LLMs through ensemble learning. However, the novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary, and the performance improvements are not uniformly consistent across datasets. Therefore, while a valuable contribution, it doesn't warrant an exceptionally high score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Theory to Application: Fine-Tuning Large EEG Model with Real-World Stress Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23042v1)
- **Authors**: Siwen Wang, Shitou Zhang, Wan-Lin Chen, Dung Truong, Tzyy-Ping Jung
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models have inspired the development of foundation models across various domains. In this study, we evaluate the efficacy of Large EEG Models (LEMs) by fine-tuning LaBraM, a state-of-the-art foundation EEG model, on a real-world stress classification dataset collected in a graduate classroom. Unlike previous studies that primarily evaluate LEMs using data from controlled clinical settings, our work assesses their applicability to real-world environments. We train a binary classifier that distinguishes between normal and elevated stress states using resting-state EEG data recorded from 18 graduate students during a class session. The best-performing fine-tuned model achieves a balanced accuracy of 90.47% with a 5-second window, significantly outperforming traditional stress classifiers in both accuracy and inference efficiency. We further evaluate the robustness of the fine-tuned LEM under random data shuffling and reduced channel counts. These results demonstrate the capability of LEMs to effectively process real-world EEG data and highlight their potential to revolutionize brain-computer interface applications by shifting the focus from model-centric to data-centric design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the applicability of Large EEG Models (LEMs) to real-world stress classification. Specifically, it fine-tunes LaBraM, a state-of-the-art LEM, on an EEG dataset collected in a classroom environment. The results demonstrate that the fine-tuned model achieves a balanced accuracy of 90.47% in distinguishing between normal and elevated stress states using a 5-second EEG window. The paper further examines the model's robustness to data shuffling and channel reduction and compares its performance with that of a newly initialized model without pre-training. The findings suggest that LEMs can effectively process real-world EEG data and offer a data-centric alternative to traditional BCI model development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Real-world Application:** The paper's primary strength lies in its evaluation of LEMs on real-world EEG data, addressing a gap in previous studies that mainly focused on controlled laboratory settings. This is a significant step towards practical BCI applications.
    *   **High Accuracy:** Achieving 90.47% balanced accuracy in stress classification with a 5-second window is a notable accomplishment, highlighting the potential of LEMs for efficient and accurate inference.
    *   **Robustness Analysis:** The paper systematically examines the robustness of the fine-tuned LEM with respect to data shuffling, channel reduction, and initialization.
    *   **Comparison with Traditional Methods:** The comparison with the results from previous studies provides a benchmark to evaluate the performance of the model.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** The dataset, while collected in a real-world setting, is relatively small, comprising only 18 participants and 92 recordings. This limitation impacts the generalizability and statistical power of the results. The claim "robustness" is weakly supported by small dataset size ( only 2 elevated data point in the test dataset).
    *   **Data Augmentation:** While data augmentation is used, the effectiveness of this technique may be limited, as it only introduces slight variations of existing data.
    *   **Limited Interpretability:** The paper briefly mentions the "black box" nature of LEMs and does not delve into any interpretability analysis. This omission hinders understanding of how the model makes its predictions and limits its practical utility.
    *   **Overfitting risk:** It seems from the graphs in Fig. 2 and Fig. 3 that overfitting might be an issue.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper showcases the applicability of LEMs in a practical setting. The findings are significant because they suggest that LEMs can potentially revolutionize BCI development by shifting the focus from manual model architecture design to data-centric approaches. However, the conclusions need to be validated with larger and more diverse datasets.
    *   While the technical contributions in terms of modifying the LEM are not significant, applying existing LEMs to real-world data and analyzing the results is potentially novel, particularly for stress detection.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of 6. The paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating the applicability of LEMs to a real-world stress classification task. However, the limited dataset size, lack of interpretability analysis, and potential overfitting concerns temper the impact of these findings. While the results are promising, further research is needed to validate the generalizability and robustness of the proposed approach with larger, more diverse datasets, as well as methods for making these models more interpretable and transparent.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### DenoiseRotator: Enhance Pruning Robustness for LLMs via Importance Concentration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23049v1)
- **Authors**: Tianteng Gu, Bei Liu, Bo Xiao, Ke Zeng, Jiacheng Liu, Yanmin Qian
- **Abstract**: Pruning is a widely used technique to compress large language models (LLMs) by removing unimportant weights, but it often suffers from significant performance degradation - especially under semi-structured sparsity constraints. Existing pruning methods primarily focus on estimating the importance of individual weights, which limits their ability to preserve critical capabilities of the model. In this work, we propose a new perspective: rather than merely selecting which weights to prune, we first redistribute parameter importance to make the model inherently more amenable to pruning. By minimizing the information entropy of normalized importance scores, our approach concentrates importance onto a smaller subset of weights, thereby enhancing pruning robustness. We instantiate this idea through DenoiseRotator, which applies learnable orthogonal transformations to the model's weight matrices. Our method is model-agnostic and can be seamlessly integrated with existing pruning techniques such as Magnitude, SparseGPT, and Wanda. Evaluated on LLaMA3, Qwen2.5, and Mistral models under 50% unstructured and 2:4 semi-structured sparsity, DenoiseRotator consistently improves perplexity and zero-shot accuracy. For instance, on LLaMA3-70B pruned with SparseGPT at 2:4 semi-structured sparsity, DenoiseRotator reduces the perplexity gap to the dense model by 58%, narrowing the degradation from 8.1 to 3.4 points. Codes are available at https://github.com/Axel-gu/DenoiseRotator.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DenoiseRotator paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DenoiseRotator, a novel framework designed to enhance the robustness of large language model (LLM) pruning.  Instead of solely focusing on identifying which weights to prune (the traditional approach), DenoiseRotator aims to redistribute parameter importance before pruning, making the model inherently more prunable. It achieves this through learnable orthogonal transformations applied to the weight matrices of the model.  The transformations are trained to concentrate importance scores onto a smaller subset of weights by minimizing the information entropy of normalized importance scores. The method is model-agnostic and integrates seamlessly with existing pruning techniques like Magnitude, SparseGPT, and Wanda. Empirical results on LLaMA3, Qwen2.5, and Mistral models demonstrate consistent improvements in perplexity and zero-shot accuracy under both unstructured and semi-structured sparsity patterns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of reshaping the importance distribution *before* pruning, rather than just selecting weights to prune, is a significant contribution. This fundamentally differs from most existing pruning approaches, which operate within the fixed parameter space of the pre-trained model. The use of orthogonal transformations to achieve this redistribution leverages the computational invariance property of transformer architectures, which is clever. However, similar approaches exist in quantization (e.g. QuaRot) and ROTPruner.

* **Significance:** The paper's contribution is significant because it directly addresses the challenges of pruning LLMs, particularly under semi-structured sparsity constraints, which are crucial for hardware acceleration. The demonstrated improvements in perplexity and zero-shot accuracy are compelling. The modular "plug-and-play" nature of DenoiseRotator is also a considerable strength, as it allows it to be easily incorporated into existing pruning pipelines.  The demonstrated results of significantly reducing the perplexity gap compared to dense models post pruning, especially on larger models like LLaMA3-70B, are practically meaningful.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Principled Approach:** The method is based on a sound theoretical foundation: minimizing information entropy. This provides a rationale for the effectiveness of the approach and distinguishes it from purely heuristic methods.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents extensive experimental results across various models, sparsity levels, and evaluation tasks, demonstrating the consistent effectiveness of DenoiseRotator.
    * **Practicality:** The method is model-agnostic and compatible with existing pruning techniques, making it readily usable in real-world scenarios. Also, overhead during inference is relatively low.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Theoretical Depth:** While the entropy minimization is a good starting point, a more in-depth theoretical analysis of *why* concentrating importance leads to better pruning performance would strengthen the paper. A more rigorous explanation of the "random permutation" effect for semi-structured sparsity would also be beneficial.
    * **Lack of Comparison to Related Work:** The paper would benefit from a more detailed comparison with other methods that leverage orthogonal transformations for model compression, especially techniques such as ROTPruner and QuaRot. A clear explanation of how DenoiseRotator differs from and outperforms these existing approaches is essential.
    * **Limited Exploration of Sparsity Patterns:** While the paper considers both unstructured and 2:4 sparsity, exploring the performance of DenoiseRotator under different, more sophisticated sparsity patterns (e.g., block sparsity) would further demonstrate its generalizability.
    * **Finetuning:** It lacks comparison with models that have undergone finetuning after pruning. Finetuning significantly improves model performance.
* **Concerns:** DenoiseRotator requires orthogonal matrices, which needs significant computational resources to maintain the orthogonality. Also, it adds matrix multiplication during inference.

* **Potential Influence:**  DenoiseRotator could influence future research in pruning by shifting the focus from solely selecting weights to prune to also reshaping the underlying importance landscape. The plug-and-play nature of the method may also encourage its adoption in practical LLM compression pipelines.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and practically valuable approach to LLM pruning with strong empirical support, it has some theoretical and comparative limitations. The idea of reshaping the importance distribution is a compelling one, and the results are significant enough to suggest the broader community can benefit from this method. However, the lack of a more detailed theoretical analysis, the omission of explicit comparison to related methods using similar transformations, and no comparison with finetuning limit its novelty and completeness. These considerations temper its potential impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Query Routing for Retrieval-Augmented Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23052v1)
- **Authors**: Jiarui Zhang, Xiangyu Liu, Yong Hu, Chaoyue Niu, Fan Wu, Guihai Chen
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) significantly improves the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on knowledge-intensive tasks. However, varying response quality across LLMs under RAG necessitates intelligent routing mechanisms, which select the most suitable model for each query from multiple retrieval-augmented LLMs via a dedicated router model. We observe that external documents dynamically affect LLMs' ability to answer queries, while existing routing methods, which rely on static parametric knowledge representations, exhibit suboptimal performance in RAG scenarios. To address this, we formally define the new retrieval-augmented LLM routing problem, incorporating the influence of retrieved documents into the routing framework. We propose RAGRouter, a RAG-aware routing design, which leverages document embeddings and RAG capability embeddings with contrastive learning to capture knowledge representation shifts and enable informed routing decisions. Extensive experiments on diverse knowledge-intensive tasks and retrieval settings show that RAGRouter outperforms the best individual LLM by 3.61% on average and existing routing methods by 3.29%-9.33%. With an extended score-threshold-based mechanism, it also achieves strong performance-efficiency trade-offs under low-latency constraints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of routing queries to the most suitable Large Language Model (LLM) in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines.  It argues that existing routing methods, which primarily rely on static LLM knowledge representations, are suboptimal in RAG settings because they fail to account for the dynamic influence of retrieved documents on LLM response quality. To address this, the paper formally defines the problem of RAG-aware LLM routing and proposes a novel routing framework called RAGRouter. RAGRouter incorporates document embeddings, RAG capability embeddings, and a contrastive learning objective to capture knowledge representation shifts induced by retrieved documents. The paper presents experimental results on various knowledge-intensive tasks and retrieval settings, demonstrating that RAGRouter outperforms existing methods and the best individual LLM.  It also proposes a score-threshold-based mechanism to achieve performance-efficiency trade-offs under low-latency constraints.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strength:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its explicit consideration of the RAG context in LLM routing. Most previous routing methods are designed for non-RAG scenarios and assume static LLM knowledge. RAGRouter directly addresses the dynamic interplay between LLMs and retrieved documents, representing a crucial step forward. The contrastive learning approach, using both Cross-Setting and Intra-Setting contrast, appears to be a novel and effective way to capture knowledge shifts due to the introduction of retrieved documents.
*   **Weakness:** Some elements of the proposed approach, such as the use of document embeddings and cross-encoders, are not entirely new in the broader context of RAG research. The core contribution rests on the novel integration of these elements within a routing framework and the contrastive learning strategy.

**Significance:**

*   **Strength:**  The work is significant because it tackles a relevant and increasingly important problem. RAG is becoming a standard paradigm for improving LLM performance, and efficient routing is essential for scaling and optimizing RAG pipelines.  The paper’s finding that static knowledge-based routing is insufficient in RAG settings is valuable for the community. The improvement in performance demonstrated by RAGRouter shows the potential of RAG-aware routing.
*   **Weakness:** While the experiments are comprehensive across various datasets and models, it's unclear how well RAGRouter would generalize to drastically different RAG architectures (e.g., those with more complex retrieval or generation stages).  The gains, while significant, aren’t overwhelmingly large. The paper could benefit from deeper analysis of failure cases or scenarios where RAGRouter still struggles. Furthermore, the focus on Cover Exact Match as a metric, while commonly used, might not fully capture the nuances of response quality.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by explicitly addressing the LLM routing problem within RAG. The contrastive learning approach and the incorporation of document embeddings are promising. The empirical evaluation is thorough.

However, the incremental nature of some elements and the lack of analysis of potential edge cases prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. Although it has a clear novelty by tackling the RAG-aware LLM routing, the general concept of contrastive learning and LLM routing have already been present in existing work. Moreover, the experiments can have a deeper evaluation on potential cases where the proposed RAGRouter may not perform optimally. Therefore, this is not a high breakthrough research that would significantly impact the current status.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Augment or Not? A Comparative Study of Pure and Augmented Large Language Model Recommenders
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23053v1)
- **Authors**: Wei-Hsiang Huang, Chen-Wei Ke, Wei-Ning Chiu, Yu-Xuan Su, Chun-Chun Yang, Chieh-Yuan Cheng, Yun-Nung Chen, Pu-Jen Cheng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have introduced new paradigms for recommender systems by enabling richer semantic understanding and incorporating implicit world knowledge. In this study, we propose a systematic taxonomy that classifies existing approaches into two categories: (1) Pure LLM Recommenders, which rely solely on LLMs, and (2) Augmented LLM Recommenders, which integrate additional non-LLM techniques to enhance performance. This taxonomy provides a novel lens through which to examine the evolving landscape of LLM-based recommendation. To support fair comparison, we introduce a unified evaluation platform that benchmarks representative models under consistent experimental settings, highlighting key design choices that impact effectiveness. We conclude by discussing open challenges and outlining promising directions for future research. This work offers both a comprehensive overview and practical guidance for advancing next-generation LLM-powered recommender.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comparative study of Large Language Model (LLM) Recommenders, categorizing them into two main groups: Pure LLM Recommenders (relying solely on LLMs) and Augmented LLM Recommenders (integrating non-LLM techniques). It proposes a systematic taxonomy for these categories and introduces a unified evaluation platform to benchmark representative models under consistent experimental settings. The study identifies key design choices impacting effectiveness, discusses open challenges, and outlines future research directions for LLM-powered recommender systems. The paper also includes a fair comparison of different methods through dedicated experiments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper’s novelty lies primarily in its systematic taxonomy and the unified evaluation platform. While using LLMs for recommendation and augmenting them with other techniques are not entirely new, the structured classification based on the "augmentation map" (presence or absence of non-LLM techniques) offers a valuable perspective.  The emphasis on isolating the impact of different design choices through consistent experiments is also a strength.  However, the individual techniques used within each category (e.g., instruction tuning, embedding utilization, collaborative filtering integration) are largely drawn from existing literature, so the core algorithms lack fundamental innovation.

**Significance:** The paper contributes to the field by:

*   **Providing Clarity:** The taxonomy helps organize the rapidly growing body of research on LLM Recommenders, making it easier to understand the different approaches.
*   **Enabling Fair Comparison:** The unified evaluation platform facilitates a more objective comparison of different models.
*   **Highlighting Key Factors:** The identification of key design choices that impact performance provides practical guidance for researchers and practitioners.
*   **Identifying Challenges:** The discussion of challenges such as the distribution gap and echo chamber effects brings attention to important issues that need to be addressed.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses primarily on scenarios where LLMs are the final decision-makers, excluding cases where LLMs play supporting roles. This narrows the scope and could miss important contributions.
*   **Incremental Improvements:** While the paper offers a valuable high-level organization, the experimental improvements stemming from integrating non-LLM models into recommendation LLMs do not introduce a fundamental breakthrough, thus lacking a game-changing improvement compared to existing methodologies.
*   **Experimental Datasets:** The usage of Amazon'23 data in isolation, although more current, might not provide sufficient generalization ability and could have used a bigger panel of available datasets for thorough results.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field of LLM Recommenders, offering a well-organized framework and a fair evaluation platform. However, the lack of fundamental algorithmic innovation and the limited scope prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. The focus is more on systemizing and evaluating existing approaches than on introducing completely new concepts. Therefore, a score of 7 is appropriate. The paper provides a significant consolidation of efforts, but lacks a groundbreaking innovation that would have warranted a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Be.FM: Open Foundation Models for Human Behavior
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23058v1)
- **Authors**: Yutong Xie, Zhuoheng Li, Xiyuan Wang, Yijun Pan, Qijia Liu, Xingzhi Cui, Kuang-Yu Lo, Ruoyi Gao, Xingjian Zhang, Jin Huang, Walter Yuan, Matthew O. Jackson, Qiaozhu Mei
- **Abstract**: Despite their success in numerous fields, the potential of foundation models for modeling and understanding human behavior remains largely unexplored. We introduce Be.FM, one of the first open foundation models designed for human behavior modeling.
