    *   Dataset curation reliance on LLM as well.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the above analysis, I assign the paper a score of **6**. The work presents a valuable dataset and a reasonable context-modeling architecture. The experimental results indicate some performance gains over baselines. However, the limited technical depth, evaluation methodology, and comparison restrict the overall impact. The ideas are interesting and the new dataset could provide great value, but the paper should be considered more of an incremental improvement than a groundbreaking advance. The methodology (and in particular the dataset curation) have been greatly boosted by LLMs, but this has also introduced significant bias.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### PBEBench: A Multi-Step Programming by Examples Reasoning Benchmark inspired by Historical Linguistics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23126v1)
- **Authors**: Atharva Naik, Darsh Agrawal, Manav Kapadnis, Yuwei An, Yash Mathur, Carolyn Rose, David Mortensen
- **Abstract**: Recently, long chain of thought (LCoT), Large Language Models (LLMs), have taken the machine learning world by storm with their breathtaking reasoning capabilities. However, are the abstract reasoning abilities of these models general enough for problems of practical importance? Unlike past work, which has focused mainly on math, coding, and data wrangling, we focus on a historical linguistics-inspired inductive reasoning problem, formulated as Programming by Examples. We develop a fully automated pipeline for dynamically generating a benchmark for this task with controllable difficulty in order to tackle scalability and contamination issues to which many reasoning benchmarks are subject. Using our pipeline, we generate a test set with nearly 1k instances that is challenging for all state-of-the-art reasoning LLMs, with the best model (Claude-3.7-Sonnet) achieving a mere 54% pass rate, demonstrating that LCoT LLMs still struggle with a class or reasoning that is ubiquitous in historical linguistics as well as many other domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "PBEBench," a new benchmark for evaluating the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The benchmark is inspired by problems in historical linguistics, specifically forward reconstruction – the task of inferring sound change rules and their ordering given sets of etymologically related words. The authors develop an automated pipeline for generating benchmark instances with controllable difficulty, addressing scalability and contamination concerns present in many existing reasoning benchmarks.  The paper demonstrates that state-of-the-art reasoning LLMs still struggle on this task, even with relatively small program sizes and simple rules, suggesting that the abstract reasoning abilities of these models are not yet sufficient for this type of complex, multi-step reasoning. The authors analyze the complexity of the generated problems and relate this complexity to the performance of various LLMs. They also show that, while some features are correlated with difficulty (e.g., cascade length), reasoning about “opaque” rule orderings remains a challenge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in:

    *   **Domain Application:** The application of Program By Example (PBE) principles to a historical linguistics problem (forward reconstruction) is a fresh perspective.  Existing PBE benchmarks are usually focused on math, coding, or data manipulation.  The shift to a linguistic problem with specific constraints is an interesting change.
    *   **Benchmark Generation Pipeline:**  The fully automated benchmark generation pipeline is a strength.  The ability to dynamically create problems and control difficulty is a significant benefit, particularly in light of contamination concerns in other benchmarks.
    *   **Formalization of Relations:** The formalization of feeding and bleeding relationships provides a theoretically sound basis for the task, and the proof of correctness is crucial for the consistency of the benchmark.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is arguable:

    *   **Reasoning Evaluation:**  It provides another valuable tool for assessing LLM reasoning abilities. Showing limitations of current LLMs on a novel reasoning task helps to direct future research.
    *   **Practical Application:** The historical linguistics domain itself may have limited direct practical applications. However, the type of reasoning required (inferring ordered sequences of transformations) is relevant to other domains (e.g., planning, robotics).
    *   **Scalability and Contamination:** Addresses challenges related to scalability and contamination, but is ultimately limited by degenerate programs and skewed distributions of features.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Task:** The forward reconstruction problem is well-defined and explained, with clear examples.
    *   **Automated Benchmark Generation:** The pipeline's ability to create diverse and scalable datasets is a significant advantage.
    *   **Controllable Difficulty:**  The control over difficulty via the generation parameters is useful for targeted model evaluation.
    *   **Analysis of Results:** The analysis of model performance relative to problem complexity provides insights into where LLMs struggle.
    *   **Proof of Correctness:** The inclusion of formal definitions and proofs bolsters the rigor of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Linguistic Complexity:** The rules are simplified to string rewriting for PBE purposes, limiting the scope and richness of real-world historical linguistic analysis.
    *   **Skewed Distribution:**  The issue with "degenerate" programs leading to a skewed distribution of relations is a notable weakness.  The fact that only a small fraction of instances contain bleeding relations, means it does not accurately measure models ability to reason about those specific linguistic characteristics.
    *   **Domain Relevance:** While the reasoning is relevant, the relatively niche application in historical linguistics means that researchers in more central AI areas may not find it relevant.
    *   **Overstated Claims:** The paper claims their benchmark is more resistant to leakage, but that claim is only verifiable with future use of the benchmark.

*   **Influence on the field:**
    *   The benchmark provides a novel test domain, which can influence future research in improving LLM's reasoning abilities.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is novel in its approach to reasoning benchmark generation by drawing inspiration from historical linguistics. The automated pipeline provides a solid foundation for scalable and customizable benchmark creation. However, issues with skewed data distributions, limited linguistic depth, and potentially overstated claims limit its ultimate impact. Future work that addresses these limitations could significantly enhance the value of the benchmark. Taking into account the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of **6** is warranted.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### VERINA: Benchmarking Verifiable Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23135v1)
- **Authors**: Zhe Ye, Zhengxu Yan, Jingxuan He, Timothe Kasriel, Kaiyu Yang, Dawn Song
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated in software development, but ensuring correctness in LLM-generated code remains challenging and often requires costly manual review. Verifiable code generation -- jointly generating code, specifications, and proofs of code-specification alignment -- offers a promising path to address this limitation and further unleash LLMs' benefits in coding. Yet, there exists a significant gap in evaluation: current benchmarks often lack support for end-to-end verifiable code generation. In this paper, we introduce Verina (Verifiable Code Generation Arena), a high-quality benchmark enabling a comprehensive and modular evaluation of code, specification, and proof generation as well as their compositions. Verina consists of 189 manually curated coding tasks in Lean, with detailed problem descriptions, reference implementations, formal specifications, and extensive test suites. Our extensive evaluation of state-of-the-art LLMs reveals significant challenges in verifiable code generation, especially in proof generation, underscoring the need for improving LLM-based theorem provers in verification domains. The best model, OpenAI o4-mini, generates only 61.4% correct code, 51.0% sound and complete specifications, and 3.6% successful proofs, with one trial per task. We hope Verina will catalyze progress in verifiable code generation by providing a rigorous and comprehensive benchmark. We release our dataset on https://huggingface.co/datasets/sunblaze-ucb/verina and our evaluation code on https://github.com/sunblaze-ucb/verina.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VERINA: Benchmarking Verifiable Code Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VERINA, a new benchmark for evaluating verifiable code generation using Large Language Models (LLMs).  VERINA consists of 189 manually curated coding tasks in Lean, a general-purpose programming language with interactive theorem proving (ITP) capabilities.  Each task includes a detailed problem description, reference implementation, formal specification (pre- and post-conditions), formal proof (optional but present in some tasks), and a comprehensive test suite.  The benchmark facilitates the evaluation of code generation, specification generation, proof generation, and combinations thereof. The authors evaluate several state-of-the-art LLMs using VERINA, revealing significant challenges, especially in proof generation, highlighting the need for better LLM-based theorem provers. They release the dataset and evaluation code to encourage further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in creating a *comprehensive* and *modular* benchmark for verifiable code generation, specifically targeting interactive theorem proving (ITP) within the Lean environment. While there are existing benchmarks for code generation and even verification, VERINA is a more complete arena for evaluating all three core tasks and their combinations in a single unified environment. It addresses a clear gap in resources available for evaluating end-to-end verifiable code generation. The choice of Lean is also somewhat novel, as many existing benchmarks are based on SMT solvers using Dafny/Verus. However, similar work Clover exist which provides a full support of these tasks.

*   **Significance:** VERINA's significance stems from the increasing importance of ensuring the correctness of LLM-generated code.  The benchmark provides a rigorous platform for researchers to track progress in verifiable code generation, which has the potential to dramatically improve software reliability. By enabling modular evaluation, VERINA allows researchers to focus on specific challenges, such as improving proof generation or generating accurate specifications. The paper's findings highlighting the current limitations of LLMs in this domain are valuable for guiding future research directions. The release of the dataset and evaluation tools will facilitate further exploration.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **High-quality data:** Manual curation and verification of problems, code, specifications, and test suites ensure data quality.
    *   **Comprehensive scope:** VERINA covers code, specification, and proof generation, offering a holistic view of verifiable code generation.
    *   **Modular design:** The benchmark allows for flexible evaluation of individual tasks and combinations thereof.
    *   **Practical evaluation metrics:** The paper introduces a testing-based approach to evaluate specification quality, overcoming the limitations of LLM-based provers.
    *   **Choice of Lean:** Lean’s interactive theorem proving environment offers greater visibility into the proving process.
    *   Comprehensive test suite covering both positive and negative cases.
    *   Clearly defined soundness and completeness metrics for specification evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited size:** 189 examples, while carefully curated, might not be sufficient to fully capture the diversity of real-world programming tasks. Also it is a relatively small number comparing to other large scale benchmarks.
    *   **Task simplicity:** The tasks are standalone programs, which may not fully represent the complexity of real-world verification projects.
    *   **Domain Specificity:** While the tasks are taken from a variety of sources, they are still limited to a small domain.
    *   **Dependency on Testing-Based Metric:** the SpecGen metric is testing based, so could be fragile for cases involving complicated logical properties. In particular, its metric will result `unknown` for such cases.

*   **Potential Influence:** VERINA has the potential to become a standard benchmark in the field of verifiable code generation, driving research and development in this area. The insights gained from VERINA could lead to improved LLM-based tools for software development, enhancing code reliability and reducing the need for costly manual review. The benchmark could also influence the design of new programming languages and verification tools that are better suited for LLM-assisted development.

**Justification for the Score:**

Given the paper's novelty in providing a comprehensive and modular benchmark for verifiable code generation using Lean, along with its potential to drive research and development in this increasingly important area, a score of 7 is warranted. The high-quality data, practical evaluation metrics, and insightful findings demonstrate the value of VERINA as a resource for the community. The limitations in dataset size and task complexity, prevents it from receiving a higher rating.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing Large Language Models'Machine Translation via Dynamic Focus Anchoring
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23140v1)
- **Authors**: Qiuyu Ding, Zhiqiang Cao, Hailong Cao, Tiejun Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large language models have demonstrated exceptional performance across multiple crosslingual NLP tasks, including machine translation (MT). However, persistent challenges remain in addressing context-sensitive units (CSUs), such as polysemous words. These CSUs not only affect the local translation accuracy of LLMs, but also affect LLMs' understanding capability for sentences and tasks, and even lead to translation failure. To address this problem, we propose a simple but effective method to enhance LLMs' MT capabilities by acquiring CSUs and applying semantic focus. Specifically, we dynamically analyze and identify translation challenges, then incorporate them into LLMs in a structured manner to mitigate mistranslations or misunderstandings of CSUs caused by information flattening. Efficiently activate LLMs to identify and apply relevant knowledge from its vast data pool in this way, ensuring more accurate translations for translating difficult terms. On a benchmark dataset of MT, our proposed method achieved competitive performance compared to multiple existing open-sourced MT baseline models. It demonstrates effectiveness and robustness across multiple language pairs, including both similar language pairs and distant language pairs. Notably, the proposed method requires no additional model training and enhances LLMs' performance across multiple NLP tasks with minimal resource consumption.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a method called Dynamic Focus Anchoring (DFA) to improve the machine translation performance of large language models (LLMs). DFA addresses the "semantic ambiguity" problem, which arises when LLMs struggle with context-sensitive units (CSUs) like polysemous words, domain-specific terms, and culturally specific concepts. DFA consists of two steps: identifying and classifying CSUs using both external knowledge (lexicons) and internal knowledge activation within the LLM and then injecting semantic focus into a structured prompt by explicitly guiding the LLM to focus on these CSUs during translation. The authors evaluate DFA on WMT benchmark datasets for English-German and English-Chinese translation using Llama2-7b and Llama3-8b, demonstrating improvements over several baseline LLM-based translation systems without requiring additional model training or parallel data. Ablation studies, analyses of polysemy and CSU numbers, and case studies are provided to support the claims.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the semantic ambiguity problem in LLM-based MT, specifically focusing on the challenges posed by CSUs.
*   **Simple and effective method:** DFA is a relatively straightforward method to implement, and the results show a consistent improvement across different language pairs and backbone models. The method is resource-efficient, requiring no additional model training or parallel data.
*   **Dual-path approach:** Using both external and internal knowledge for CSU identification offers a robust mechanism.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough experimental evaluation, including ablation studies, analyses of key parameters, and case studies. Comparisons against strong baseline LLMs are presented.
*   **Clear and well-written:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:**  The core idea of guiding the LLM's attention to challenging words isn't entirely novel. Similar prompt engineering techniques have been explored before, although this paper provides a specific structure with the introduction of external data for identification.  The "dynamic" aspect might be argued to be overstated.
*   **Reliance on External Resources:** While the paper argues against the need for training data, it relies on external resources like the MUSE dataset for identifying polysemous words, which presents a dependency. Moreover, the construction of the dictionary impacts the results and may not be available in all LLM-supported languages.
*   **Limited Generalizability Discussions:** The discussion on generalizability of DFA beyond the specifically chosen CSU types is limited. Do similar techniques work for more complex cases?
*   **Incremental improvements:** The improvements reported are incremental and might not always be statistically significant, although they are generally consistent. The improvement in the Llama3 case over Bayling2 is marginal, which calls into question DFA's effectiveness on an already strong baseline model.
*   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** While analysis of 'k' parameter is provided, the paper lacks sensitivity analysis for the parameters used in the clustering mechanism (word embeddings) in the external knowledge acquition. This makes it difficult to generalize the results to other datasets or new languages.

**Significance:**

The paper demonstrates that a simple, targeted approach to prompt engineering can improve the translation accuracy of LLMs by explicitly addressing the semantic ambiguity problem. The resource efficiency of DFA makes it a practical solution for enhancing LLM-based MT without the need for extensive training or data. Despite the novelty limitations, the results contribute to a better understanding of how to guide LLMs to leverage their knowledge more effectively for complex translation tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The DFA method presents an incremental advancement within the field of LLM-based MT. While it tackles a relevant problem and demonstrates consistent performance gains, the approach leans more towards an application of existing prompt engineering concepts than a groundbreaking innovation.  The reliance on external resources for CSU identification and the marginal improvements with strong baseline models further temper the overall significance. However, the comprehensive evaluation, clear problem definition, resource efficiency, and contribution to our understanding of how to guide LLMs justify a moderate score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Implicit Inversion turns CLIP into a Decoder
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23161v1)
- **Authors**: Antonio D'Orazio, Maria Rosaria Briglia, Donato Crisostomi, Dario Loi, Emanuele Rodolà, Iacopo Masi
- **Abstract**: CLIP is a discriminative model trained to align images and text in a shared embedding space. Due to its multimodal structure, it serves as the backbone of many generative pipelines, where a decoder is trained to map from the shared space back to images. In this work, we show that image synthesis is nevertheless possible using CLIP alone -- without any decoder, training, or fine-tuning. Our approach optimizes a frequency-aware implicit neural representation that encourages coarse-to-fine generation by stratifying frequencies across network layers. To stabilize this inverse mapping, we introduce adversarially robust initialization, a lightweight Orthogonal Procrustes projection to align local text and image embeddings, and a blending loss that anchors outputs to natural image statistics. Without altering CLIP's weights, this framework unlocks capabilities such as text-to-image generation, style transfer, and image reconstruction. These findings suggest that discriminative models may hold untapped generative potential, hidden in plain sight.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a method called CLIP-1, which achieves text-to-image generation using only the CLIP model, without requiring a separate decoder, training, or fine-tuning.  CLIP is a discriminative model and this paper shows it can also perform generative tasks. CLIP-1 optimizes an implicit neural representation (INR) guided by the CLIP text embedding. To improve the quality, it employs several key techniques: frequency-aware INR optimization, robust INR initialization using adversarial weight perturbation, and an orthogonal Procrustes projection to align text and image embeddings. The paper demonstrates that CLIP-1 can perform text-to-image synthesis, image reconstruction, controlled edits, and style transfer.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in demonstrating that a frozen, pre-trained CLIP model can be directly inverted for image generation without any additional training or decoders. This is a surprising result, given CLIP's discriminative nature. Using INRs for this inversion is a good choice, allowing for efficient optimization and coarse-to-fine generation. Adversarial robustness initialization (AWP) is a worthwhile method to stabilize the inverse mapping, and Procrustes alignment helps to tackle the modality gap between text and image embeddings in CLIP.
*   **Significance:**  The significance is multi-fold:
    *   **Rethinking pre-trained models:** It suggests that discriminative models might have untapped generative capabilities.
    *   **Efficiency:** It offers a potentially more efficient alternative to current generative pipelines, which rely heavily on complex and computationally expensive decoders (e.g., diffusion models).
    *   **Generalizability:** It demonstrates that this method can perform multiple tasks without requiring any task-specific training or modifications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem and the proposed solution.
    *   The technical components (frequency-aware INRs, AWP, Procrustes alignment) are well-motivated and integrated.
    *   The experiments are thorough and the results are compelling, especially given the simplicity of the approach. The ablation study clearly shows the impact of each component.
    *   Zero-shot capabilities across different tasks is a strong point.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The image quality, while good, isn't on par with state-of-the-art diffusion models. The visual quality of the generations and detail level is not close to that of larger models such as DALL-E3. The supplementary material shows that the quality is highly variable based on prompt.
    *   The approach depends on finding similar examples in the dataset, which could limit performance for unusual prompts and highlights a dependency on a large and diverse dataset.
    *   The Procrustes alignment step, while helpful, relies on k-NN pairs, which limits the scaling as `k` grows.
    *  It is likely limited by the quality and nature of CLIP embeddings itself, especially for very complex or abstract concepts.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by:
    *   Inspiring further research into inverting pre-trained models for generative tasks.
    *   Motivating the development of more efficient and lightweight generative pipelines.
    *   Shifting the focus from purely generative models to leveraging discriminative models for generation.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

While the visual quality is not on par with SOTA diffusion models, the paper clearly establishes that a pre-trained discriminative model can be effectively repurposed for text-to-image generation, achieving good results without training or fine-tuning any components and providing interesting zero-shot capabilities. This is a valuable addition to the field, inspiring novel perspectives and simplifying existing pipeline approaches. It is an early-stage contribution, that can pave the way for subsequent works to enhance and overcome some current quality limitations.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Infinite-Instruct: Synthesizing Scaling Code instruction Data with Bidirectional Synthesis and Static Verification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23177v1)
- **Authors**: Wenjing Xing, Wenke Lu, Yeheng Duan, Bing Zhao, Zhenghui kang, Yaolong Wang, Kai Gao, Lei Qiao
- **Abstract**: Traditional code instruction data synthesis methods suffer from limited diversity and poor logic. We introduce Infinite-Instruct, an automated framework for synthesizing high-quality question-answer pairs, designed to enhance the code generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs). The framework focuses on improving the internal logic of synthesized problems and the quality of synthesized code. First, "Reverse Construction" transforms code snippets into diverse programming problems. Then, through "Backfeeding Construction," keywords in programming problems are structured into a knowledge graph to reconstruct them into programming problems with stronger internal logic. Finally, a cross-lingual static code analysis pipeline filters invalid samples to ensure data quality. Experiments show that on mainstream code generation benchmarks, our fine-tuned models achieve an average performance improvement of 21.70% on 7B-parameter models and 36.95% on 32B-parameter models. Using less than one-tenth of the instruction fine-tuning data, we achieved performance comparable to the Qwen-2.5-Coder-Instruct. Infinite-Instruct provides a scalable solution for LLM training in programming. We open-source the datasets used in the experiments, including both unfiltered versions and filtered versions via static analysis. The data are available at https://github.com/xingwenjing417/Infinite-Instruct-dataset
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Infinite-Instruct: Synthesizing Scaling Code Instruction Data with Bidirectional Synthesis and Static Verification."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Infinite-Instruct, a novel framework for automatically generating high-quality code instruction data to improve the code generation capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the limitations of existing methods by using a bidirectional approach: "Reverse Construction" converts code snippets into diverse programming problems, and "Backfeeding Construction" leverages a knowledge graph to transform keywords into programming tasks with stronger internal logic. A cross-lingual static code analysis pipeline then filters invalid samples. Experiments show significant performance improvements on code generation benchmarks when fine-tuning models with the generated data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines several existing ideas but in a novel way to address the instruction data bottleneck. The use of OSS-Instruct is not new, but the bidirectional synthesis (Reverse and Backfeeding Construction) and comprehensive static analysis pipeline represent a significant advancement. The knowledge graph approach is intriguing.
*   **Significance:** The ability to automatically generate high-quality instruction data is crucial for scaling LLMs in code generation. The paper's claim of achieving comparable performance to Qwen-2.5-Coder-Instruct with significantly less data is significant.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a key bottleneck in LLM code generation: instruction data scarcity.
    *   Offers a unique bidirectional synthesis approach with Reverse and Backfeeding Construction.
    *   Implements a comprehensive seven-dimensional quality assessment with a cross-lingual static code analysis pipeline.
    *   Demonstrates significant performance gains on multiple benchmarks (MultiPL-E HumanEval, MBPP, etc.).
    *   Provides open-source datasets, including unfiltered and filtered versions, promoting reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The description of the complexity adjustment mechanisms is somewhat simplistic and lacks concrete details. The paper mentions adding constraints, deepening context, etc., but it doesn't elaborate on how these modifications are specifically implemented or controlled.
    *   While static analysis is a valuable quality control step, it acknowledges its limitations in detecting logical errors, algorithmic complexity issues, and architectural design flaws.
    *   The performance gain on Arena Hard is relatively modest, which might indicate that Infinite-Instruct excels more at coding-specific tasks than general knowledge reasoning.
    *   The paper relies heavily on GPT-4 for various tasks (prompt generation, question quality assessment). This dependence could introduce biases from the underlying model and might not be scalable or cost-effective in all scenarios.
    *   While the paper demonstrates improvements, the specific performance of Qwen-2.5-Coder-Instruct (a strong baseline) on some metrics is never reached with the 7B or 32B models, so future research should be done to understand how the methods described in this paper can achieve or exceed SOTA performance in the code generation field.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by promoting automated instruction data synthesis. The bidirectional approach and static verification pipeline offer a valuable framework for other researchers to build upon. The open-sourced datasets will also encourage community contributions and further advancements.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of LLM code generation by proposing a novel framework for automated instruction data synthesis. While there are limitations regarding the simplicity of the difficulty adjustment mechanism, reliance on GPT-4, and not reaching SOTA performance, the significant performance improvements on multiple benchmarks, the unique bidirectional synthesis approach, and the open-sourcing of datasets demonstrate significant value. The impact could be substantial, enabling more efficient training of code-generating LLMs.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DIP-R1: Deep Inspection and Perception with RL Looking Through and Understanding Complex Scenes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23179v1)
- **Authors**: Sungjune Park, Hyunjun Kim, Junho Kim, Seongho Kim, Yong Man Ro
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated significant visual understanding capabilities, yet their fine-grained visual perception in complex real-world scenarios, such as densely crowded public areas, remains limited. Inspired by the recent success of reinforcement learning (RL) in both LLMs and MLLMs, in this paper, we explore how RL can enhance visual perception ability of MLLMs. Then we develop a novel RL-based framework, Deep Inspection and Perception with RL (DIP-R1) designed to enhance the visual perception capabilities of MLLMs, by comprehending complex scenes and looking through visual instances closely. DIP-R1 guides MLLMs through detailed inspection of visual scene via three simply designed rule-based reward modelings. First, we adopt a standard reasoning reward encouraging the model to include three step-by-step processes: 1) reasoning for understanding visual scenes, 2) observing for looking through interested but ambiguous regions, and 3) decision-making for predicting answer. Second, a variance-guided looking reward is designed to examine uncertain regions for the second observing process. It explicitly enables the model to inspect ambiguous areas, improving its ability to mitigate perceptual uncertainties. Third, we model a weighted precision-recall accuracy reward enhancing accurate decision-making. We explore its effectiveness across diverse fine-grained object detection data consisting of challenging real-world environments, such as densely crowded scenes. Built upon existing MLLMs, DIP-R1 achieves consistent and significant improvement across various in-domain and out-of-domain scenarios. It also outperforms various existing baseline models and supervised fine-tuning methods. Our findings highlight the substantial potential of integrating RL into MLLMs for enhancing capabilities in complex real-world perception tasks.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DIP-R1: Deep Inspection and Perception with RL Looking Through and Understanding Complex Scenes":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DIP-R1, a novel Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework designed to enhance the visual perception capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in complex, real-world scenes, particularly those involving dense crowds. The key idea is to guide MLLMs through a detailed inspection process using three rule-based reward modelings: a reasoning reward that encourages step-by-step reasoning, an observation reward that focuses on ambiguous regions, and a decision-making reward that balances precision and recall. Experiments conducted on challenging object detection datasets like CrowdHuman, CityPersons, WiderPedestrian, and UAVDT demonstrate that DIP-R1 significantly improves performance compared to existing MLLMs and supervised fine-tuning methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The concept of using RL to improve the fine-grained visual perception abilities of MLLMs, particularly in complex crowded scenes, presents a reasonable level of novelty.
    *   The proposed DIP-R1 framework with its specific rule-based reward modelings has a novel approach. The reward modelings are designed to encourage reasoning, targeted observation of uncertain regions, and accurate object detection. The variance-guided "looking" reward especially attempts to estimate how much the model is uncertain about its predictions.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper tackles a practical limitation of current MLLMs: their difficulty in handling complex, densely crowded scenes. Addressing this is important for real-world applications such as public safety, autonomous driving, and aerial monitoring.
    *   The empirical results on multiple datasets suggest that DIP-R1 can significantly improve MLLMs' object detection performance in challenging environments. This has the potential to make MLLMs more useful in real-world scenarios.
    *   The paper provides evidence that RL can be effectively integrated with MLLMs to improve their visual perception capabilities without requiring large amounts of labeled data, which can reduce development cost.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The framework is conceptually simple and easy to implement.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate consistent and significant improvements across multiple datasets and baseline models.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of DIP-R1.
    *   The qualitative analysis and visualizations further illustrate the benefits of the framework in crowded scenes.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reward modelings are based on predefined rules, which might not be optimal for all scenarios. It's unclear how robust the framework is to changes in scene characteristics or task requirements.
    *   The experiments focus primarily on object detection. While the improvements are convincing, it's uncertain whether DIP-R1 would be effective for other visual perception tasks (e.g., instance segmentation, scene understanding) in similar complex scenes.
    *   The paper lacks comparisons with other RL-based methods for improving visual perception. Comparing against alternative approaches could provide a stronger assessment of DIP-R1's superiority.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could inspire further research into using RL to improve MLLMs' visual perception capabilities in complex environments.
    *   The DIP-R1 framework provides a practical approach that can be adopted and extended by other researchers and practitioners.
    *   The findings could encourage the development of more robust and reliable MLLMs for real-world applications such as public safety, autonomous driving, and aerial monitoring.

*   **Overall Assessment:**

    This paper presents an innovative approach to address a specific limitation of MLLMs in complex visual scenes. The DIP-R1 framework demonstrates significant performance improvements across multiple datasets. However, the reliance on rule-based reward modelings and the limited scope of experiments warrant further investigation. While the paper demonstrates strong empirical gains, the methodology lacks some of the sophistication needed to merit a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unsupervised Word-level Quality Estimation for Machine Translation Through the Lens of Annotators (Dis)agreement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23183v1)
- **Authors**: Gabriele Sarti, Vilém Zouhar, Malvina Nissim, Arianna Bisazza
- **Abstract**: Word-level quality estimation (WQE) aims to automatically identify fine-grained error spans in machine-translated outputs and has found many uses, including assisting translators during post-editing. Modern WQE techniques are often expensive, involving prompting of large language models or ad-hoc training on large amounts of human-labeled data. In this work, we investigate efficient alternatives exploiting recent advances in language model interpretability and uncertainty quantification to identify translation errors from the inner workings of translation models. In our evaluation spanning 14 metrics across 12 translation directions, we quantify the impact of human label variation on metric performance by using multiple sets of human labels. Our results highlight the untapped potential of unsupervised metrics, the shortcomings of supervised methods when faced with label uncertainty, and the brittleness of single-annotator evaluation practices.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates unsupervised word-level quality estimation (WQE) for machine translation, focusing on exploiting recent advances in language model interpretability and uncertainty quantification to identify translation errors. Instead of relying on costly large language model prompting or ad-hoc training on human-labeled data, the authors explore efficient alternatives based on the internal workings of translation models.  They evaluate 14 metrics across 12 translation directions, using multiple human annotations per text to quantify the impact of human label variation on metric performance. The results highlight the potential of unsupervised metrics, the limitations of supervised methods in the face of label uncertainty, and the fragility of evaluation practices based on single annotators.  The paper concludes with recommendations for future WQE systems, including the importance of considering annotator agreement and the need for calibration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its comprehensive evaluation of unsupervised WQE metrics across a diverse set of languages, models, and, most importantly, multiple sets of human annotations.  While the individual unsupervised metrics themselves may not be entirely novel (some are adaptations of existing techniques), the systematic comparison across conditions and the explicit focus on the impact of human annotator (dis)agreement represent a significant advancement.  The "human-level baseline" provided by analyzing multiple annotations is a crucial contribution to the field, often neglected in prior work due to annotation costs. The proposed confidence weighting strategy for XCOMET calibration adds a practical improvement to supervised methods.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is several-fold:

    *   It demonstrates the potential of unsupervised WQE, offering a more efficient and accessible alternative to expensive LLM-based or heavily supervised approaches. This is particularly important for low-resource languages or scenarios where large labeled datasets are unavailable.

    *   It exposes the limitations of existing supervised WQE methods when dealing with inherent subjectivity and variability in human annotations. This highlights the importance of robust evaluation methodologies that account for inter-annotator disagreement.

    *   It provides practical recommendations for future WQE research, emphasizing the need for multiple annotations and appropriate calibration techniques. These guidelines are valuable for the community to develop more reliable and generalizable WQE systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the research questions, methodology, and results.
    *   The experimental design is rigorous, spanning multiple languages, models, and evaluation metrics.
    *   The use of multiple human annotations provides a valuable "human-level baseline" and allows for a more nuanced understanding of metric performance.
    *   The paper makes practical recommendations for future research and development in WQE.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper demonstrates the potential of unsupervised WQE, the performance of supervised methods, particularly when calibrated, is still generally stronger. The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the specific types of errors that unsupervised methods are better at identifying, to better guide future research.

    *   The reliance on "simulated" error spans derived from post-edits, while practical, is a potential limitation. A direct comparison with error spans annotated according to a specific error typology (e.g., MQM) would provide a more comprehensive evaluation.

    *   While the paper considers multiple annotations, a more in-depth analysis of the *nature* of annotator disagreement would be beneficial. Were the disagreements mainly about severity, or the boundaries of error spans, or something else?  Understanding this could guide the development of better metrics.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper's emphasis on annotator agreement and the need for robust evaluation methodologies is likely to influence future research in WQE.
    *   The demonstration of the potential of unsupervised metrics could spur further research into efficient and accessible WQE methods.
    *   The practical recommendations provided in the paper are likely to be adopted by researchers and developers in the field.

**Overall Score:**

While the individual unsupervised metrics might not be groundbreaking, the paper's systematic evaluation, focus on annotator agreement, and practical recommendations make it a valuable contribution to the field. The paper provides a solid foundation for future research in WQE, emphasizing the need for robust evaluation methodologies and highlighting the potential of efficient unsupervised techniques. The paper has the potential to impact how the community evaluates WQE metrics going forward. I would give it a 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Two Is Better Than One: Rotations Scale LoRAs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23184v1)
- **Authors**: Hongcan Guo, Guoshun Nan, Yuan Yang, Diyang Zhang, Haotian Li, Zhican Chen, Qinchuan Zhou, Yuhan Ran, Xinye Cao, Sicong Leng, Xiaofeng Tao, Xudong Jiang
- **Abstract**: Scaling Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)-based Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) facilitates large language models (LLMs) to efficiently adapt to diverse tasks. However, traditional gating mechanisms that route inputs to the best experts may fundamentally hinder LLMs' scalability, leading to poor generalization and underfitting issues. We identify that the root cause lies in the restricted expressiveness of existing weighted-sum mechanisms, both within and outside the convex cone of LoRA representations. This motivates us to propose RadarGate, a novel geometrically inspired gating method that introduces rotational operations of LoRAs representations to boost the expressiveness and facilitate richer feature interactions among multiple LoRAs for scalable LLMs. Specifically, we first fuse each LoRA representation to other LoRAs using a learnable component and then feed the output to a rotation matrix. This matrix involves learnable parameters that define the relative angular relationship between LoRA representations. Such a simple yet effective mechanism provides an extra degree of freedom, facilitating the learning of cross-LoRA synergies and properly tracking the challenging poor generalization and underfitting issues as the number of LoRA grows. Extensive experiments on 6 public benchmarks across 21 tasks show the effectiveness of our RadarGate for scaling LoRAs. We also provide valuable insights, revealing that the rotations to each pair of representations are contrastive, encouraging closer alignment of semantically similar representations during geometrical transformation while pushing distance ones further apart. We will release our code to the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Two Is Better Than One: Rotations Scale LoRAs" addresses the challenges of scaling LoRA-based Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) for large language models (LLMs). It identifies that existing gating mechanisms in LoRA-MoE architectures suffer from limited expressiveness, leading to poor generalization and underfitting as the number of LoRA experts increases. To address this, the authors propose RadarGate, a novel geometrically inspired gating method that introduces rotational operations on LoRA representations. RadarGate consists of a RotationGate, which learns complex interactions between LoRAs through angular relationships, and a StretchGate, which assigns weights to each LoRA representation. The paper provides theoretical justifications for RadarGate's effectiveness in mitigating underfitting and improving generalization, and presents experimental results on various benchmarks demonstrating its superior performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of introducing rotational operations to LoRA representations for improving expressiveness is relatively novel. While MoE and LoRA techniques are established, their combination and the specific geometric interpretation and implementation within RadarGate distinguish this work. Prior works predominantly rely on weighted sums, limiting the exploration of the LoRA representations. *However*, while the high-level idea is new, using rotation matrices for feature transformation isn't entirely ground-breaking; it has some roots in areas like computer vision and signal processing. The novelty primarily lies in adapting this concept to LoRA-MoE specifically.

*   **Significance:** Improving the scalability of LoRA-MoE is a significant goal. Parameter-efficient adaptation methods like LoRA are crucial for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments and for handling diverse tasks. By addressing the generalization and underfitting issues associated with scaling LoRAs, the paper contributes to the development of more practical and efficient LLM adaptation techniques. The performance gains demonstrated on a variety of benchmarks further support the importance of this work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing LoRA-MoE methods and identifies the root cause as limited expressiveness of gating mechanisms.
    *   **Strong Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides theoretical explanations (Lemmas and Observations) to support the effectiveness of RadarGate in mitigating underfitting and improving generalization.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents extensive experimental results on multiple benchmarks, comparing RadarGate with a variety of existing methods and demonstrating its superior performance.
    *   **Ablation studies**: Strong ablation studies that show the necessity of both rotation and stretching components to the RadarGate and how the components reinforce each other.
    *   **Case Study**: Good explanation of how RadarGate is able to surpass MoLE by improving LORA representation in a reasoning task.
    *   **Scaling Improvements:** The consistent performance gains and scaling benefits of the proposed RadarGate is well illustrated in the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Components:** While the overall approach is novel, the individual components (rotation matrices, weighted sums) are well-established techniques. The paper could benefit from a more detailed comparison to related techniques that use rotation to transform feature spaces.
    *   **Complexity:** While they claim negligible computational overhead, the math may not match how much the improvement the RotationGate is providing.
    *   **No Full Multi-modal Testing**: Though the results are promising, the approach has not been tested or proven to adapt well in other modalities.

*   **Potential Impact:** The proposed RadarGate has the potential to influence the design of future LoRA-MoE architectures, particularly in scenarios where scalability and generalization are critical. The insights into the limitations of existing gating mechanisms can guide future research efforts in developing more expressive and efficient adaptation methods. Releasing the code will further facilitate adoption and experimentation by the research community.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a useful technique with solid theoretical backing and compelling experimental validation. It addresses an important problem in the field of parameter-efficient LLM adaptation. *However*, the relative novelty is arguably incremental.  Rotation matrices have been used extensively across multiple problems. More thorough testing into more modalities would solidify the paper's position to be an incredible one. Therefore, it's a good paper but not a landmark contribution.
Given this, it's hard to score the paper very highly because the math is somewhat complex and adds to the computational overhead.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### HiGarment: Cross-modal Harmony Based Diffusion Model for Flat Sketch to Realistic Garment Image
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23186v1)
- **Authors**: Junyi Guo, Jingxuan Zhang, Fangyu Wu, Huanda Lu, Qiufeng Wang, Wenmian Yang, Eng Gee Lim, Dongming Lu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion-based garment synthesis tasks primarily focus on the design phase in the fashion domain, while the garment production process remains largely underexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce a new task: Flat Sketch to Realistic Garment Image (FS2RG), which generates realistic garment images by integrating flat sketches and textual guidance. FS2RG presents two key challenges: 1) fabric characteristics are solely guided by textual prompts, providing insufficient visual supervision for diffusion-based models, which limits their ability to capture fine-grained fabric details; 2) flat sketches and textual guidance may provide conflicting information, requiring the model to selectively preserve or modify garment attributes while maintaining structural coherence. To tackle this task, we propose HiGarment, a novel framework that comprises two core components: i) a multi-modal semantic enhancement mechanism that enhances fabric representation across textual and visual modalities, and ii) a harmonized cross-attention mechanism that dynamically balances information from flat sketches and text prompts, allowing controllable synthesis by generating either sketch-aligned (image-biased) or text-guided (text-biased) outputs. Furthermore, we collect Multi-modal Detailed Garment, the largest open-source dataset for garment generation. Experimental results and user studies demonstrate the effectiveness of HiGarment in garment synthesis. The code and dataset will be released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new task called "Flat Sketch to Realistic Garment Image" (FS2RG), which focuses on generating realistic garment images from flat sketches and textual descriptions. This bridges the gap between design and production in the fashion industry.  The authors address challenges related to fabric detail generation and conflicts between sketch and text information by proposing "HiGarment," a framework with two main components: a multi-modal semantic enhancement mechanism (MSE) and a harmonized cross-attention mechanism (HCA).  They also contribute MMDGarment, a new large-scale multi-modal fashion dataset.  The paper presents qualitative and quantitative results, demonstrating the effectiveness of HiGarment in generating realistic garment images.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in introducing the FS2RG task itself, which represents a practical and relevant problem in the fashion industry, moving beyond purely creative design tasks towards addressing production needs. The proposed HiGarment architecture is a solid contribution, particularly the HCA which dynamically balances image and text modalities. The creation of the MMDGarment dataset addresses a significant gap in publicly available, well-annotated fashion datasets.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential impact on streamlining the garment production process. By enabling designers to generate realistic prototypes from flat sketches and text prompts, it could reduce material sourcing iterations and time-to-market. The MMDGarment dataset will also be valuable for future research in garment synthesis and related areas.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly defines a new and practically relevant task.
    *   Proposes a well-designed architecture (HiGarment) with effective components (MSE and HCA).
    *   Contributes a large-scale, multi-modal dataset (MMDGarment).
    *   Presents strong qualitative and quantitative results.
    *   Includes a user study that supports the efficacy of their work

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The improvements in the HiGarment architecture (MSE and HCA components) appear incremental compared to existing attention mechanisms and multi-modal architectures, but they are well-tailored to this particular task.
    *   The paper mentions that further improvements are needed to preserve tiny logos. This indicates limitations in handling fine-grained details, which could impact the practical applicability for some garment designs.
    *   The experimental comparison only shows results with two types of diffusion models, which could limit the generality of the claims.

*   **Score Justification:**

    The paper is a significant contribution in a niche but important area. The introduction of the FS2RG task and the MMDGarment dataset provides a foundation for future research.  While the architectural innovations of HiGarment, by themselves, might not be groundbreaking, they are thoughtfully designed and demonstrate clear improvements for this specific task.  The results presented are compelling.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### TrackVLA: Embodied Visual Tracking in the Wild
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23189v1)
- **Authors**: Shaoan Wang, Jiazhao Zhang, Minghan Li, Jiahang Liu, Anqi Li, Kui Wu, Fangwei Zhong, Junzhi Yu, Zhizheng Zhang, He Wang
- **Abstract**: Embodied visual tracking is a fundamental skill in Embodied AI, enabling an agent to follow a specific target in dynamic environments using only egocentric vision. This task is inherently challenging as it requires both accurate target recognition and effective trajectory planning under conditions of severe occlusion and high scene dynamics. Existing approaches typically address this challenge through a modular separation of recognition and planning. In this work, we propose TrackVLA, a Vision-Language-Action (VLA) model that learns the synergy between object recognition and trajectory planning. Leveraging a shared LLM backbone, we employ a language modeling head for recognition and an anchor-based diffusion model for trajectory planning. To train TrackVLA, we construct an Embodied Visual Tracking Benchmark (EVT-Bench) and collect diverse difficulty levels of recognition samples, resulting in a dataset of 1.7 million samples. Through extensive experiments in both synthetic and real-world environments, TrackVLA demonstrates SOTA performance and strong generalizability. It significantly outperforms existing methods on public benchmarks in a zero-shot manner while remaining robust to high dynamics and occlusion in real-world scenarios at 10 FPS inference speed. Our project page is: https://pku-epic.github.io/TrackVLA-web.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TrackVLA: Embodied Visual Tracking in the Wild":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TrackVLA, a Vision-Language-Action (VLA) model designed for embodied visual tracking (EVT). The key idea is to learn the synergy between object recognition and trajectory planning by leveraging a shared LLM backbone.  TrackVLA employs a language modeling head for recognition and an anchor-based diffusion model for trajectory planning.  To train the model, the authors created a new Embodied Visual Tracking Benchmark (EVT-Bench), a large dataset of 1.7 million samples with varying difficulty levels.  Experimental results, conducted in both synthetic and real-world environments, demonstrate that TrackVLA achieves state-of-the-art performance and strong generalizability, outperforming existing methods, particularly in scenarios with high dynamics and occlusion. The authors claim that TrackVLA can operate at 10 FPS inference speed in real-world settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novelty in Architecture:** The integration of a shared LLM backbone with separate heads for recognition and trajectory planning (language modeling head and an anchor-based diffusion model) represents a novel architectural approach to EVT. Combining these two in an end-to-end way is important because it is likely to reduce the error propogation common in other more modular methods.
    * **Dataset Contribution:** The creation of EVT-Bench is a significant contribution. The dataset's size (1.7 million samples) and diversity of difficulty levels address a gap in available resources for EVT research. The inclusion of detailed language instructions and realistic, dynamic environments makes the benchmark valuable.
    * **Strong Performance:** The paper presents compelling experimental results, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on both the Gym-UnrealCV benchmark (zero-shot) and the newly created EVT-Bench. The real-world evaluations showcase the model's robustness to challenging conditions such as occlusion, dynamic scenes, and cluttered environments. The inference speed (10 FPS) is also a practical advantage.
    * **Sim-to-Real Generalization:** The qualitative results in real-world environments provide further evidence for the model's ability to generalize from simulation to the real world. It is important to demonstrate that these simulations can actually be applied to a real world scenario.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental LLM Use:** While the use of an LLM is a positive direction, the core algorithmic contributions beyond leveraging a pre-trained LLM are somewhat limited. The anchor-based diffusion model might be an interesting contribution but it is unclear to me that this is substantially different than existing diffusion models. The LLM is used as a shared encoder for both tasks, but its not clear whether this is a real improvement because the two tasks may actually be improved with different features.
    * **Limited Scope of Real-World Evaluation:** While the real-world demonstrations are promising, the details about the real-world environment are limited. A more thorough analysis of failure cases and a comparison against a broader range of real-world EVT methods (if available) would strengthen the evaluation.
    * **Evaluation Metrics:** Although standard benchmarks are used, the paper focuses on success rate and length of episode, so other potentially insightful metrics that could give a deeper insight on how the tracking model actually moves.

* **Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its architectural novelty, the introduction of a large-scale EVT benchmark, and the demonstration of strong performance and generalization capabilities. TrackVLA represents a step forward in the field of embodied AI, offering a more robust and efficient approach to visual tracking in dynamic environments. The EVT-Bench dataset has the potential to significantly accelerate research in this area, enabling the development of more sophisticated EVT models.

* **Potential Influence:**
If the code and EVT-Bench dataset are released as promised, this paper has the potential to become a influential. Other researchers can use the dataset to directly evaluate their methods which will enable a better understanding of the methods.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

TrackVLA is a significant contribution to the EVT field, driven by a large-scale dataset and a strong empirical evaluation. The use of a shared LLM backbone with task specific heads does present a novel direction. However, the underlying approach still relies on an incremental application of LLMs and the diffusion modeling contribution seems limited. I rated the paper a 7 instead of a rating of 8 given that, despite the importance of the approach, it still largely leverages an incremental use of LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### ExpeTrans: LLMs Are Experiential Transfer Learners
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23191v1)
- **Authors**: Jinglong Gao, Xiao Ding, Lingxiao Zou, Bibo Cai, Bing Qin, Ting Liu
- **Abstract**: Recent studies provide large language models (LLMs) with textual task-solving experiences via prompts to improve their performance. However, previous methods rely on substantial human labor or time to gather such experiences for each task, which is impractical given the growing variety of task types in user queries to LLMs. To address this issue, we design an autonomous experience transfer framework to explore whether LLMs can mimic human cognitive intelligence to autonomously transfer experience from existing source tasks to newly encountered target tasks. This not only allows the acquisition of experience without extensive costs of previous methods, but also offers a novel path for the generalization of LLMs. Experimental results on 13 datasets demonstrate that our framework effectively improves the performance of LLMs. Furthermore, we provide a detailed analysis of each module in the framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ExpeTrans: LLMs Are Experiential Transfer Learners":

**Summary:**

The paper "ExpeTrans: LLMs Are Experiential Transfer Learners" introduces a framework that enables Large Language Models (LLMs) to autonomously transfer experiential knowledge from existing "source" tasks to new, unseen "target" tasks. The key idea is to mimic human cognitive intelligence by identifying similarities in task function and process between source and target tasks. This approach allows LLMs to acquire experience without the expensive manual effort or computational overhead required by previous methods that generate experience for each new task independently. The framework involves a task-wise experience memory, a module for accumulating source task experiences, and a module for transferring and reasoning with the experience to answer user queries. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their framework on 13 NLP datasets, showing performance improvements over several baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of *autonomously* transferring experiential knowledge between tasks for LLMs is a significant contribution. Prior work focused mainly on either manually crafted experiences or automatically generating them for individual tasks, which doesn't scale well. The concept of mimicking human transfer learning principles by considering task function and process similarity is novel. The framework's design, incorporating experience accumulation, selection, transfer, and reasoning modules, is also innovative.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of current LLM usage: the high cost of acquiring task-specific knowledge. If LLMs can effectively transfer experience, it could significantly improve their generalization capabilities and reduce the need for task-specific data and training. The empirical results on 13 datasets provide strong evidence that the proposed framework achieves this, potentially leading to more efficient and adaptable LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing methods for providing LLMs with task-solving experience.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The proposed framework offers a novel and scalable solution to address the problem.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct extensive experiments on a diverse set of datasets, comparing their method against strong baselines.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:**  The paper includes an ablation study and various analyses (task transferability, source task granularity, effects of different modules, etc.) that provide valuable insights into the framework's behavior.
    *   **Well-Structured and Written:** The paper is easy to follow and understand, with a clear presentation of the problem, proposed solution, experiments, and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework heavily relies on the capabilities of existing LLMs like ChatGPT for various modules (summarization, task selection, experience transfer).  The performance is thus tied to the underlying LLM's abilities and biases.
    *   **Complexity of Modules:** Although designed for autonomous knowledge transfer, the framework involves several modules and hyperparameters, which might require careful tuning for different tasks.
    *   **Scope of Evaluation:** While the experiments cover diverse NLP tasks, the framework's applicability to other domains (e.g., computer vision, robotics) is not explored.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The insights gained from the error analysis in appendix G are valuable but can be further deepened to analyze and improve specific failure modes.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence future research by:
    *   Inspiring new methods for autonomous knowledge transfer in LLMs and other AI systems.
    *   Promoting the development of more generalizable and adaptable LLMs.
    *   Guiding the design of better experience representation and reasoning techniques for LLMs.
    *   Encouraging further investigation into the cognitive principles underlying human transfer learning.
    *   The paper provides a useful set of prompts that could be studied and improved.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:**
The paper makes a strong contribution by addressing an important problem with a novel solution supported by thorough experimentation and analysis. The approach has the potential to significantly improve the efficiency and adaptability of LLMs. While the method depends on base LLMs and involves some complexity, the innovation and empirical validation make it a significant step forward in the field. While it would benefit from a deeper discussion around failure modes, and has only explored classification problems, the overall contribution is significant, and the quality of the paper is very strong.

- **Score**: 8/10

### HyperPointFormer: Multimodal Fusion in 3D Space with Dual-Branch Cross-Attention Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23206v1)
- **Authors**: Aldino Rizaldy, Richard Gloaguen, Fabian Ewald Fassnacht, Pedram Ghamisi
- **Abstract**: Multimodal remote sensing data, including spectral and lidar or photogrammetry, is crucial for achieving satisfactory land-use / land-cover classification results in urban scenes. So far, most studies have been conducted in a 2D context. When 3D information is available in the dataset, it is typically integrated with the 2D data by rasterizing the 3D data into 2D formats. Although this method yields satisfactory classification results, it falls short in fully exploiting the potential of 3D data by restricting the model's ability to learn 3D spatial features directly from raw point clouds. Additionally, it limits the generation of 3D predictions, as the dimensionality of the input data has been reduced. In this study, we propose a fully 3D-based method that fuses all modalities within the 3D point cloud and employs a dedicated dual-branch Transformer model to simultaneously learn geometric and spectral features. To enhance the fusion process, we introduce a cross-attention-based mechanism that fully operates on 3D points, effectively integrating features from various modalities across multiple scales. The purpose of cross-attention is to allow one modality to assess the importance of another by weighing the relevant features. We evaluated our method by comparing it against both 3D and 2D methods using the 2018 IEEE GRSS Data Fusion Contest (DFC2018) dataset. Our findings indicate that 3D fusion delivers competitive results compared to 2D methods and offers more flexibility by providing 3D predictions. These predictions can be projected onto 2D maps, a capability that is not feasible in reverse. Additionally, we evaluated our method on different datasets, specifically the ISPRS Vaihingen 3D and the IEEE 2019 Data Fusion Contest. Our code will be published here: https://github.com/aldinorizaldy/hyperpointformer.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "HyperPointFormer: Multimodal Fusion in 3D Space with Dual-Branch Cross-Attention Transformers" presents a novel deep learning architecture for fusing multimodal remote sensing data, specifically lidar point clouds and hyperspectral imagery, for 3D semantic segmentation. The key innovation is the HyperPointFormer network, a dual-branch Transformer model that processes geometric (lidar) and spectral (hyperspectral) data separately before fusing them using a proposed CrossPointAttention (CPA) mechanism. CPA allows features from each modality to attend to and inform the other at various scales, enabling richer feature interactions. The authors evaluate their method on the IEEE GRSS Data Fusion Contest (DFC2018) dataset, as well as the ISPRS Vaihingen 3D semantic segmentation dataset, and the 2019 Data Fusion Contest. They demonstrate competitive performance compared to existing 2D and 3D fusion approaches and highlight the advantages of generating 3D predictions which can then be projected to 2D, as opposed to the reverse. The authors also perform ablation studies and provide visualizations to interpret the contributions of different architectural components.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and timely problem: the fusion of multimodal remote sensing data in a 3D context. While much of the existing work focuses on 2D fusion, the paper argues convincingly for the benefits of directly processing 3D point clouds and generating 3D predictions.

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the specific architecture of the HyperPointFormer. The dual-branch approach is not entirely new, but the combination with the CrossPointAttention (CPA) mechanism, specifically designed for point cloud data and multi-scale fusion, is a significant contribution. The paper also innovates by adapting Cross-ViT for point cloud processing and for a segmentation task, which is a non-trivial adaptation. This is justified well through the paper’s selection of VSA for the self-attention, and also the incorporation of ideas from the edge-graph operator for point relation encoding.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from several aspects. First, by demonstrating competitive performance with 2D methods, the authors validate the 3D fusion approach as a viable alternative. Second, the ability to generate 3D predictions opens up possibilities for more sophisticated applications and spatial analysis. Third, the ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of specific design choices, which can inform future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem statement and motivation:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of 2D fusion approaches and motivates the need for a 3D alternative.
    *   **Well-defined architecture:** The HyperPointFormer is described in detail, including the rationale behind each design choice.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The paper includes thorough experiments on multiple datasets, comparing the proposed method to both 2D and 3D baselines.
    *   **Insightful ablation studies:**  The ablation studies provide evidence for the effectiveness of the CPA mechanism and other architectural components.
    *   **Effective visualizations and interpretation:** The visualizations help to understand the behavior of the model and the benefits of different fusion strategies.
    * The paper provides an excellent discussion with the description of the limitations and the challenges on section V part D.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper demonstrates competitive results on the DFC2018 dataset, and two other datasets, the improvement over certain 3D baselines is not always substantial. The gains in mean IoU on Vaihingen3D and the DFC2019 datasets are marginal (0.4% and 2.9%, respectively). More aggressive experimentation and comparisons against already high-performing baseline architectures such as those based on transformers will benefit the paper.
    *   The paper could benefit from further exploration of geometric mismatch correction and addressing data imbalance, as also stated as a limitation.
    * Some parts of the paper have issues with clarity/flow of arguments. E.g., Table III discussion jumps between different arguments.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper is likely to influence future research on multimodal remote sensing data fusion.
    *   The HyperPointFormer architecture, particularly the CPA mechanism, could be adopted and adapted by other researchers in the field.
    *   The insights from the ablation studies could guide the design of new fusion strategies and deep learning models.
    * The paper opens up new avenues for incorporating and projecting labeled data.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-evaluated 3D fusion approach that addresses a relevant problem in remote sensing. The HyperPointFormer architecture and CPA mechanism represent a significant contribution, and the comprehensive experiments provide valuable insights. While the performance gains over some baselines are not always substantial, the 3D predictions and flexibility of projections, makes the paper an interesting work. The paper shows the great potential of VSA. The presented limitations offer valuable insight that will guide for future works. The aforementioned weaknesses and limitations lower the score from being a higher one.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Daunce: Data Attribution through Uncertainty Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23223v1)
- **Authors**: Xingyuan Pan, Chenlu Ye, Joseph Melkonian, Jiaqi W. Ma, Tong Zhang
- **Abstract**: Training data attribution (TDA) methods aim to identify which training examples influence a model's predictions on specific test data most. By quantifying these influences, TDA supports critical applications such as data debugging, curation, and valuation. Gradient-based TDA methods rely on gradients and second-order information, limiting their applicability at scale. While recent random projection-based methods improve scalability, they often suffer from degraded attribution accuracy. Motivated by connections between uncertainty and influence functions, we introduce Daunce - a simple yet effective data attribution approach through uncertainty estimation. Our method operates by fine-tuning a collection of perturbed models and computing the covariance of per-example losses across these models as the attribution score. Daunce is scalable to large language models (LLMs) and achieves more accurate attribution compared to existing TDA methods. We validate Daunce on tasks ranging from vision tasks to LLM fine-tuning, and further demonstrate its compatibility with black-box model access. Applied to OpenAI's GPT models, our method achieves, to our knowledge, the first instance of data attribution on proprietary LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DAUNCE, a novel and scalable training data attribution (TDA) method. DAUNCE estimates data influence by fine-tuning perturbed models and computing the covariance of per-example losses.  The core idea leverages the connection between uncertainty estimation and influence functions. Key benefits highlighted are scalability to large language models (LLMs), improved attribution accuracy compared to existing TDA methods, and compatibility with black-box model access.
