**Justification:** The paper presents a plausible but preliminary exploration of a relevant application of LLMs. While it highlights the challenges and suggests a reasonable path forward, the limited empirical evidence, lack of concrete solutions for integrating LLMs with formal tools, and limited novelty prevent it from receiving a higher score. A score of 5 reflects that the paper contains worthwhile ideas but requires significantly more development and validation to demonstrate its true potential.

- **Classification**: cs.LO
- **Score**: 5/10

### TRACE: Trajectory-Constrained Concept Erasure in Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23312v1)
- **Authors**: Finn Carter
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image diffusion models have shown unprecedented generative capability, but their ability to produce undesirable concepts (e.g.~pornographic content, sensitive identities, copyrighted styles) poses serious concerns for privacy, fairness, and safety. {Concept erasure} aims to remove or suppress specific concept information in a generative model. In this paper, we introduce \textbf{TRACE (Trajectory-Constrained Attentional Concept Erasure)}, a novel method to erase targeted concepts from diffusion models while preserving overall generative quality. Our approach combines a rigorous theoretical framework, establishing formal conditions under which a concept can be provably suppressed in the diffusion process, with an effective fine-tuning procedure compatible with both conventional latent diffusion (Stable Diffusion) and emerging rectified flow models (e.g.~FLUX). We first derive a closed-form update to the model's cross-attention layers that removes hidden representations of the target concept. We then introduce a trajectory-aware finetuning objective that steers the denoising process away from the concept only in the late sampling stages, thus maintaining the model's fidelity on unrelated content. Empirically, we evaluate TRACE on multiple benchmarks used in prior concept erasure studies (object classes, celebrity faces, artistic styles, and explicit content from the I2P dataset). TRACE achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming recent methods such as ANT, EraseAnything, and MACE in terms of removal efficacy and output quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TRACE: Trajectory-Constrained Concept Erasure in Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRACE, a novel method for erasing specific concepts from text-to-image diffusion models.  It addresses the challenge of removing undesirable concepts (e.g., pornography, celebrity faces) while preserving the overall generative quality of the model. TRACE combines a theoretical framework establishing formal conditions for concept suppression in diffusion processes with an effective fine-tuning procedure. The method involves a closed-form update to cross-attention layers to remove hidden representations of the target concept and a trajectory-aware fine-tuning objective that steers the denoising process away from the concept only in the later sampling stages.  The approach is demonstrated to work effectively on both latent diffusion models (Stable Diffusion) and rectified flow models (FLUX), outperforming existing methods in terms of removal efficacy and output quality across several benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper offers a rigorous theoretical analysis of concept erasure in diffusion models, which is a significant contribution.  The formal criteria for complete concept removal and the analysis of how interventions at different diffusion stages impact other content provide valuable insights. The theorems provide a clear, mathematically grounded justification for the method.
*   **Novel Method:** The proposed TRACE method is innovative. The combination of closed-form cross-attention editing with a trajectory-aware loss is a novel approach that addresses the limitations of existing techniques.  The modular design (separate LoRA modules for each concept with an integration loss) is also a strength, facilitating multi-concept erasure at scale.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results on a variety of benchmarks (object erasure, celebrity faces, artistic styles, and explicit content). The comprehensive evaluation demonstrates TRACE's superior performance compared to state-of-the-art methods, especially in terms of balancing removal efficacy and output quality (FID, CLIP score).  The inclusion of the FLUX model is also important, as it shows TRACE's generalizability.
*   **Clarity and Completeness:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the method, its theoretical underpinnings, and the experimental setup. The ablation studies provide evidence supporting the importance of each component of TRACE.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** While LoRA and modularity reduce computational cost compared to full retraining, the approach requires fine-tuning. The time for fine-tuning can be substantial, especially if multi concepts needs to be removed. The paper mentions 1000-2000 steps are enough for one concepts to be removed. This need to be run for different models. While the time for each can be short, the overall computation is extensive.
*   **Reliance on Existing Classifiers/Detectors:** The evaluation relies on the accuracy of existing classifiers and detectors (e.g., NudeNet, ResNet-50 for object classification, face recognition models). The performance of TRACE is therefore indirectly dependent on the quality of these tools. If the base models are not accurate, the data generation is questionable.
*   **Complexity:** TRACE involves several components: closed-form updates, trajectory constraints, LoRA modules, and an integration loss. Although each component is justified, the overall complexity might make it challenging to implement and adapt for different models.
*   **Potential for Jailbreaking:** The paper acknowledges the possibility of jailbreaking, but the analysis of adversarial attacks and proxy concepts is limited. It would be valuable to investigate the robustness of TRACE against more sophisticated adversarial strategies.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of generative AI safety.  It introduces a principled and effective method for concept erasure that can help mitigate the risks associated with generating harmful or undesirable content.  TRACE's ability to work on both latent diffusion and rectified flow models, as well as its modular design, enhances its practical applicability. While the possibility of jailbreaking remains a concern, TRACE represents a substantial step towards more responsible and controlled generation.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

TRACE is a strong paper due to its solid theoretical foundation, innovative methodology, and extensive empirical validation. While there are weaknesses related to the reliance on external classifiers, computational cost, and potential for jailbreaking, the strengths outweigh these limitations. The theoretical analysis and integration with a practical fine-tuning strategy distinguish it from other works. This is why it is more than a good paper. The practical aspects and potential impact on safety and responsible AI generation (especially with the current interest) makes it a very solid paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Proximalized Preference Optimization for Diverse Feedback Types: A Decomposed Perspective on DPO
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23316v1)
- **Authors**: Kaiyang Guo, Yinchuan Li, Zhitang Chen
- **Abstract**: Direct alignment methods typically optimize large language models (LLMs) by contrasting the likelihoods of preferred versus dispreferred responses. While effective in steering LLMs to match relative preference, these methods are frequently noted for decreasing the absolute likelihoods of example responses. As a result, aligned models tend to generate outputs that deviate from the expected patterns, exhibiting reward-hacking effect even without a reward model. This undesired consequence exposes a fundamental limitation in contrastive alignment, which we characterize as likelihood underdetermination. In this work, we revisit direct preference optimization (DPO) -- the seminal direct alignment method -- and demonstrate that its loss theoretically admits a decomposed reformulation. The reformulated loss not only broadens applicability to a wider range of feedback types, but also provides novel insights into the underlying cause of likelihood underdetermination. Specifically, the standard DPO implementation implicitly oversimplifies a regularizer in the reformulated loss, and reinstating its complete version effectively resolves the underdetermination issue. Leveraging these findings, we introduce PRoximalized PReference Optimization (PRO), a unified method to align with diverse feeback types, eliminating likelihood underdetermination through an efficient approximation of the complete regularizer. Comprehensive experiments show the superiority of PRO over existing methods in scenarios involving pairwise, binary and scalar feedback.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Proximalized Preference Optimization for Diverse Feedback Types: A Decomposed Perspective on DPO":

**Summary:**

The paper tackles the issue of likelihood underdetermination in Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), a common method for aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human preferences. The authors show that the standard DPO loss can be decomposed into separate optimizer and regularizer terms. They argue that standard DPO implementations implicitly oversimplify the regularizer, leading to a decrease in the absolute likelihoods of both preferred and dispreferred responses, which, in turn, induces reward hacking. To address this, they propose Proximalized Preference Optimization (PRO), a method that uses an efficient approximation of the complete regularizer through a hyper-response mechanism. PRO is designed to work with diverse feedback types (pairwise, binary, and scalar) and mitigate likelihood underdetermination. Experiments demonstrate that PRO performs comparably or better than existing methods, particularly in challenging scenarios with imbalanced binary feedback.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the decomposition of the DPO loss function and the identification of the simplified regularizer as the root cause of likelihood underdetermination. While other works have observed this issue and attempted to remedy it through other means (e.g., adding supervised signals), this paper offers a more fundamental analysis and a principled solution through a better regularizer. The hyper-response mechanism for efficient regularizer approximation is also a practical contribution. PROs ability to apply to different types of feedback is also useful.

*   **Significance:** Likelihood underdetermination is a known problem in alignment, leading to undesirable model behaviors. Addressing it effectively is significant for building reliable and trustworthy LLMs. PRO offers a promising approach to mitigating this issue, supported by theoretical arguments and empirical results. The ability of PRO to work with diverse feedback types makes it useful for aligning models when all you can do is provide binary feedback.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides a clear and well-argued theoretical analysis of the problem and the proposed solution.
    *   **Principled Approach:** PRO addresses the issue at its root by reinstating a complete regularizer instead of adding ad-hoc fixes.
    *   **Unified Framework:** PRO offers a unified approach for aligning LLMs with diverse feedback types.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  Experiments demonstrate PRO's effectiveness in mitigating likelihood underdetermination and achieving good performance across various tasks and feedback types.
    *   **Good Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains complex concepts.
    *   The connection to Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) offers interesting insight for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** While the theoretical analysis is strong, it might be difficult for practitioners to fully grasp the nuances of the decomposed loss function and the hyper-response mechanism. While there are claims to computational costs, they should be validated empirically.
    *   **Limited Explanation of the Alpha tuning:** While there is explanation in the appendices, the selection of the optimal `a` (tradeoff between optimization and regularization) values seems to require task-specific tuning. A better understanding of the relationship could benefit the user.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on LLM alignment by providing a deeper understanding of the limitations of contrastive alignment methods and a more principled approach to addressing them. PRO could become a standard method for aligning LLMs, especially in scenarios with limited data and diverse feedback types.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper makes a solid theoretical contribution, providing interesting insight. The performance across all feedback types is very important as well. While the paper does involve a new hyperparameter that requires tuning, as well as some complexity in implementing it, it's very impactful on its theoretical analysis and practicality.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### CF-DETR: Coarse-to-Fine Transformer for Real-Time Object Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23317v1)
- **Authors**: Woojin Shin, Donghwa Kang, Byeongyun Park, Brent Byunghoon Kang, Jinkyu Lee, Hyeongboo Baek
- **Abstract**: Detection Transformers (DETR) are increasingly adopted in autonomous vehicle (AV) perception systems due to their superior accuracy over convolutional networks. However, concurrently executing multiple DETR tasks presents significant challenges in meeting firm real-time deadlines (R1) and high accuracy requirements (R2), particularly for safety-critical objects, while navigating the inherent latency-accuracy trade-off under resource constraints. Existing real-time DNN scheduling approaches often treat models generically, failing to leverage Transformer-specific properties for efficient resource allocation. To address these challenges, we propose CF-DETR, an integrated system featuring a novel coarse-to-fine Transformer architecture and a dedicated real-time scheduling framework NPFP**. CF-DETR employs three key strategies (A1: coarse-to-fine inference, A2: selective fine inference, A3: multi-level batch inference) that exploit Transformer properties to dynamically adjust patch granularity and attention scope based on object criticality, aiming to satisfy R2. The NPFP** scheduling framework (A4) orchestrates these adaptive mechanisms A1-A3. It partitions each DETR task into a safety-critical coarse subtask for guaranteed critical object detection within its deadline (ensuring R1), and an optional fine subtask for enhanced overall accuracy (R2), while managing individual and batched execution. Our extensive evaluations on server, GPU-enabled embedded platforms, and actual AV platforms demonstrate that CF-DETR, under an NPFP** policy, successfully meets strict timing guarantees for critical operations and achieves significantly higher overall and critical object detection accuracy compared to existing baselines across diverse AV workloads.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "CF-DETR: Coarse-to-Fine Transformer for Real-Time Object Detection" proposes a novel architecture and scheduling framework to address the latency-accuracy trade-off in Detection Transformers (DETRs) for real-time object detection in autonomous vehicles (AVs).  The CF-DETR architecture uses a coarse-to-fine approach, adjusting patch granularity and attention scope based on object criticality. A Non-Preemptive Fixed-Priority (NPFP**) scheduling framework orchestrates the system by partitioning each DETR task into safety-critical coarse subtasks and optional fine subtasks. This framework aims to guarantee real-time deadlines for critical object detection while maximizing overall accuracy. The paper presents evaluations on server, embedded GPU, and AV platforms, demonstrating improved accuracy and real-time performance compared to existing baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates good novelty by combining a Transformer-specific coarse-to-fine architecture with a dedicated real-time scheduling framework. The specific strategies (A1-A3) to exploit transformer properties (patch granularity, attention) for adaptive resource allocation based on object criticality is novel. The development of the NPFP** scheduler tailored to the coarse-to-fine DETR is also a novel element.  The model-aware approach, contrasting with generic DNN schedulers, is a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in addressing a critical challenge for deploying DETR models in safety-critical AV applications: meeting both real-time deadlines and high accuracy requirements. The proposed solution offers improvements over existing methods. The experimental validation on different hardware platforms, including an AV prototype, bolsters the paper's credibility and practical relevance. However, the performance gain might be incremental in realistic self-driving scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly identifies a key problem in the deployment of DETR models for AV perception.
    *   The proposed CF-DETR architecture and NPFP** scheduler are well-motivated and designed.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough and includes diverse hardware platforms.
    *   The writing is clear and well-structured.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the individual components (coarse-to-fine, multi-level batching, prioritized scheduling) are not entirely new in the broader DNN or embedded systems context, the *specific integration* within a DETR architecture and custom real-time scheduler contributes to the novel aspect.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more thorough ablation study to demonstrate the contributions of each component (A1-A4) more explicitly.  While the results show overall system improvement, quantifying each component's independent contribution would be valuable.
    *   The WCET analysis methodology needs to be described with more details. The guarantees largely depend on the tightness of these bounds. How the analysis addresses the non-deterministic nature of GPU execution needs further clarification.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

I assign a score of 7.5 to this paper.  The paper builds upon existing concepts but innovatively combines them within the DETR architecture and real-time scheduling context. While coarse-to-fine strategies and adaptive batching exist in the literature, tailoring them *specifically* for Transformer-based object detection with guaranteed real-time constraints is a valuable contribution. The experimental results demonstrate practical improvements.  However, the incremental nature of the improvements compared to existing RT-DNN systems,  and the relatively weak discussion on WCET, prevents a higher score. Also, I would rate the contributions as highly specialized.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: eess.SY
- **Score**: 7/10

### Dimension-Reduction Attack! Video Generative Models are Experts on Controllable Image Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23325v1)
- **Authors**: Hengyuan Cao, Yutong Feng, Biao Gong, Yijing Tian, Yunhong Lu, Chuang Liu, Bin Wang
- **Abstract**: Video generative models can be regarded as world simulators due to their ability to capture dynamic, continuous changes inherent in real-world environments. These models integrate high-dimensional information across visual, temporal, spatial, and causal dimensions, enabling predictions of subjects in various status. A natural and valuable research direction is to explore whether a fully trained video generative model in high-dimensional space can effectively support lower-dimensional tasks such as controllable image generation. In this work, we propose a paradigm for video-to-image knowledge compression and task adaptation, termed \textit{Dimension-Reduction Attack} (\texttt{DRA-Ctrl}), which utilizes the strengths of video models, including long-range context modeling and flatten full-attention, to perform various generation tasks. Specially, to address the challenging gap between continuous video frames and discrete image generation, we introduce a mixup-based transition strategy that ensures smooth adaptation. Moreover, we redesign the attention structure with a tailored masking mechanism to better align text prompts with image-level control. Experiments across diverse image generation tasks, such as subject-driven and spatially conditioned generation, show that repurposed video models outperform those trained directly on images. These results highlight the untapped potential of large-scale video generators for broader visual applications. \texttt{DRA-Ctrl} provides new insights into reusing resource-intensive video models and lays foundation for future unified generative models across visual modalities. The project page is https://dra-ctrl-2025.github.io/DRA-Ctrl/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper "Dimension-Reduction Attack! Video Generative Models are Experts on Controllable Image Synthesis":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel framework called Dimension-Reduction Attack (DRA-Ctrl) that repurposes video generative models for controllable image generation tasks. The core idea is to leverage the high-dimensional knowledge learned by video models, encompassing temporal dependencies and long-range context, and apply it to lower-dimensional image generation tasks. DRA-Ctrl addresses the challenge of adapting video models, which are trained on continuous frame sequences, to the discrete nature of image generation by introducing a mixup-based transition strategy. This strategy constructs a smooth transition between condition and target images. Additionally, the paper proposes Frame-Skip Position Embedding to reduce computational cost, and an attention masking mechanism to better align text prompts with image-level control. Experimental results demonstrate that DRA-Ctrl effectively repurposes video models for diverse image generation tasks, outperforming image-specific models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using video generative models for controllable image generation is interesting, though not entirely unprecedented. Prior works have explored video models for image editing. However, the approach of adapting them via a mixup strategy to bridge the gap between continuous video and discrete image transitions, Frame-Skip Position Embedding for efficient transfer, and attention masking is novel. The framework and its components are well-designed and build on existing knowledge effectively.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential to unlock the untapped potential of large-scale video generators for a wider range of visual applications.  If successfully adopted and extended, this paradigm could lead to a new generation of controllable image generation techniques that incorporate richer contextual information and generate more realistic and coherent results. The results showcasing repurposed video models outperforming specialized image models is a strong demonstration of the potential benefit of the approach. It lays the foundation for future research in unifying generative models across visual modalities.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The core idea is innovative.
    *   The mixup-based transition strategy addresses a critical problem in adapting video models.
    *   The paper provides strong experimental results demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the technical details.
    *   Ablation studies are conducted to validate the effectiveness of the individual components of DRA-Ctrl.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper acknowledges that the method can result in slightly inferior performance compared to models optimized for image generation when considering image quality metrics, which demonstrates some trade-offs need to be considered.
    *   While the paper touches on societal impact and safety, more in-depth consideration is needed regarding potential misuse and bias associated with controllable image generation technology.
    *   The computational efficiency gain of Frame-Skip Position Embedding should be further quantified.
    *   The method might be sensitive to the choice of hyperparameters involved in the mixup-based transition.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This work can be a catalyst for further research into repurposing large-scale video models for various visual tasks. It could inspire the development of more unified generative models capable of handling diverse visual modalities. The reported “Dimension-Reduction Attack” effect provides a compelling perspective on how high-dimensional priors can enhance control in lower-dimensional tasks.

**Overall Justification:**

This paper presents a significant and innovative approach to controllable image generation by repurposing video generative models. The proposed DRA-Ctrl framework addresses the key challenges in adapting video models to discrete image generation tasks, achieves impressive results, and opens promising avenues for future research. While the approach has some limitations, its potential impact on the field justifies a strong score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Diffusion Sampling Path Tells More: An Efficient Plug-and-Play Strategy for Sample Filtering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23343v1)
- **Authors**: Sixian Wang, Zhiwei Tang, Tsung-Hui Chang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models often exhibit inconsistent sample quality due to stochastic variations inherent in their sampling trajectories. Although training-based fine-tuning (e.g. DDPO [1]) and inference-time alignment techniques[2] aim to improve sample fidelity, they typically necessitate full denoising processes and external reward signals. This incurs substantial computational costs, hindering their broader applicability. In this work, we unveil an intriguing phenomenon: a previously unobserved yet exploitable link between sample quality and characteristics of the denoising trajectory during classifier-free guidance (CFG). Specifically, we identify a strong correlation between high-density regions of the sample distribution and the Accumulated Score Differences (ASD)--the cumulative divergence between conditional and unconditional scores. Leveraging this insight, we introduce CFG-Rejection, an efficient, plug-and-play strategy that filters low-quality samples at an early stage of the denoising process, crucially without requiring external reward signals or model retraining. Importantly, our approach necessitates no modifications to model architectures or sampling schedules and maintains full compatibility with existing diffusion frameworks. We validate the effectiveness of CFG-Rejection in image generation through extensive experiments, demonstrating marked improvements on human preference scores (HPSv2, PickScore) and challenging benchmarks (GenEval, DPG-Bench). We anticipate that CFG-Rejection will offer significant advantages for diverse generative modalities beyond images, paving the way for more efficient and reliable high-quality sample generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CFG-Rejection, a novel and efficient plug-and-play strategy for filtering low-quality samples generated by diffusion models.  The core idea is based on an observed strong correlation between sample quality and the "Accumulated Score Differences" (ASD) during the denoising process under classifier-free guidance (CFG).  ASD measures the cumulative divergence between conditional and unconditional score predictions. By monitoring ASD in the initial denoising steps, CFG-Rejection can prematurely terminate low-quality trajectories, reducing computational cost without requiring external reward signals, model retraining, or architectural modifications. The paper validates CFG-Rejection through extensive experiments on image generation tasks, demonstrating improved human preference scores and performance on challenging benchmarks.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in identifying and exploiting the previously unobserved correlation between ASD and sample quality in diffusion models. While inference-time filtering and alignment are not entirely new concepts, leveraging intrinsic signals within the CFG mechanism to achieve this is a significant contribution. The ASD metric itself, while conceptually simple (cumulative sum of score differences), proves surprisingly effective. The "plug-and-play" nature of the method, requiring no architectural changes or retraining, further adds to its practical value. However, cumulative score differences or score dynamics have been looked at in other contexts (e.g. measuring overconfidence), reducing the novelty slightly.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the efficiency and reliability of diffusion model-based generative applications. By filtering out low-quality samples early in the process, CFG-Rejection can save considerable computational resources and reduce the reliance on trial-and-error workflows.  The "free lunch" aspect, achieved by leveraging existing mechanisms without additional training, makes it widely applicable. The improvements in human preference scores and benchmark performance, though incremental, are solid evidence of the method's effectiveness. The potential to generalize to other generative modalities beyond images further enhances its impact. However, the paper does not offer a theoretical grounding for the observed correlation beyond the empirical evidence. The results depend on the effectiveness of CFG, making the utility dependent on that aspect.

* **Strengths:**
    *  Well-defined and motivated problem.
    *  Clear and concise presentation of the method.
    *  Extensive experimental validation across different models and datasets.
    *  Practical and easily implementable solution.
    *  No reliance on external reward models or model retraining.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *  Lack of a strong theoretical justification for the ASD-sample quality correlation.  The paper describes the *what* but not the *why*.
    *  Limited performance improvements on some datasets (e.g., SDXL), suggesting that the method may not be universally effective or that better parameters could be explored. The gains, while statistically significant, may be of limited practical value.
    *  Dependence on the quality of CFG. If CFG is not working well, ASD cannot capture useful information.
    * The approach does not address the quality of the CFG guidance itself, i.e., is the prompt effective?
    * The experiments are limited to image generation. Generalization to other modalities is claimed but not directly demonstrated.

* **Potential Influence:** CFG-Rejection has the potential to become a standard technique in diffusion model-based generative pipelines, particularly in resource-constrained settings or where immediate feedback is needed. Its simplicity and ease of integration make it attractive to practitioners. The work could also inspire further research into understanding and exploiting intrinsic signals within generative models for quality control and efficiency improvements.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**  While the method is relatively simple and lacks a solid theoretical basis, its practical effectiveness, ease of implementation, and potential for widespread adoption justify a score of 7. The paper makes a clear and valuable contribution to improving the efficiency and reliability of diffusion models, but the limitations in theoretical understanding and performance improvements prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The novelty is good, but not ground-breaking as cumulative scores have been looked at in other contexts for other aims. The relatively limited scope of the experimental validation also prevents a higher score. However, the potential impact on the diffusion model community is significant.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Reward Fairness in RLHF: From a Resource Allocation Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23349v1)
- **Authors**: Sheng Ouyang, Yulan Hu, Ge Chen, Qingyang Li, Fuzheng Zhang, Yong Liu
- **Abstract**: Rewards serve as proxies for human preferences and play a crucial role in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). However, if these rewards are inherently imperfect, exhibiting various biases, they can adversely affect the alignment of large language models (LLMs). In this paper, we collectively define the various biases present in rewards as the problem of reward unfairness. We propose a bias-agnostic method to address the issue of reward fairness from a resource allocation perspective, without specifically designing for each type of bias, yet effectively mitigating them. Specifically, we model preference learning as a resource allocation problem, treating rewards as resources to be allocated while considering the trade-off between utility and fairness in their distribution. We propose two methods, Fairness Regularization and Fairness Coefficient, to achieve fairness in rewards. We apply our methods in both verification and reinforcement learning scenarios to obtain a fairness reward model and a policy model, respectively. Experiments conducted in these scenarios demonstrate that our approach aligns LLMs with human preferences in a more fair manner.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of "reward unfairness" in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF).  It argues that biases in reward models, such as length bias or category bias, stem from a more fundamental issue of unfair resource allocation. The paper proposes a unified framework that models preference learning as a resource allocation problem, where rewards are resources to be allocated.  It then introduces two methods, Fairness Regularization and Fairness Coefficient, to achieve fairness in reward distribution while considering the trade-off between utility (alignment with human preference) and fairness. The authors apply their methods in verification and reinforcement learning scenarios and demonstrate improved fairness and performance compared to baseline methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The central idea of framing reward bias as a resource allocation problem is a fresh perspective.  While previous work has targeted specific biases like length bias, this paper offers a more general, bias-agnostic approach.  The introduction of Fairness Regularization and Fairness Coefficient, while not fundamentally new algorithmic techniques, are presented in a novel context within the RLHF pipeline. The use of existing fairness metrics from resource allocation literature within the reward modeling framework also contributes to its novelty.
* **Significance:** Reward bias is a recognized and important challenge in RLHF.  If left unaddressed, it can lead to unintended model behavior and misaligned AI. The paper tackles a core problem that impacts the reliability and trustworthiness of language models trained with RLHF. The experimental results, showing improvements in both verification and RL scenarios, provides empirical evidence of the practical benefits of the proposed framework. Moreover, the authors demonstrated improved fairness across different data types (helpful and harmless, short vs. long sentences) indicating it is a more robust solution.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Unified Perspective:** Provides a coherent framework for understanding various reward biases as instances of a broader problem of reward unfairness.
    * **Bias-Agnostic Approach:** The method doesn't require explicitly designing solutions for each type of bias, making it more adaptable.
    * **Empirical Validation:**  Demonstrates effectiveness in both reward model verification and policy learning through experiments on standard benchmarks.
    * **Strong Theoretical Justification:** The resource allocation formulation with fairness functions provides a solid theoretical foundation.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Bias Validation:** Focuses mainly on length and category bias, with a limited experiment on social bias. A more comprehensive analysis across diverse types of bias would strengthen the paper.
    * **Incremental Algorithmic Contribution:**  The specific methods (Fairness Regularization and Fairness Coefficient) are essentially applying existing techniques in a new context. The core advance is in the framing and problem definition, not in developing entirely new optimization algorithms.
    * **Complexity of Implementation:** Tuning the hyperparameters α and γ, which control the fairness contribution, requires careful experimentation and might add complexity to the training process. The authors state "Considering the trade-off between fairness and utility, we typically set a = 0.1 in practical experiments" but more details on how one finds the trade-off would be better.
* **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence future research on mitigating reward bias in RLHF.  The resource allocation perspective can inspire new fairness-aware reward modeling techniques.  It may also prompt further investigation into the relationship between different types of reward biases and their impact on model behavior.
* **Concerns:** The paper's limitation on testing social bias could be improved to make a strong claim for the unified perspective.
**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a significant advance by offering a novel, unified perspective on reward bias in RLHF.  The resource allocation framework provides a useful abstraction for understanding and addressing the problem. The empirical results demonstrate the potential of the proposed methods. However, the incremental nature of the algorithmic contribution and the limited scope of bias validation prevent it from achieving a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### VideoReasonBench: Can MLLMs Perform Vision-Centric Complex Video Reasoning?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23359v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanxin Liu, Kun Ouyang, Haoning Wu, Yi Liu, Lin Sui, Xinhao Li, Yan Zhong, Y. Charles, Xinyu Zhou, Xu Sun
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have shown that long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can significantly enhance the performance of large language models (LLMs) on complex tasks. However, this benefit is yet to be demonstrated in the domain of video understanding, since most existing benchmarks lack the reasoning depth required to demonstrate the advantages of extended CoT chains. While recent efforts have proposed benchmarks aimed at video reasoning, the tasks are often knowledge-driven and do not rely heavily on visual content. To bridge this gap, we introduce VideoReasonBench, a benchmark designed to evaluate vision-centric, complex video reasoning. To ensure visual richness and high reasoning complexity, each video in VideoReasonBench depicts a sequence of fine-grained operations on a latent state that is only visible in part of the video. The questions evaluate three escalating levels of video reasoning skills: recalling observed visual information, inferring the content of latent states, and predicting information beyond the video. Under such task setting, models have to precisely recall multiple operations in the video, and perform step-by-step reasoning to get correct final answers for these questions. Using VideoReasonBench, we comprehensively evaluate 18 state-of-the-art multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), finding that most perform poorly on complex video reasoning, e.g., GPT-4o achieves only 6.9% accuracy, while the thinking-enhanced Gemini-2.5-Pro significantly outperforms others with 56.0% accuracy. Our investigations on "test-time scaling" further reveal that extended thinking budget, while offering none or minimal benefits on existing video benchmarks, is essential for improving the performance on VideoReasonBench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VIDEOREASONBENCH, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the vision-centric complex video reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Unlike existing video benchmarks, VIDEOREASONBENCH focuses on scenarios requiring models to precisely recall visual information, infer hidden states, and predict future outcomes based on a sequence of fine-grained operations. The benchmark features six video categories with varying scenes and reasoning skills across three levels of complexity. The authors conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 18 state-of-the-art MLLMs and find that many perform poorly, highlighting their limitations in complex video reasoning. They show that augmenting models with a 'thinking' mode, as exemplified by Gemini-2.5-Pro, significantly improves performance.  The paper further demonstrates that VIDEOREASONBENCH is more reliant on visual information compared to existing benchmarks and benefits from extended chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the design of the VIDEOREASONBENCH benchmark itself.  Existing video reasoning benchmarks often focus on knowledge-driven reasoning or lack the complexity needed to fully demonstrate the benefits of extended CoT. VIDEOREASONBENCH addresses this gap by creating scenarios where models must track sequential operations on partially observable states, requiring fine-grained visual perception and step-by-step inference. This vision-centric approach distinguishes it from benchmarks that lean heavily on external knowledge.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates that current state-of-the-art MLLMs struggle with complex video reasoning tasks specifically designed to stress visual understanding and sequential inference. This finding is significant because it highlights the limitations of current models in a critical area of video understanding. The benchmark provides a tool for researchers to develop and evaluate models with improved vision-centric reasoning abilities. The paper's analysis of the effectiveness of thinking-enhanced models and the reliance on visual information offers insights into how to improve performance on these types of tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined task:** The paper provides a clear and systematic definition of vision-centric complex video reasoning, which contributes to a more rigorous understanding of the field.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The evaluation of 18 state-of-the-art MLLMs provides a valuable overview of the current landscape and highlights the challenges in complex video reasoning.
    *   **Insightful analysis:** The analysis of the effects of 'thinking', visual information, and video complexity offers actionable insights for future research.
    *   **Publicly available dataset:** The release of the VIDEOREASONBENCH dataset and code promotes reproducibility and enables further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scope of the video types:** While the benchmark includes six different video demonstrations, these may not cover the entire spectrum of real-world video scenarios. More diverse and complex settings could provide further challenges and insights.
    *   **Lack of improvement methods:** While identifying the problem, the paper doesn't propose concrete methods for improving MLLM performance on the benchmark. Future research could focus on developing novel architectures or training strategies tailored to these types of tasks.
    *   **Human performance:** While human performance is presented, comparing the model's performance to the human baseline is essential for understanding the gap between the current models and human-level performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The benchmark is likely to influence future research in video reasoning by providing a challenging and well-defined evaluation environment. It could drive the development of new architectures, training strategies, and reasoning techniques specifically designed for complex visual understanding.

**Score:** 7.5/10

**Justification:**
The paper presents a novel and valuable benchmark that fills a gap in the existing video reasoning landscape. VIDEOREASONBENCH challenges the existing approaches and identifies limitations of current MLLMs. However, the impact is somewhat tempered by the limited scope of video types and the absence of concrete methods for improving model performance on the benchmark. Therefore, while the paper represents a significant contribution, it falls short of being a truly exceptional breakthrough.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Threading the Needle: Reweaving Chain-of-Thought Reasoning to Explain Human Label Variation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23368v1)
- **Authors**: Beiduo Chen, Yang Janet Liu, Anna Korhonen, Barbara Plank
- **Abstract**: The recent rise of reasoning-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs)--which generate chains of thought (CoTs) before giving the final answer--has attracted significant attention and offers new opportunities for gaining insights into human label variation, which refers to plausible differences in how multiple annotators label the same data instance. Prior work has shown that LLM-generated explanations can help align model predictions with human label distributions, but typically adopt a reverse paradigm: producing explanations based on given answers. In contrast, CoTs provide a forward reasoning path that may implicitly embed rationales for each answer option, before generating the answers. We thus propose a novel LLM-based pipeline enriched with linguistically-grounded discourse segmenters to extract supporting and opposing statements for each answer option from CoTs with improved accuracy. We also propose a rank-based HLV evaluation framework that prioritizes the ranking of answers over exact scores, which instead favor direct comparison of label distributions. Our method outperforms a direct generation method as well as baselines on three datasets, and shows better alignment of ranking methods with humans, highlighting the effectiveness of our approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of human label variation (HLV) in NLP tasks, which refers to the phenomenon where different annotators provide divergent but valid labels for the same input.  It proposes a novel approach, CoT2EL, that repurposes chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning generated by large language models (LLMs) as a source of explanations to understand and model HLV. Unlike previous methods that generate explanations *after* a label is chosen (post-hoc), this approach leverages the *forward* reasoning inherent in CoTs, where reasoning steps precede the final answer, potentially capturing label-specific rationales.  The CoT2EL pipeline uses discourse segmenters to extract supporting and opposing statements for each answer option from the CoT. A new rank-based HLV evaluation framework is also introduced, prioritizing the ranking of answers over exact probability distributions. The paper demonstrates improved performance compared to direct explanation generation and explanation-free baselines on three datasets (VariErr NLI, CommonsenseQA, and Social IQa) and shows better alignment with human rankings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits moderate novelty. Repurposing CoTs for HLV is a clever idea and is well-motivated, especially given the limitations of existing post-hoc explanation methods. The use of discourse segmenters for extracting relevant statements and imposing structure onto the CoTs also adds to the novelty. The rank-based evaluation is a departure from the more typical probability distribution comparison, and, as the authors argue, is indeed more robust.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in offering a new perspective on exploiting the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. The work challenges the common practice of explanation generation and provides a more nuanced method by extracting explanations directly from the forward reasoning flow. The improved accuracy and alignment with human annotation practices on benchmark datasets suggest that this approach could be valuable for building more robust and human-aligned NLP systems. The emphasis on ranking is also well-placed.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Motivated Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing HLV modeling approaches and motivates the need for a new perspective.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The proposed pipeline, CoT2EL, is clearly described, and the integration of discourse segmenters is a sensible way to extract structured explanations.
    *   **Novel Evaluation Framework:** The introduction of a rank-based evaluation framework addresses some of the limitations of traditional distribution-based metrics, providing a more robust assessment of HLV modeling.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across multiple datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach compared to relevant baselines.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The ablation studies (e.g., support vs. oppose, structure analysis) offer valuable insights into the contributing factors to the model's performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLM Quality:** The approach is inherently dependent on the quality and structure of the LLM-generated CoTs. If the CoTs are poorly reasoned or lack sufficient information, the extracted explanations may not be helpful. This dependence is acknowledged but not fully mitigated.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While not explicitly addressed, the CoT2EL pipeline is likely more computationally expensive than direct explanation generation, requiring multiple LLM calls.
    *   **Generalizability**: While tested on three datasets, the generalizability of these findings to other domains or tasks should be explored further. In particular, it's crucial to investigate how this approach scales and adapts to tasks with different types of reasoning or more complex label structures.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Discourse Relations:** While discourse segmentation is used, the paper notes in the Conclusion that they only leveraged discourse segmentation and further work could incorporate explicit relations. The use of discourse relations has great potential to improve the models' ability to interpret the relations between supporting and opposing rationales.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence future research in HLV modeling and explanation generation. It encourages researchers to explore the potential of leveraging the forward reasoning capabilities of LLMs. The rank-based evaluation framework could also become a more common practice for evaluating HLV models.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by presenting a novel and well-executed approach to addressing the challenging problem of HLV. The technical soundness, empirical validation, and novel evaluation framework all contribute to the paper's strengths. However, the dependence on LLM-generated CoTs and the computational cost are real limitations. While tested in multiple settings, more work could be done to examine the generalizability of these findings. For these reasons, I assign a score in the moderately significant range.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### UniRL: Self-Improving Unified Multimodal Models via Supervised and Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23380v1)
- **Authors**: Weijia Mao, Zhenheng Yang, Mike Zheng Shou
- **Abstract**: Unified multimodal large language models such as Show-o and Janus have achieved strong performance across both generation and understanding tasks. However, these models typically rely on large-scale datasets and require substantial computation during the pretraining stage. In addition, several post-training methods have been proposed, but they often depend on external data or are limited to task-specific customization. In this work, we introduce UniRL, a self-improving post-training approach. Our approach enables the model to generate images from prompts and use them as training data in each iteration, without relying on any external image data. Moreover, it enables the two tasks to enhance each other: the generated images are used for understanding, and the understanding results are used to supervise generation. We explore supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to optimize the models. UniRL offers three key advantages: (1) it requires no external image data, as all training samples are generated by the model itself during training; (2) it not only improves individual task performance, but also reduces the imbalance between generation and understanding; and (3) it requires only several additional training steps during the post-training stage. We evaluate UniRL on top of Show-o and Janus, achieving a GenEval score of 0.77 for Show-o and 0.65 for Janus. Code and models will be released in https://github.com/showlab/UniRL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the UniRL paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniRL, a self-improving post-training approach for unified multimodal large language models. UniRL aims to improve both image generation and understanding capabilities of such models without relying on external image data. It achieves this by having the model generate images from prompts and then using those generated images, along with questions about them, as training data to fine-tune the model. This process creates a feedback loop where generation improves understanding, and understanding enhances generation. The paper explores both supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) for optimizing the models and introduces a new metric to quantify the imbalance between generation and understanding tasks. They evaluate UniRL on Show-o and Janus, showing improvements in both individual task performance and reduced imbalance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of self-improving training for multimodal models is interesting, although not entirely novel. The concept of using generated data to improve model performance has been explored in other domains. However, the application of this concept specifically to *unified* multimodal models to address the generation/understanding imbalance is a key distinguishing factor. Also, the careful combination of supervised and reinforcement learning strategies (SFT and GRPO) tailored for the unique characteristics of multimodal models contribute to the paper's novelty.
*   **Significance:** Improving the efficiency and effectiveness of post-training for multimodal models is a significant problem. Pre-training these models is computationally expensive and data-hungry. Therefore, a method that can refine existing models with minimal resources is valuable. The paper’s emphasis on addressing the imbalance between generation and understanding is also noteworthy, as it highlights a common challenge in multimodal models that prior work hasn't tackled directly. The paper's contributions in terms of a new metric for measuring the balance between the generation and understanding tasks is valuable to the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Relevant Problem:** Focuses on a practical issue (post-training optimization) for an important class of models (unified multimodal LLMs).
    *   **Self-Improving Loop:** The core idea of the self-improving loop is clever and could be extended to other areas.
    *   **No External Data:** Avoids the need for large, labeled datasets, making it more accessible.
    *   **GRPO Strategy:** The use of GRPO shows good results overall, however it might require more explorations to fully optimize the performance on top of a better performing SFT model.
    *   **Balance Metric:** Attempting to quantify the balance between generation and understanding is a good approach to evaluating multimodal models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results show improvements, they aren't ground-breaking. The gains on existing models are appreciable but not transformative.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments focus on relatively simple understanding tasks (counting, color, position). It's unclear how well UniRL would generalize to more complex reasoning or abstract understanding tasks.
    *   **GRPO Performance:** In the Show-o case, the SFT strategy gave better T2I metrics which means the GRPO strategy should be built on top of a better trained SFT to enhance performance.
    *   **Evaluation Limitations:** The evaluation is somewhat limited to the GenEval benchmark.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** While the paper mentions ablation studies, a deeper dive into the effects of different components, such as the type of prompts used, reward function design, or temperature parameter in Gumbel-Softmax, would further solidify the claims.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper’s ideas could influence future research in efficient training methods for multimodal models. The balance metric could become a standard evaluation criterion. The idea of using generated data for self-improvement could inspire other creative training strategies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid, incremental contribution to the field of multimodal learning. The idea of self-improving through generated data is interesting and the focus on balancing generation and understanding is important. The method is clearly explained and evaluated. However, the results are not revolutionary and the scope of the experiments is somewhat limited. While the metric is useful and easy to interpret, the paper fails to build a SFT model that would be a strong baseline to demonstrate the potential of GRPO.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Afterburner: Reinforcement Learning Facilitates Self-Improving Code Efficiency Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23387v1)
- **Authors**: Mingzhe Du, Luu Tuan Tuan, Yue Liu, Yuhao Qing, Dong Huang, Xinyi He, Qian Liu, Zejun Ma, See-kiong Ng
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) generate functionally correct solutions but often fall short in code efficiency, a critical bottleneck for real-world deployment. In this paper, we introduce a novel test-time iterative optimization framework to address this, employing a closed-loop system where LLMs iteratively refine code based on empirical performance feedback from an execution sandbox. We explore three training strategies: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Group Relative Policy Optimization~(GRPO). Experiments on our Venus dataset and the APPS benchmark show that SFT and DPO rapidly saturate in efficiency gains. In contrast, GRPO, using reinforcement learning (RL) with execution feedback, continuously optimizes code performance, significantly boosting both pass@1 (from 47% to 62%) and the likelihood of outperforming human submissions in efficiency (from 31% to 45%). Our work demonstrates effective test-time code efficiency improvement and critically reveals the power of RL in teaching LLMs to truly self-improve code efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Afterburner," a novel framework for iteratively optimizing the code efficiency of Large Language Model (LLM)-generated code at test time. The approach employs a closed-loop system: an "Afterburner" model refines the code based on performance feedback from a "Monolith" sandbox (execution environment). The paper explores three training strategies for the Afterburner model: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Experiments on a new dataset (Venus) and the APPS benchmark show that GRPO, using reinforcement learning (RL) with execution feedback, outperforms SFT and DPO, significantly improving both the pass rate and the ability to outperform human-written code in efficiency. The work argues that RL is crucial for teaching LLMs to truly self-improve code efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to code optimization. While iterative refinement and RL have been explored in other domains, the specific application to LLM-generated code efficiency, with the careful design of the feedback loop and the GRPO training strategy, is a solid contribution.  The combination of "Afterburner" for code modification and "Monolith" for execution feedback seems like a promising approach.  The construction of the Venus dataset, targeted specifically at code efficiency evaluation, is also a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:**  The significance is high because code efficiency is a critical bottleneck for deploying LLMs in real-world systems.  Demonstrating a practical method to improve efficiency post-generation directly addresses this challenge.  The finding that RL-based GRPO significantly outperforms SFT and DPO in this context provides valuable insight for future research in LLM optimization. The demonstration that LLMs can, with appropriate training, surpass human-written code *in efficiency* is a striking result. This could change expectations for what is possible with automated code generation.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies a crucial problem (code efficiency of LLMs) and offers a practical solution.
    * **Rigorous Experiments:** The experiments are comprehensive, using both a new dataset (Venus) and a standard benchmark (APPS).  The comparison of different training strategies (SFT, DPO, GRPO) is well-executed.
    * **Strong Results:** The results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the Afterburner framework, especially with the GRPO training strategy. The ablation studies provide further insights.
    * **Clear Explanations:** The paper clearly explains the rationale behind each component of the framework and the observed experimental results. The explanations for why GRPO outperforms other methods are well-reasoned.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The current framework appears primarily focused on algorithmic code, specifically targeting competition-level programming tasks. The applicability of the approach to larger, more complex software engineering projects (with more diverse efficiency criteria and task decomposition requirements) could be further discussed.
    * **Inference Time Tradeoff:** The iterative optimization process inherently increases inference time. The paper mentions this but could provide a more detailed analysis of the cost-benefit trade-off in various deployment scenarios. How many iterations are typically needed to achieve substantial improvements? How does the increased inference time compare to the execution time savings achieved by more efficient code? This kind of analysis would make the work more compelling from a practical perspective.
    * **Dataset Bias:** The Venus dataset, while valuable, is still based on LeetCode problems. It would be beneficial to explore the framework's performance on a more diverse and realistic set of code generation tasks.
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:**  Like all RL-based methods, GRPO likely has some degree of hyperparameter sensitivity.  While the paper describes the hyperparameters used, a more thorough exploration of their impact on performance would add to the robustness of the study.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM-based code generation. It highlights a crucial area of improvement (efficiency) and presents a promising, data-driven approach to addressing it. The findings regarding RL-based training could guide future research in this area.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a significant and novel contribution to the field by directly addressing the crucial issue of code efficiency in LLM-generated code. The Afterburner framework, particularly with the GRPO training strategy, demonstrates a substantial improvement over existing methods. The experimental results are compelling, and the paper provides a solid analysis of the findings. While there are some limitations in scope (algorithmic code vs. large software projects) and a need for further analysis of the inference time tradeoff, the overall impact and potential influence of this work are high, warranting a strong score.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Adaptive Jailbreaking Strategies Based on the Semantic Understanding Capabilities of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23404v1)
- **Authors**: Mingyu Yu, Wei Wang, Yanjie Wei, Sujuan Qin
- **Abstract**: Adversarial attacks on Large Language Models (LLMs) via jailbreaking techniques-methods that circumvent their built-in safety and ethical constraints-have emerged as a critical challenge in AI security. These attacks compromise the reliability of LLMs by exploiting inherent weaknesses in their comprehension capabilities. This paper investigates the efficacy of jailbreaking strategies that are specifically adapted to the diverse levels of understanding exhibited by different LLMs. We propose the Adaptive Jailbreaking Strategies Based on the Semantic Understanding Capabilities of Large Language Models, a novel framework that classifies LLMs into Type I and Type II categories according to their semantic comprehension abilities. For each category, we design tailored jailbreaking strategies aimed at leveraging their vulnerabilities to facilitate successful attacks. Extensive experiments conducted on multiple LLMs demonstrate that our adaptive strategy markedly improves the success rate of jailbreaking. Notably, our approach achieves an exceptional 98.9% success rate in jailbreaking GPT-4o(29 May 2025 release)
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an "Adaptive Jailbreaking Strategies Based on the Semantic Understanding Capabilities of Large Language Models" framework. This framework aims to improve the success rate of jailbreaking attacks against LLMs by tailoring attack strategies to the semantic understanding capabilities of different LLMs. The core idea is to classify LLMs into two types: Type I (weaker understanding) and Type II (stronger understanding), and then design separate jailbreaking strategies for each type. Type I models use a dual-layer mutation, while Type II models use a triple-layer mutation with Caesar cipher encryption for further obfuscation. The paper claims a very high jailbreak success rate, particularly against GPT-4o (May 2025 release). The techniques involve input obfuscation, task abstraction through decryption prompts, and output encryption. The authors argue that their method comprehensively targets input filtering, inference-time compliance checking, and output moderation, resulting in superior performance compared to baseline approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Adaptive Approach:** The key strength lies in its adaptive approach that tailors jailbreaking strategies based on the LLM's level of understanding. This is a more sophisticated approach than simply applying one technique to all models.
*   **Comprehensive Attack Framework:** The combined use of input obfuscation, task abstraction, and output encryption addresses multiple defensive layers used in LLMs, increasing the potential for success.
*   **High Reported Success Rate:** The paper boasts a very high success rate, especially against a recent version of GPT-4o, which, if verified, is a significant accomplishment.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly outlines the methodology, including the two types of LLMs, the distinct strategies for each type, and the encryption techniques used.
*   **Well-Structured:** the paper is well-structured which makes it easy to understand the core concepts and components.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Future date GPT-4o:** The paper relies heavily on the performance against a future iteration of GPT-4o. The claimed performance is difficult to independently verify, given the non-existent nature of this model.
*   **Limited Details on Real-World Scenarios:** The paper uses a restricted set of prompts that may not fully reflect real-world vulnerabilities. More diverse and complex prompts would strengthen the findings.
*   **Limited Public Availability of Code and Datasets:** The reproducibility of the experiments is also questionable without publicly available code.
*   **Overemphasis on Results:** The paper emphasizes the high success rates while potentially understating the limitations.
*   **Evaluation Metric (ASR):** It relies on the evaluation of LLMs as evaluators.
*   **Novelty:** Some of the techniques aren't entirely new. Input obfuscation/encryption, multi-step prompts are all known techniques, and this paper simply combines these techniques.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The *idea* of adapting jailbreaking strategies to the LLM's understanding capabilities has potential and brings *some* novelty to the area. The high reported success rates of the proposed framework (if independently verified) may have impact. However, *without* the ability to verify the results, and with some of the techniques being known, the overall novelty is somewhat limited.

**Potential Influence:**

If the results are reproducible and generalizable, the paper could influence the direction of research towards more adaptive and targeted jailbreaking techniques. However, the lack of transparency and the questionable nature of the future-dated LLM limits its immediate influence.

**Justification for the Score:**

Given the potential significance of the adaptive approach and the comprehensive attack framework, but tempered by the verifiability issues, and the potentially inflated results, the score is:

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### From Parameters to Prompts: Understanding and Mitigating the Factuality Gap between Fine-Tuned LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23410v1)
- **Authors**: Xuan Gong, Hanbo Huang, Shiyu Liang
- **Abstract**: Factual knowledge extraction aims to explicitly extract knowledge parameterized in pre-trained language models for application in downstream tasks. While prior work has been investigating the impact of supervised fine-tuning data on the factuality of large language models (LLMs), its mechanism remains poorly understood. We revisit this impact through systematic experiments, with a particular focus on the factuality gap that arises when fine-tuning on known versus unknown knowledge. Our findings show that this gap can be mitigated at the inference stage, either under out-of-distribution (OOD) settings or by using appropriate in-context learning (ICL) prompts (i.e., few-shot learning and Chain of Thought (CoT)). We prove this phenomenon theoretically from the perspective of knowledge graphs, showing that the test-time prompt may diminish or even overshadow the impact of fine-tuning data and play a dominant role in knowledge extraction. Ultimately, our results shed light on the interaction between finetuning data and test-time prompt, demonstrating that ICL can effectively compensate for shortcomings in fine-tuning data, and highlighting the need to reconsider the use of ICL prompting as a means to evaluate the effectiveness of fine-tuning data selection methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "From Parameters to Prompts: Understanding and Mitigating the Factuality Gap between Fine-Tuned LLMs."

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of fine-tuning data on the factuality of large language models (LLMs), with a specific focus on the factuality gap that emerges when LLMs are fine-tuned on known versus unknown knowledge.  Through systematic experiments, the authors demonstrate that this factuality gap (where models perform worse on unknown facts after being fine-tuned on a dataset containing a mix of known and unknown facts) can be mitigated at inference time using appropriate in-context learning (ICL) prompts, such as few-shot learning and Chain of Thought (CoT). The paper provides a theoretical justification for this phenomenon using a knowledge graph perspective, arguing that test-time prompts can diminish or even overshadow the impact of fine-tuning data, playing a dominant role in knowledge extraction.  The study concludes that ICL can compensate for shortcomings in fine-tuning data and that the effectiveness of fine-tuning data selection should be re-evaluated in the context of ICL prompting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its focus on the *interaction* between fine-tuning data characteristics (known vs. unknown facts) and test-time prompts (specifically ICL) in determining the factuality of LLM outputs. While prior work has examined the impact of fine-tuning data and ICL independently, this paper offers a more nuanced understanding of how they jointly influence factuality. The use of a knowledge graph framework to theoretically explain the observed phenomena is also a novel contribution. However, the individual components (studying factuality, fine-tuning impact, and ICL's influence) are not entirely new; it's the combination and explanation that provide the incremental advance.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant practical implications. By demonstrating that ICL can mitigate the factuality gap caused by fine-tuning on imperfect datasets, the paper suggests that careful prompt design can be a valuable tool for improving the reliability of LLMs, even when training data is limited or contains noise. Furthermore, the knowledge graph perspective provides a useful framework for understanding how knowledge is represented and accessed in LLMs, which could inform the development of more effective fine-tuning strategies and prompting techniques. The paper questions the current evaluation protocols for finetuning data selection methods, suggesting that incorporating ICL into the evaluation is necessary.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic experiments across multiple models (Llama, Mistral) and tasks (QA, open-ended generation).
    *   Theoretical justification grounded in knowledge graph modeling.
    *   Clear articulation of research questions and well-defined experimental setups.
    *   Practical implications for prompt engineering and data selection strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical framework, while insightful, relies on simplifying assumptions about LLM architecture and knowledge representation. It is a one-layer transformer so it cannot directly be applied to very complex models.
    *   The study does not fully address anomalous behaviors in complex tasks. The limitations mentioned in the paper, such as dealing with complex tasks, should be explored in future work.
    *   The degree of effectiveness may vary with LLM model type and task type. The analysis does not give guidance for which prompt types work in which scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on LLM factuality, prompting strategies, and data selection for fine-tuning. It highlights the importance of considering the interplay between training data and inference-time techniques, which could lead to the development of more robust and reliable LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid, incremental contribution to the field of LLM research. It combines empirical observations with a theoretical framework to provide a more nuanced understanding of the factuality gap. It doesn't represent a groundbreaking paradigm shift, but it does offer valuable insights and practical implications. The limitations are clearly articulated.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### KVzip: Query-Agnostic KV Cache Compression with Context Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23416v1)
- **Authors**: Jang-Hyun Kim, Jinuk Kim, Sangwoo Kwon, Jae W. Lee, Sangdoo Yun, Hyun Oh Song
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based large language models (LLMs) cache context as key-value (KV) pairs during inference. As context length grows, KV cache sizes expand, leading to substantial memory overhead and increased attention latency. This paper introduces KVzip, a query-agnostic KV cache eviction method enabling effective reuse of compressed KV caches across diverse queries. KVzip quantifies the importance of a KV pair using the underlying LLM to reconstruct original contexts from cached KV pairs, subsequently evicting pairs with lower importance. Extensive empirical evaluations demonstrate that KVzip reduces KV cache size by 3-4$\times$ and FlashAttention decoding latency by approximately 2$\times$, with negligible performance loss in question-answering, retrieval, reasoning, and code comprehension tasks. Evaluations include various models such as LLaMA3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-14B, and Gemma3-12B, with context lengths reaching up to 170K tokens. KVzip significantly outperforms existing query-aware KV eviction methods, which suffer from performance degradation even at a 90% cache budget ratio under multi-query scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KVzip, a novel query-agnostic KV cache eviction method for transformer-based language models (LLMs). Unlike existing query-aware approaches that evict KV pairs based on the relevance to the current query, KVzip aims to create a reusable, compressed KV cache that generalizes across diverse future queries.  The key idea is to quantify the importance of KV pairs based on their ability to reconstruct the original context using the underlying LLM itself.  The method simulates context reconstruction during prefill, assigns importance scores based on maximum attention scores, and evicts pairs with low importance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that KVzip achieves significant KV cache size reduction (3-4x) and FlashAttention decoding latency reduction (approximately 2x) with minimal performance loss on various tasks and models.

**Critical Evaluation:**
