### Cognitive Guardrails for Open-World Decision Making in Autonomous Drone Swarms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23576v1)
- **Authors**: Jane Cleland-Huang, Pedro Antonio Alarcon Granadeno, Arturo Miguel Russell Bernal, Demetrius Hernandez, Michael Murphy, Maureen Petterson, Walter Scheirer
- **Abstract**: Small Uncrewed Aerial Systems (sUAS) are increasingly deployed as autonomous swarms in search-and-rescue and other disaster-response scenarios. In these settings, they use computer vision (CV) to detect objects of interest and autonomously adapt their missions. However, traditional CV systems often struggle to recognize unfamiliar objects in open-world environments or to infer their relevance for mission planning. To address this, we incorporate large language models (LLMs) to reason about detected objects and their implications. While LLMs can offer valuable insights, they are also prone to hallucinations and may produce incorrect, misleading, or unsafe recommendations. To ensure safe and sensible decision-making under uncertainty, high-level decisions must be governed by cognitive guardrails. This article presents the design, simulation, and real-world integration of these guardrails for sUAS swarms in search-and-rescue missions.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper introduces CAIRN, a framework for enabling autonomous decision-making in drone swarms operating in open-world environments, particularly in search-and-rescue (SAR) missions. CAIRN combines Bayesian belief updates, contextual reasoning powered by large language models (LLMs), and cognitive guardrails to ensure safe, transparent, and interpretable decision-making. The framework aims to address the limitations of traditional computer vision systems in recognizing unfamiliar objects and inferring their relevance in dynamic and uncertain scenarios. It leverages LLMs to reason about detected objects, while implementing safeguards to mitigate potential hallucinations and unsafe recommendations. The paper presents the design of CAIRN, describes its integration with a simulation environment, and discusses its potential for deployment in a physical drone swarming platform.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The combination of Bayesian inference with LLM-based reasoning and cognitive guardrails for drone swarm decision-making represents a significant step forward. While individual components like Bayesian models and LLMs have been used in robotics, CAIRN’s integrated architecture provides a novel solution to open-world reasoning.  The addition of runtime cognitive guardrails specifically designed for mitigating LLM limitations in the context of sUAS control is a notable contribution.

**Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial challenge in autonomous robotics: enabling reliable and safe operation in complex and unpredictable environments.  Search and rescue missions are an excellent application domain due to the high-stakes nature of the decisions and the inherent uncertainties. The framework's potential to enhance the effectiveness and safety of drone swarms in such scenarios is significant. If successfully deployed and validated, CAIRN could influence the design of future autonomous systems operating in similar domains.  The emphasis on human-machine teaming and the inclusion of ethical and regulatory considerations enhance the framework's practical relevance.

**Strengths:**
*   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the problem of open-world reasoning and the limitations of current CV systems.
*   **Integrated Approach:** The integration of Bayesian methods, LLMs, and cognitive guardrails is a well-reasoned and promising approach.
*   **Practical Focus:**  The SAR mission context provides a concrete application for the framework, demonstrating its potential benefits.
*   **Comprehensive Design:** The paper details the key components of CAIRN, including the Bayesian model, LLM pipeline, and cognitive guardrails.
*   **Consideration of Limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limitations of LLMs and implement safeguards to mitigate potential risks.
*   **Discussion of Evaluation:** Includes discussion of evaluation using a simulated enviornment.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Limited Empirical Validation:** The results are primarily based on simulation and a replay video. Extensive real-world testing and validation are needed to demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of CAIRN in practical SAR scenarios.  Detailed metrics quantifying the improvement in SAR performance (e.g., search time, area coverage) compared to existing methods are lacking.
*   **Reliance on GPT-40:** The framework's performance is heavily dependent on the capabilities of GPT-40. The sensitivity of CAIRN to the specific LLM used and the potential for degradation with different LLM models or versions should be investigated.
*   **CPT Definition:** The reliance on domain knowledge and subjective assumptions for defining the conditional probability tables (CPTs) in the Bayesian model is a potential weakness. A more systematic approach to CPT construction based on empirical data or expert elicitation may be needed.
*   **Heuristic parameters**: Many of the weightings and parameters (gamma and lambada) are defined heuristically and may require tuning.
*   **Guardrail efficacy:** The degree to which the guardrails prevent unsafe behavior isn't clearly quantifiable.
*   **Implementation details**: Much of the implementation is glossed over, such as the specific prompts used in the LLM.

**Potential Influence:**

CAIRN has the potential to influence the design of autonomous systems for various domains, particularly those involving complex environments and uncertain information. The framework's emphasis on cognitive guardrails and human-machine teaming could promote the development of more responsible and trustworthy autonomous systems. The integrated approach could serve as a blueprint for future research in open-world reasoning and decision-making in robotics.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty and significance of the approach, but also acknowledging the limitations related to empirical validation and implementation details, a score of 7 is appropriate. The integration of different AI techniques to address a pertinent problem is significant. However, the lack of robust validation currently limits the demonstrable impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### On-Policy RL with Optimal Reward Baseline
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23585v1)
- **Authors**: Yaru Hao, Li Dong, Xun Wu, Shaohan Huang, Zewen Chi, Furu Wei
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning algorithms are fundamental to align large language models with human preferences and to enhance their reasoning capabilities. However, current reinforcement learning algorithms often suffer from training instability due to loose on-policy constraints and computational inefficiency due to auxiliary models. In this work, we propose On-Policy RL with Optimal reward baseline (OPO), a novel and simplified reinforcement learning algorithm designed to address these challenges. OPO emphasizes the importance of exact on-policy training, which empirically stabilizes the training process and enhances exploration. Moreover, OPO introduces the optimal reward baseline that theoretically minimizes gradient variance. We evaluate OPO on mathematical reasoning benchmarks. The results demonstrate its superior performance and training stability without additional models or regularization terms. Furthermore, OPO achieves lower policy shifts and higher output entropy, encouraging more diverse and less repetitive responses. These results highlight OPO as a promising direction for stable and effective reinforcement learning in large language model alignment and reasoning tasks. The implementation is provided at https://github.com/microsoft/LMOps/tree/main/opo.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces On-Policy RL with Optimal Reward Baseline (OPO), a new reinforcement learning algorithm designed to improve the stability and efficiency of training large language models (LLMs). OPO emphasizes exact on-policy training to stabilize the learning process and introduces a theoretically derived optimal reward baseline to minimize gradient variance. The authors demonstrate that OPO outperforms existing baselines on mathematical reasoning benchmarks while maintaining lower policy shift, higher output entropy, and eliminates the need for auxiliary components like value models and regularization terms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces two key components: strict on-policy training and an optimal reward baseline. While on-policy methods have been explored before, the emphasis on *exact* on-policy training as a central design principle for LLM alignment and the derivation of a closed-form optimal reward baseline *is* a notable contribution. The simplification of the reward baseline formula for sequence generation is also a valuable practical consideration. The claim of the exact on-policy training mitigating KL and entropy collapse, negating the need for regularization, is quite powerful.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is significant. RLHF is a cornerstone of LLM alignment. Making RLHF more stable and efficient directly addresses a critical bottleneck in LLM development. By simplifying the RL training process and reducing the computational overhead (no value function), OPO could make RLHF more accessible and scalable. The empirical results on mathematical reasoning are promising, suggesting that OPO can improve the ability of LLMs to perform complex tasks. The benefits of exact on-policy are also potentially significant.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a clear theoretical justification for the optimal reward baseline, deriving it from first principles. This is a significant strength because it provides a solid foundation for the algorithm.
    *   **Simplicity:** OPO is a relatively simple algorithm compared to other RLHF methods. It removes the need for extra value functions and careful hyperparameter tuning for KL and entropy penalties. This simplicity makes it easier to implement and potentially more robust.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents strong empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of OPO on mathematical reasoning benchmarks. It also demonstrates that OPO leads to lower policy shift and higher output entropy.
    *   **Comprehensive Implementation Details:** The authors make their implementation publicly available, which is a critical step for reproducibility and adoption by the community.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The empirical evaluation is primarily focused on mathematical reasoning tasks. While these tasks are important, it is unclear how well OPO would perform on other RLHF tasks, such as safety alignment or instruction following. The rule-based reward in this regime also makes it easier for the reward baseline to be beneficial, it is unclear whether this will generalize to the more nuanced scenarios.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** While the paper compares OPO to several baselines, it lacks thorough ablation studies to isolate the individual contributions of exact on-policy training and the optimal reward baseline. While it attempts to compare on- and off- policy performance with the GRPO set up, a more thorough exploration of combining regular and optimal reward baselines would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Rule-based Reward:** The reliance on a rule-based reward for the experimental evaluation could be seen as a weakness. It would be beneficial to see how OPO performs with a learned reward model, which is the standard setting for RLHF.
    *   **Potential Limitations:** The simplified optimal reward baseline is predicated on approximate orthogonality. This is not necessarily true, and the results from adopting these simplifications would need to be further analyzed.
*   **Potential influence on the field:** It provides a new way to approach RLHF and tackles the critical problem of efficient alignment and reasoning. It opens potential avenues for future research, especially in more efficient training regimes.
**Overall:**

This is a strong paper that introduces a novel and well-justified RL algorithm. While there are some limitations in the empirical evaluation and the need for more ablation experiments, the potential impact of OPO on the field of LLM alignment is significant. It provides a promising direction for future research and has the potential to make RLHF more accessible and scalable.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**
The novelty and practical utility of OPO are well argued. The work offers a compelling alternative approach to the RLHF and reasoning. However, it stops short of the critical elements to achieve a high-impact paper, most significantly a demonstrated benefit in an RLHF setting. Also, the assumptions surrounding the simplifications made in equation (10) and a stronger set of ablation experiments will be needed to fully establish this algorithm's utility to its potential and merit a significantly higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Jigsaw-R1: A Study of Rule-based Visual Reinforcement Learning with Jigsaw Puzzles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23590v1)
- **Authors**: Zifu Wang, Junyi Zhu, Bo Tang, Zhiyu Li, Feiyu Xiong, Jiaqian Yu, Matthew B. Blaschko
- **Abstract**: The application of rule-based reinforcement learning (RL) to multimodal large language models (MLLMs) introduces unique challenges and potential deviations from findings in text-only domains, particularly for perception-heavy tasks. This paper provides a comprehensive study of rule-based visual RL using jigsaw puzzles as a structured experimental framework, revealing several key findings. \textit{Firstly,} we find that MLLMs, initially performing near to random guessing on simple puzzles, achieve near-perfect accuracy and generalize to complex, unseen configurations through fine-tuning. \textit{Secondly,} training on jigsaw puzzles can induce generalization to other visual tasks, with effectiveness tied to specific task configurations. \textit{Thirdly,} MLLMs can learn and generalize with or without explicit reasoning, though open-source models often favor direct answering. Consequently, even when trained for step-by-step reasoning, they can ignore the thinking process in deriving the final answer. \textit{Fourthly,} we observe that complex reasoning patterns appear to be pre-existing rather than emergent, with their frequency increasing alongside training and task difficulty. \textit{Finally,} our results demonstrate that RL exhibits more effective generalization than Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), and an initial SFT cold start phase can hinder subsequent RL optimization. Although these observations are based on jigsaw puzzles and may vary across other visual tasks, this research contributes a valuable piece of jigsaw to the larger puzzle of collective understanding rule-based visual RL and its potential in multimodal learning. The code is available at: \href{https://github.com/zifuwanggg/Jigsaw-R1}{https://github.com/zifuwanggg/Jigsaw-R1}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Jigsaw-R1: A Study of Rule-based Visual Reinforcement Learning with Jigsaw Puzzles" explores rule-based reinforcement learning (RL) applied to multimodal large language models (MLLMs) using jigsaw puzzles as a structured visual task. The authors systematically investigate the capabilities of MLLMs in solving jigsaw puzzles, analyzing generalization, the role of explicit reasoning, and the impact of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) versus RL.  Key findings include MLLMs can achieve near-perfect accuracy on jigsaw puzzles with fine-tuning and generalize to unseen configurations, that jigsaw puzzle training can generalize to other visual tasks, the MLLMs can learn with or without explicit reasoning (although open source models tend towards direct answering), pre-existing reasoning patterns, and that RL exhibits better generalization than SFT.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  While jigsaw puzzles are not novel in computer vision or self-supervised learning, their use as a *structured environment* for *studying the nuances of rule-based visual RL with MLLMs* represents a valuable contribution. The systematic exploration of different aspects (reasoning, generalization, SFT vs. RL) is a strength. A previous paper applied reinforcement learning to jigsaw puzzles [https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.07893.pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.07893.pdf). The novelty here, compared to that paper, is mainly in the investigation of multimodal models, large language models, and some interesting features like "aha moment" and reasoning behavior.
* **Significance:** The paper tackles a relevant and timely problem: understanding the interaction of RL, visual perception, and reasoning in MLLMs. The findings have practical implications for training MLLMs for visual tasks and provide valuable insights into the models' reasoning processes. Showing that RL surpasses SFT for generalization is an important result. Additionally, by revealing the pre-existing nature of complex reasoning patterns and evaluating the necessity of explicit reasoning steps, the paper challenges some common assumptions in the field. The observations on how a SFT start can *hinder* subsequent RL fine-tuning is highly interesting and practically significant.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Systematic Approach:** The paper employs a well-designed experimental framework to investigate different aspects of visual RL.
    * **Clear Research Questions:** The research questions are well-defined and address important challenges.
    * **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the experimental results, supported by tables, figures, and illustrative examples.
    * **Practical Implications:** The findings offer practical guidelines for training MLLMs for visual tasks.
    * **Open Source Code:** Making the code publicly available enhances reproducibility and allows others to build on their work.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Task Specificity:** The study focuses solely on jigsaw puzzles, which might limit the generalizability of the findings to other visual tasks. The authors themselves acknowledge that the observations "may vary across other visual tasks."
    * **Limited Model Scope:**  The paper mainly focuses on Qwen2 models and GPT-4. More in-depth comparison with other MLLMs could strengthen the results. Specifically, mentioning the "vision reasoning" models (e.g., [25. Li et al., 2025a] and many other similar papers) while not testing them directly leaves some room for future study.
    * **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:**  The paper relies heavily on empirical observations.  Some theoretical grounding of the phenomena observed, even if high-level, would strengthen the contribution. The "aha moment" analysis relies on keyword analysis, which can be somewhat subjective.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in rule-based visual RL for MLLMs.  The insights on generalization, the role of reasoning, and SFT vs. RL will be valuable for researchers and practitioners working in this area. The jigsaw puzzle environment offers a straightforward way to study MLLMs without requiring extensive manual annotation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to understanding rule-based visual RL within MLLMs. The systematic approach, clear research questions, comprehensive analysis, and practical implications outweigh the limitations related to task specificity and model scope. While the basic jigsaw framework is not new, its use in dissecting MLLM learning, reasoning, and generalization in a structured way justifies a good score. The demonstration of potential harm arising from SFT initialization provides significant practical value.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### MAPLE: A Mobile Assistant with Persistent Finite State Machines for Recovery Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23596v1)
- **Authors**: Linqiang Guo, Wei Liu, Yi Wen Heng, Tse-Hsun, Chen, Yang Wang
- **Abstract**: Mobile GUI agents aim to autonomously complete user-instructed tasks across mobile apps. Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) enable these agents to interpret UI screens, identify actionable elements, and perform interactions such as tapping or typing. However, existing agents remain reactive: they reason only over the current screen and lack a structured model of app navigation flow, limiting their ability to understand context, detect unexpected outcomes, and recover from errors. We present MAPLE, a state-aware multi-agent framework that abstracts app interactions as a Finite State Machine (FSM). We computationally model each UI screen as a discrete state and user actions as transitions, allowing the FSM to provide a structured representation of the app execution. MAPLE consists of specialized agents responsible for four phases of task execution: planning, execution, verification, error recovery, and knowledge retention. These agents collaborate to dynamically construct FSMs in real time based on perception data extracted from the UI screen, allowing the GUI agents to track navigation progress and flow, validate action outcomes through pre- and post-conditions of the states, and recover from errors by rolling back to previously stable states. Our evaluation results on two challenging cross-app benchmarks, Mobile-Eval-E and SPA-Bench, show that MAPLE outperforms the state-of-the-art baseline, improving task success rate by up to 12%, recovery success by 13.8%, and action accuracy by 6.5%. Our results highlight the importance of structured state modeling in guiding mobile GUI agents during task execution. Moreover, our FSM representation can be integrated into future GUI agent architectures as a lightweight, model-agnostic memory layer to support structured planning, execution verification, and error recovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces MAPLE, a mobile GUI agent framework that uses Finite State Machines (FSMs) to improve task execution, error recovery, and knowledge retention across mobile applications. MAPLE addresses the limitations of existing GUI agents, which are primarily reactive and lack a structured model of app navigation flow. The framework consists of specialized agents for planning, execution, verification, error recovery, and knowledge retention.  MAPLE constructs FSMs in real-time based on UI screen perception data, allowing it to track navigation progress, validate action outcomes, and recover from errors by reverting to previously stable states.  The paper evaluates MAPLE on the Mobile-Eval-E and SPA-Bench benchmarks, demonstrating improvements over the state-of-the-art (Mobile-Agent-E + Evo) in task success rate, recovery success, and action accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of FSMs for representing app navigation within a mobile GUI agent framework. While FSMs are not new in themselves, their application to model and manage mobile app interactions in real-time, particularly for improving error recovery, is a worthwhile contribution. It provides structure to an otherwise unstructured navigation problem. However, graph-based approaches have been used to solve a similar problem in other contexts; the main innovation is the context (mobile GUI agents) rather than a purely algorithmic improvement.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key challenge in the field of mobile GUI agents: the lack of structured reasoning and context understanding. By using FSMs, MAPLE can better track its progress, anticipate outcomes, and recover from errors, leading to improved task completion rates and user satisfaction. The experimental results, demonstrating improvements over existing baselines, support the significance of this approach.
    However, the improvements are incremental and while they may be statistically significant, and while a 12% increase in task success may be practically relevant, the paper needs to show it scales with larger and more complex task scenarios. The limitations aren't strongly discussed either, especially concerning state explosion and the generalization capabilities to new, unseen apps. The reliance on large language models (LLMs) is also not critiqued well enough. The dependence on large, pre-trained models for tasks such as state description and UI element identification introduces vulnerabilities relating to model biases, cost, and reliance on cloud infrastructure, hindering widespread deployment.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Structured Approach:** The use of FSMs provides a principled way to model and reason about app navigation.
    *   **Multi-Agent Architecture:** The framework decomposes the task into specialized agents, enabling modularity and flexibility.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents strong experimental results on established benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of MAPLE. The results are well-quantified.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of reactive GUI agents and proposes a solution to address them.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework's performance depends heavily on the accuracy and reliability of LLMs for tasks such as state description and UI element identification. This reliance could lead to errors and inconsistencies. A more robust discussion of the limitations related to the use of LLMs is warranted.
    *   **State Explosion:** The number of states in the FSM could grow exponentially for complex apps with many screens and interactions. The paper does not adequately address this issue or propose strategies for mitigating it.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** The paper does not extensively explore the generalization capabilities of MAPLE to new, unseen apps. The performance on the benchmarks might not be indicative of its performance in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Lack of Comparison with Other Modeling Techniques**: It compares to other agents, but does not clearly show how it is superior to other state-tracking or planning methods. It does not provide a clear ablation of the different factors that contribute to MAPLE's success.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the design of future mobile GUI agents by highlighting the importance of structured reasoning and context understanding. The FSM-based approach could be integrated into other agent architectures as a lightweight memory layer. However, the influence will depend on addressing the limitations mentioned above and demonstrating its scalability and generalizability.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel application of FSMs within the domain of mobile GUI agents, addressing a critical gap in structured reasoning and context management. The results demonstrate quantifiable improvements over the state-of-the-art, indicating the potential of the proposed approach. The multi-agent architecture provides a modular and flexible design.

However, the paper is not without its limitations. The heavy reliance on LLMs raises concerns about accuracy, cost, and bias. The potential for state explosion in complex applications is not adequately addressed. Moreover, the paper needs to provide a more in-depth discussion of the trade-offs and challenges associated with using FSMs for modeling app navigation. Given these limitations, and that the gains while significant, are incremental, a score of 6.5 reflects a solid contribution with noteworthy potential for future research and development. The paper would have benefited from a broader discussion of limitations and more robust ablation studies to justify the score.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### LLM Performance for Code Generation on Noisy Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23598v1)
- **Authors**: Radzim Sendyka, Christian Cabrera, Andrei Paleyes, Diana Robinson, Neil Lawrence
- **Abstract**: This paper investigates the ability of large language models (LLMs) to recognise and solve tasks which have been obfuscated beyond recognition. Focusing on competitive programming and benchmark tasks (LeetCode and MATH), we compare performance across multiple models and obfuscation methods, such as noise and redaction. We demonstrate that all evaluated LLMs can solve tasks obfuscated to a level where the text would be unintelligible to human readers, and does not contain key pieces of instruction or context. We introduce the concept of eager pattern matching to describe this behaviour, which is not observed in tasks published after the models' knowledge cutoff date, indicating strong memorisation or overfitting to training data, rather than legitimate reasoning about the presented problem. We report empirical evidence of distinct performance decay patterns between contaminated and unseen datasets. We discuss the implications for benchmarking and evaluations of model behaviour, arguing for caution when designing experiments using standard datasets. We also propose measuring the decay of performance under obfuscation as a possible strategy for detecting dataset contamination and highlighting potential safety risks and interpretability issues for automated software systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to solve coding and mathematical reasoning problems that have been obfuscated to the point of being unrecognizable to humans. The study uses standard benchmark datasets (LeetCode, MATH) and applies different obfuscation methods like noise injection and redaction. The key finding is that LLMs can often solve these highly obfuscated problems, suggesting that they are relying on memorization and "eager pattern matching" rather than genuine reasoning. The paper introduces the concept of "eager pattern matching," where models solve tasks beyond human readability, especially when the task is present in the pre-training data. Further, performance decay under extreme obfuscation is shown to be a practical indicator of dataset contamination and overfitting. The paper concludes that benchmarking LLMs on contaminated datasets can be misleading, potentially overestimating their real capabilities and posing risks when deploying them in automated software systems. It also discusses implications for the safety and interpretability of these systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Important and Timely Problem:**  The paper addresses a critical and growing concern in the LLM community: dataset contamination and its impact on model evaluation. As LLMs are increasingly deployed in safety-critical applications (like code generation), reliable evaluation is crucial.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The use of extreme obfuscation as a probe for memorization and pattern matching is a clever and relatively novel approach. This method is more direct than analyzing token probability or completion overlap.
    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides solid empirical evidence across multiple models, datasets, and obfuscation techniques. The experiments and analysis are clearly presented.  The introduction of both LeetCode Old and New allows for a better assessment of contamination vs. actual reasoning.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper discusses the real-world implications of over-reliance on memorization, especially regarding safety risks and intellectual debt in multi-agent systems.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors have made their code and data available, enhancing the reproducibility and verifiability of their findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Datasets/Tasks:** The study is limited to 20 questions from each dataset, which might not be fully representative. Expanding the datasets to include a more comprehensive range of problems would enhance the generalizability of the conclusions.  The study also doesn't account for different levels of difficulty among the questions.
    *   **Human Baseline:** The human baseline assessment, while useful, is not perfectly comparable to the LLM evaluation metric. A direct comparison would be more compelling, although impractical.
    *   **Obfuscation Techniques:** While the obfuscation techniques (truncation, typos, deletion) are reasonable starting points, they might not fully capture all possible forms of adversarial noise.  More sophisticated obfuscation methods might reveal different aspects of LLM behavior.
    *   **Definition of "Eager Pattern Matching:"** While the paper introduces the term "eager pattern matching," it doesn't offer a formal or precise definition, which could leave it open to interpretation.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper offers a fresh perspective on dataset contamination by directly probing for memorization through extreme obfuscation. The findings challenge the validity of current evaluation practices and highlight the potential safety risks of deploying LLMs that rely heavily on memorization. While other studies have addressed dataset contamination, this paper's approach and emphasis on practical implications make it a significant contribution. The concept of eager pattern matching provides a useful framework for understanding this behaviour. The demonstration of its presence through the use of obfuscation is fairly novel, as is its application to the problem of dataset contamination.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM evaluation. It addresses a significant problem with a creative approach, provides strong empirical evidence, and discusses important practical implications. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses. The methodology is sound, and the findings are clearly presented. The study contributes new understanding to how these models make decisions.
