### Keyed Chaotic Tensor Transformations for Secure And Attributable Neural Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23655v1)
- **Authors**: Peter David Fagan
- **Abstract**: This work introduces a novel framework for secure and privacy-preserving neural network inference based on keyed chaotic dynamical transformations. The proposed method applies a deterministic, cryptographically seeded chaotic system to tensors, producing non-invertible, user-specific transformations that enable authenticated inference, tensor-level watermarking, and data attribution. This framework offers a scalable and lightweight alternative to conventional cryptographic techniques, and establishes a new direction for tensor-level security in AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Keyed Chaotic Tensor Transformations for Secure and Attributable Neural Inference":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method for secure and privacy-preserving neural network inference using keyed chaotic dynamical transformations. The core idea involves applying a deterministic, cryptographically seeded chaotic system to tensors (input and output), creating user-specific transformations. This obfuscates data, enabling authenticated inference, tensor-level watermarking, and data attribution. The authors propose that this framework offers a lightweight, scalable alternative to traditional cryptographic techniques and presents a new direction for tensor-level security in AI systems. The paper details the construction of the keyed chaotic transformation system, covering graph topology sampling, dynamical rule selection, chaos diagnostics, and system assembly. It then explores potential applications like key-authenticated inference, watermarking, and secure data contribution to shared models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The concept of using keyed chaotic systems for tensor transformation in the context of secure inference is relatively novel. While chaotic systems have been used in reservoir computing, their application as a cryptographic primitive for tensor-level security in neural networks is a new approach. Combining cryptography, dynamical systems, and representation learning offers a fresh perspective.  The specific methods detailed for graph sampling and dynamical rule selection, while leveraging existing techniques, are tailored to this particular problem domain.

**Significance:** The potential significance lies in offering a lightweight and flexible alternative to heavyweight cryptographic methods like homomorphic encryption or secure multi-party computation, which are often computationally expensive and complex to implement. The approach's architecture-agnostic nature is also beneficial. The ability to perform authenticated inference, tensor watermarking, and secure data contribution without modifying the underlying model structure is a significant advantage. The downstream applications, particularly in scenarios where data privacy and attribution are crucial (e.g., federated learning, content moderation), are compelling. The ability to enable per-user control over contributions is also a strong point.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The core idea of using keyed chaotic dynamics for tensor transformation is a creative and potentially useful method for security in neural networks.
*   **Lightweight Alternative:** It offers a more computationally efficient method than fully homomorphic encryption while still obfuscating the original data.
*   **Architecture Agnostic:** Can be applied to many different model architectures.
*   **Versatility:**  Supports various applications (watermarking, authentication, privacy).
*   **Detailed Construction:** The paper provides a reasonably detailed explanation of how the transformation system is constructed, including graph topology sampling and dynamical rule selection.
*   **Emphasis on Real-World Applicability:** The discussion of applications highlights relevant use cases in modern machine learning deployments.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The biggest weakness is the absence of empirical validation. The paper states that empirical validation and formal security analysis are planned for future revisions. Without experimental results, it's difficult to assess the actual performance, scalability, and security robustness of the proposed method. How effective is the obfuscation against various attacks? How much does it impact inference accuracy? These are critical questions that remain unanswered.
*   **Security Analysis:** The paper briefly mentions security properties (key-conditioned determinism, input obfuscation, etc.), but it lacks a rigorous security analysis. What are the potential attack vectors? How resistant is the system to key recovery attacks, chosen-input attacks, or model inversion attacks? Without a formal security analysis, the claims about security and privacy remain largely speculative.
*   **Parameter Tuning and Stability:**  The chaotic nature of the system could make it sensitive to parameter tuning and potentially unstable.  The paper acknowledges the need to maintain predictable chaotic behavior but doesn't fully address the challenges of ensuring long-term stability and consistent performance.
*   **Scalability Concerns:** While touted as a lightweight alternative, the computational cost of the chaotic transformations themselves, particularly for large tensors, needs to be evaluated.
*   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** The theoretical connection between the chaotic dynamics, graph properties, and resulting security characteristics could be strengthened. More exploration of how the graph structure affects security would be valuable.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the concept and the potential significance of a lightweight and flexible security mechanism for neural networks, the paper warrants a solid score. However, the lack of empirical validation and formal security analysis significantly limits its impact at this stage. The paper is essentially a promising proposal but requires substantial further work to demonstrate its practical value and robustness. While interesting, its current form is very preliminary.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 5/10

### VideoREPA: Learning Physics for Video Generation through Relational Alignment with Foundation Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23656v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangdong Zhang, Jiaqi Liao, Shaofeng Zhang, Fanqing Meng, Xiangpeng Wan, Junchi Yan, Yu Cheng
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in text-to-video (T2V) diffusion models have enabled high-fidelity and realistic video synthesis. However, current T2V models often struggle to generate physically plausible content due to their limited inherent ability to accurately understand physics. We found that while the representations within T2V models possess some capacity for physics understanding, they lag significantly behind those from recent video self-supervised learning methods. To this end, we propose a novel framework called VideoREPA, which distills physics understanding capability from video understanding foundation models into T2V models by aligning token-level relations. This closes the physics understanding gap and enable more physics-plausible generation. Specifically, we introduce the Token Relation Distillation (TRD) loss, leveraging spatio-temporal alignment to provide soft guidance suitable for finetuning powerful pre-trained T2V models, a critical departure from prior representation alignment (REPA) methods. To our knowledge, VideoREPA is the first REPA method designed for finetuning T2V models and specifically for injecting physical knowledge. Empirical evaluations show that VideoREPA substantially enhances the physics commonsense of baseline method, CogVideoX, achieving significant improvement on relevant benchmarks and demonstrating a strong capacity for generating videos consistent with intuitive physics. More video results are available at https://videorepa.github.io/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VideoREPA, a framework designed to improve the physical plausibility of videos generated by text-to-video (T2V) diffusion models. It addresses the problem that current T2V models often struggle to generate physically realistic content due to limitations in their inherent understanding of physics.  VideoREPA distills physics understanding from video understanding foundation models (VFMs) into T2V models by aligning token-level relations between the two. The core of the approach is a Token Relation Distillation (TRD) loss, which leverages spatio-temporal alignment to provide a soft guidance for fine-tuning pre-trained T2V models. The authors demonstrate that VideoREPA significantly enhances the physics commonsense of a baseline method (CogVideoX), leading to improvements on relevant benchmarks and generating videos that align better with intuitive physics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its application of representation alignment (REPA) to the domain of T2V models, specifically with the goal of injecting physical knowledge. While REPA has been used in image generation, the adaptation to video with the introduction of the TRD loss for spatio-temporal alignment is a significant contribution. It specifically addresses the shortcomings of directly applying image-focused REPA techniques to video. The focus on fine-tuning pre-trained models is also a critical departure from prior REPA methods tailored for from-scratch training.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its ability to address a key limitation of current T2V models, namely the lack of physical plausibility in generated videos. By improving the physics understanding capability of these models, VideoREPA opens up possibilities for generating more realistic and believable video content. The experimental results, particularly the quantitative improvements on the VideoPhy and VideoPhy2 benchmarks, clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The qualitative results further support the visual improvements in physics coherence.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper identifies a clear problem and proposes a well-motivated solution.
    *   The TRD loss is a novel and effective way to incorporate spatio-temporal dynamics into the representation alignment process.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, with quantitative and qualitative evaluations on established benchmarks.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of different components of the framework, and the ineffectiveness of a naive REPA approach.
    *   The authors address key limitations of applying image-based REPA to video, and show solutions such as their "soft alignment."

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The implementation details section reveals some complexities, particularly related to the dimension misalignment between the VFM and VDM features, which might limit the accessibility and reproducibility of the work without clear explanations. The need for interpolation and careful margin selection are noteworthy.
    *   While the paper shows a significant improvement, there could be a deeper dive into the failure cases. Specifically, discussing the limitations of the distilled knowledge and the types of physical interactions that are still challenging would be insightful.
    *   The reliance on a specific VFM (VideoMAEv2) could be seen as a limitation. Exploring the effectiveness of VideoREPA with a broader range of VFMs would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.

*   **Potential Influence:** VideoREPA has the potential to significantly influence the field of T2V generation by providing a practical approach to improving physical plausibility. The TRD loss could become a standard technique for injecting physics knowledge into T2V models. The framework's ability to leverage existing VFMs opens up opportunities for continual improvement as VFMs become more powerful. The work highlights the importance of spatio-temporal alignment in video generation, which could inspire further research in this area.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the T2V field. The method is sound, the problem is significant, and the experiments show tangible results. The adaptation of REPA through the TRD loss is novel and well-motivated. The ablations also clearly showcase the importance of the design choices.

However, the paper is not without its limitations. The implementation complexities and dependency on VideoMAE limit its potential wider adoption. Also, the analysis of the types of physical events where the method fails to provide better plausible generations could have added greater value.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Active Layer-Contrastive Decoding Reduces Hallucination in Large Language Model Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23657v1)
- **Authors**: Hongxiang Zhang, Hao Chen, Tianyi Zhang, Muhao Chen
- **Abstract**: Recent decoding methods improve the factuality of large language models~(LLMs) by refining how the next token is selected during generation. These methods typically operate at the token level, leveraging internal representations to suppress superficial patterns. Nevertheless, LLMs remain prone to hallucinations, especially over longer contexts. In this paper, we propose Active Layer-Contrastive Decoding (ActLCD), a novel decoding strategy that actively decides when to apply contrasting layers during generation. By casting decoding as a sequential decision-making problem, ActLCD employs a reinforcement learning policy guided by a reward-aware classifier to optimize factuality beyond the token level. Our experiments demonstrate that ActLCD surpasses state-of-the-art methods across five benchmarks, showcasing its effectiveness in mitigating hallucinations in diverse generation scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary, critical evaluation, and novelty score for the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Active Layer-Contrastive Decoding Reduces Hallucination in Large Language Model Generation" introduces ActLCD, a novel decoding strategy for large language models (LLMs) designed to mitigate hallucinations. ActLCD frames decoding as a sequential decision-making problem, using reinforcement learning to dynamically activate layer contrasting. This approach aims to leverage the factual knowledge encoded in deep layers of the LLM while avoiding the "overthinking" and error compounding often seen with static layer contrasting methods.  The paper presents experimental results across five benchmarks demonstrating that ActLCD surpasses existing state-of-the-art decoding strategies in reducing hallucinations across various generation scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to hallucination reduction in LLMs.  While layer contrasting decoding strategies (like DoLa) exist, the innovation lies in the *active* and *sequential* nature of the layer contrasting.  Using reinforcement learning to learn *when* to apply layer contrasting, guided by a reward-aware classifier, is a key departure from static methods and directly addresses the limitations of always-on layer contrasting.

*   **Significance:** Hallucination is a major impediment to the broader adoption and trustworthiness of LLMs.  A decoding strategy that demonstrably reduces hallucination without requiring model retraining or external knowledge sources has significant practical value. ActLCD has the potential to improve the factuality and reliability of generated text across diverse applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The ActLCD approach is well-motivated, clearly explained, and addresses specific weaknesses of existing methods. The justification for dynamic layer contrasting and the design of the reward function are logically sound.
    *   The empirical evaluation is comprehensive, covering a range of benchmarks (TruthfulQA, LongFact, StrategyQA, GSM8k, Package Hallucination) and LLMs of varying scales (Llama-3.1, GLM-4, Mistral-7B, Gemma, DeepSeek-V2). This strengthens the claims regarding the robustness and generalizability of ActLCD.
    *   The paper includes a detailed ablation study providing insights into the effectiveness of various components. It also analyses edge cases to demonstrate the strengths of the approach.
    *   The paper addresses the limitations of standard benchmarks. In particular, it includes a domain-specific (software package recommendation) hallucination task, broadening the scope.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The complexity of the reinforcement learning setup (BCQ) may make ActLCD harder to implement and tune compared to simpler decoding strategies. The paper could benefit from more detailed guidelines on hyperparameter selection and training procedures.
    *   While the paper includes a decoding latency comparison, a more thorough analysis of computational overhead might be warranted, especially concerning the training and implementation of the reinforcement learning policy.
    *   While the approach is demonstrated to improve performance, the underlying mechanism by which it does so would benefit from further analysis. The insights on error snowballing and the impact on reasoning (seen in Table 2), could form the base for a deeper study on what linguistic characteristics or reasoning processes are influenced.
    *   The reliance on GPT-4o for evaluation introduces a potential bias, even though the prompts are standardized and are based on pre-existing benchmarks. It would be good to have an analysis on the sensitivity of this benchmark to the choice of LLM evaluator.

*   **Potential Influence:**  ActLCD offers a promising direction for research in decoding strategies.  It will likely inspire further work on dynamic and adaptive decoding methods. It also provides a framework for incorporating sequence-level optimization into LLM generation, which could have broader applications beyond hallucination reduction.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 8. The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to reducing hallucinations in LLMs, addressing a critical problem in the field. The empirical results are strong, the method is well-motivated, and the analysis provides valuable insights. While the method is somewhat complex and some aspects warrant further investigation, the overall contribution is significant and likely to have a lasting impact on the field.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### D-AR: Diffusion via Autoregressive Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23660v1)
- **Authors**: Ziteng Gao, Mike Zheng Shou
- **Abstract**: This paper presents Diffusion via Autoregressive models (D-AR), a new paradigm recasting the image diffusion process as a vanilla autoregressive procedure in the standard next-token-prediction fashion. We start by designing the tokenizer that converts images into sequences of discrete tokens, where tokens in different positions can be decoded into different diffusion denoising steps in the pixel space. Thanks to the diffusion properties, these tokens naturally follow a coarse-to-fine order, which directly lends itself to autoregressive modeling. Therefore, we apply standard next-token prediction on these tokens, without modifying any underlying designs (either causal masks or training/inference strategies), and such sequential autoregressive token generation directly mirrors the diffusion procedure in image space. That is, once the autoregressive model generates an increment of tokens, we can directly decode these tokens into the corresponding diffusion denoising step in the streaming manner. Our pipeline naturally reveals several intriguing properties, for example, it supports consistent previews when generating only a subset of tokens and enables zero-shot layout-controlled synthesis. On the standard ImageNet benchmark, our method achieves 2.09 FID using a 775M Llama backbone with 256 discrete tokens. We hope our work can inspire future research on unified autoregressive architectures of visual synthesis, especially with large language models. Code and models will be available at https://github.com/showlab/D-AR
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "D-AR: Diffusion via Autoregressive Models."

**Summary:**

This paper proposes a novel framework called Diffusion via Autoregressive Models (D-AR), which recasts the image diffusion process as a standard autoregressive next-token prediction task. The core idea is to use a sequential diffusion tokenizer that converts images into a sequence of discrete tokens, where tokens at different positions correspond to different diffusion denoising steps.  This allows a standard autoregressive model (like LLaMA) to be trained on these tokens, effectively performing diffusion through autoregressive generation.  The paper highlights several advantages of this approach: support for fast inference with KV caching, the ability to generate consistent previews, and the potential for zero-shot layout-controlled synthesis. Experiments on ImageNet demonstrate that D-AR can achieve competitive FID scores compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of reinterpreting diffusion as an autoregressive problem is relatively novel.  While prior works have explored combining diffusion and autoregressive models, D-AR distinguishes itself by strictly adhering to the standard next-token prediction paradigm without modifying the underlying autoregressive architecture.  The sequential diffusion tokenizer is a key contribution, enabling the linearization of the diffusion process. However, the tokenizer itself relies on existing techniques from VQ-VAE and diffusion models. The innovation comes in how they're combined and applied.

* **Significance:** The potential significance lies in bridging the gap between diffusion models and the increasingly powerful large language models (LLMs). If D-AR can truly leverage the scalability and efficiency of autoregressive architectures, it could lead to more efficient and controllable image generation. The ability to generate consistent previews and perform zero-shot layout control are also potentially valuable features. However, the paper's results, while promising, do not convincingly surpass existing approaches, and the method's practical benefits over existing, optimized diffusion implementations remain unclear.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear and well-defined approach:** The paper presents a clear and well-defined approach to combining diffusion and autoregressive models.
    * **Interesting properties:**  The inherent support for consistent previews and potential for zero-shot control are intriguing advantages.
    * **Leveraging existing architectures:**  By using a standard autoregressive model (Llama), D-AR can potentially benefit from ongoing advancements in that field.
    * **Solid experimental results:** The paper provides results on ImageNet that demonstrate the feasibility of the approach.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Tokenizer Dependency**: The entire framework relies heavily on the design and effectiveness of the custom-built diffusion tokenizer. A more thorough analysis of the tokenizer's impact, including more detailed ablation studies, is warranted.
    * **Limited Improvement:** The achieved FID scores are competitive but not drastically better than existing methods, especially given the complexity introduced by the tokenizer. Further optimization and scaling of the model may be necessary to demonstrate a clear advantage.
    * **Computational Cost:** Despite the claim of leveraging efficient autoregressive inference (KV caching), the computational cost associated with training and inference, including the tokenizer, is not thoroughly addressed. A more comprehensive comparison of computational efficiency with existing diffusion models is needed.
    * **Limited Exploration of LLMs**:  The paper states that the method is designed for use with LLMs but does not extensively explore or evaluate the use of very large language models to improve performance, missing an opportunity to show off the potential for LLM integration.
    * **Lack of ablation of decoder**: Ablation shows that decoder is important, but does not explain why some ablation studies have little to no affect.
    * **Writing clarity and some missing details**: Several parts of the paper have little to no explanation of how they work, such as the equation, and the description of the tokenizer.

* **Potential Influence:** If further developed and optimized, D-AR could potentially influence the field by providing a pathway for integrating diffusion models with the LLM ecosystem. This could lead to new applications in multimodal generation and editing. However, more significant performance improvements and a more thorough analysis of the approach's practical benefits are needed to realize this potential.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a relatively novel approach to combining diffusion and autoregressive models that has the potential to be significant. However, the experimental results are not yet convincing enough to demonstrate a clear advantage over existing methods. The paper also suffers from several weaknesses, including dependencies on tokenizer architecture, missing LLM explorations, and unclear explanation of equations. Considering the strengths and weaknesses, and the current level of impact.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### OpenUni: A Simple Baseline for Unified Multimodal Understanding and Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23661v1)
- **Authors**: Size Wu, Zhonghua Wu, Zerui Gong, Qingyi Tao, Sheng Jin, Qinyue Li, Wei Li, Chen Change Loy
- **Abstract**: In this report, we present OpenUni, a simple, lightweight, and fully open-source baseline for unifying multimodal understanding and generation. Inspired by prevailing practices in unified model learning, we adopt an efficient training strategy that minimizes the training complexity and overhead by bridging the off-the-shelf multimodal large language models (LLMs) and diffusion models through a set of learnable queries and a light-weight transformer-based connector. With a minimalist choice of architecture, we demonstrate that OpenUni can: 1) generate high-quality and instruction-aligned images, and 2) achieve exceptional performance on standard benchmarks such as GenEval, DPG- Bench, and WISE, with only 1.1B and 3.1B activated parameters. To support open research and community advancement, we release all model weights, training code, and our curated training datasets (including 23M image-text pairs) at https://github.com/wusize/OpenUni.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the OpenUni paper:

**Summary:**

The OpenUni paper introduces a simple, lightweight, and fully open-source baseline for unified multimodal understanding and generation. It aims to integrate multimodal understanding (leveraging large language models or LLMs) and image generation (via diffusion models) into a single framework. The approach utilizes learnable queries and a lightweight transformer-based connector to bridge a pre-trained multimodal LLM and a diffusion model. The paper shows that OpenUni can generate high-quality, instruction-aligned images and achieve strong performance on various multimodal benchmarks with relatively few activated parameters (1.1B and 3.1B).  Crucially, the authors release all model weights, training code, and the curated training dataset (including 23M image-text pairs) to support open research and community advancement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The architecture of OpenUni isn't particularly novel. It's heavily inspired by MetaQuery and leverages existing components like InternVL3 and SANA. The core idea of using learnable queries to connect a frozen LLM to a diffusion model has already been explored.  The primary novelty lies in the *simplicity* of the design choices and the extent of the open-sourcing effort. While other works have explored similar architectures, OpenUni emphasizes a minimalist approach and provides a complete, easily accessible package for others to build upon. This focus on simplicity and accessibility is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance comes from its practical value.  The fully open-sourced nature of the project, including the model weights, code, and a large-scale training dataset, enables broader accessibility and facilitates further research in the multimodal space. While the performance achieved is competitive with existing methods, the value is not necessarily in surpassing state-of-the-art performance. The value is in providing an accessible, well-documented, and reproducible baseline that can accelerate progress in the field. This is particularly important for researchers and practitioners who may not have the resources to train large models from scratch or curate extensive datasets. The performance is strong *given* the simplicity, light weight nature of the architecture, and the open nature of the codebase.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Open Source:** The complete release of model weights, code, and training data is a major strength.
    *   **Simplicity:** The minimalist design facilitates understanding and modification, making it a valuable tool for research and education.
    *   **Competitive Performance:** OpenUni achieves impressive results on standard benchmarks with a relatively small number of parameters.
    *   **Clear and Reproducible:** The paper emphasizes a clear and well-documented training pipeline, enhancing reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Architectural Innovation:** The architecture relies heavily on existing components and lacks significant novelty in terms of design.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** Performance gains over existing models are incremental and do not represent a major breakthrough.
    *   **Text Rendering Issues:** As mentioned in the limitations, the model struggles with rendering text in generated images, which is a significant shortcoming.

*   **Potential Impact:** OpenUni has the potential to significantly impact the multimodal research community by lowering the barrier to entry. The open-source nature can foster collaboration, facilitate new research directions, and enable practical applications. However, the lack of substantial architectural innovation may limit its long-term influence. The model might become a widely used baseline, but may not spawn a new generation of radically different architectural approaches.

**Overall Score:**

Score: 7

**Justification:**

OpenUni is a strong and valuable engineering effort, not a groundbreaking theoretical advance. It receives a 7 because of its substantial practical significance and the positive impact it can have on the research community through its open-source nature, simplicity, and competitive performance. It lacks high architectural novelty, but its strengths in accessibility and reproducibility outweigh this limitation, making it a worthwhile contribution to the field. The limitations (text rendering, etc.) are acknowledged and future work is proposed to address them. The fully open nature is crucial, differentiating it from many papers that describe results but provide insufficient artifacts for rigorous validation and reuse.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ToolHaystack: Stress-Testing Tool-Augmented Language Models in Realistic Long-Term Interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23662v1)
- **Authors**: Beong-woo Kwak, Minju Kim, Dongha Lim, Hyungjoo Chae, Dongjin Kang, Sunghwan Kim, Dongil Yang, Jinyoung Yeo
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in using external tools to address user inquiries. However, most existing evaluations assume tool use in short contexts, offering limited insight into model behavior during realistic long-term interactions. To fill this gap, we introduce ToolHaystack, a benchmark for testing the tool use capabilities in long-term interactions. Each test instance in ToolHaystack includes multiple tasks execution contexts and realistic noise within a continuous conversation, enabling assessment of how well models maintain context and handle various disruptions. By applying this benchmark to 14 state-of-the-art LLMs, we find that while current models perform well in standard multi-turn settings, they often significantly struggle in ToolHaystack, highlighting critical gaps in their long-term robustness not revealed by previous tool benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the TOOLHAYSTACK paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TOOLHAYSTACK, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the robustness of tool-augmented language models (TALMs) in realistic, long-term interaction scenarios. It addresses a gap in existing benchmarks that primarily focus on short contexts and simplified instruction-following. TOOLHAYSTACK includes multiple task execution contexts, realistic noise, evolving goals, and fragmented context within a continuous conversation, allowing for the assessment of context maintenance and the handling of various disruptions. The benchmark consists of six scenarios (Context Recall, Information Shift, and Missing Context, each with two difficulty levels) and leverages a novel "needle-in-a-haystack" approach to interleave relevant and irrelevant information. The paper evaluates 14 state-of-the-art LLMs and finds that while they perform well in standard multi-turn settings, they often struggle significantly in TOOLHAYSTACK, highlighting critical gaps in their long-term robustness.  The paper also performs analysis to identify positional bias, hallucination errors, and the effect of chain-of-thought prompting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution is the formalization and benchmarking of long-term interaction robustness in TALMs. While existing benchmarks have explored multi-turn interactions and API response simulations, TOOLHAYSTACK innovatively introduces interleaved task flows and contextual noise, more closely mimicking real-world scenarios. The "needle-in-a-haystack" approach is also a relatively new take on benchmark design for this area.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem. The current overestimation of TALM's real-world readiness (as suggested by existing benchmarks) is a crucial concern. Highlighting the performance degradation under realistic conditions emphasizes the need for more robust LLMs suitable for long-term personal assistants and other interactive applications. The detailed ablation studies (positional bias, error analysis, CoT effect) further contribute to the understanding of TALM limitations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Realistic Scenarios:**  The design of the test suite closely mimics real-world interactions, including elements like evolving goals, shifting user intent, and accumulated noise, which are often overlooked in current TALM evaluations.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a broad range of models, including both closed-source and open-source LLMs.
    *   **In-depth Analysis:** The detailed analysis of different error modes provides valuable insights for future research directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited API scope:** The paper acknowledges that it works with a smaller set of APIs. This can be a limiting factor, as evaluation might not generalize well to all types of tool interactions and may be biased to the types of APIs included.
    *   **Dataset Generation Complexity:** While the automated generation pipeline is a strength for scalability, there's a risk that the dataset might have biases or artifacts inherent in the LLMs used during its generation.  More details about human validation beyond the automated checks would strengthen the confidence in the dataset's quality.
    *   **Lack of novel algorithm design or implementation:** While the benchmark is significant, the paper does not propose or evaluate any novel algorithmic solutions to address the issues raised by the benchmark.
    *   **Limited Contextual diversity:** The benchmark focuses on technical conversations. In real world scenario, it is possible to include different variety of conversation types such as conversations around emotions and general knowledge. The benchmark does not include any measure for that.

*   **Potential Influence:** TOOLHAYSTACK has the potential to significantly influence the direction of research in TALMs by:

    *   Shifting focus from short-term performance to long-term robustness.
    *   Encouraging the development of models better equipped to handle noise, context switching, and evolving user goals.
    *   Providing a standardized benchmark for evaluating these critical capabilities.

*   **Overall:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by highlighting and quantifying the limitations of current TALMs in realistic long-term interaction scenarios.  The TOOLHAYSTACK benchmark serves as a vital tool for stress-testing LLMs and guiding future research towards building more reliable and effective tool-augmented agents. The analyses presented contribute to a deeper understanding of current model weaknesses. However, the reliance on LLMs for dataset generation and limited API scope restrain the benchmark's overall impact. Also, there is no proposed algorithm design or implementation.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant benchmark that addresses an important gap in the evaluation of tool-augmented language models. The realistic scenarios, comprehensive evaluation, and in-depth analysis are strengths. The limitations of relying heavily on LLMs for the dataset creation, smaller API set, and lack of novel model implementation prevent it from scoring higher. Overall, it's a strong contribution but not without limitations that need to be addressed in future work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LoLA: Low-Rank Linear Attention With Sparse Caching
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23666v1)
- **Authors**: Luke McDermott, Robert W. Heath Jr., Rahul Parhi
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based large language models suffer from quadratic complexity at inference on long sequences. Linear attention methods are efficient alternatives, however, they fail to provide an accurate approximation of softmax attention. By additionally incorporating sliding window attention into each linear attention head, this gap can be closed for short context-length tasks. Unfortunately, these approaches cannot recall important information from long contexts due to "memory collisions". In this paper , we propose LoLA: Low-rank Linear Attention with sparse caching. LoLA separately stores additional key-value pairs that would otherwise interfere with past associative memories. Moreover, LoLA further closes the gap between linear attention models and transformers by distributing past key-value pairs into three forms of memory: (i) recent pairs in a local sliding window; (ii) difficult-to-memorize pairs in a sparse, global cache; and (iii) generic pairs in the recurrent hidden state of linear attention. As an inference-only strategy, LoLA enables pass-key retrieval on up to 8K context lengths on needle-in-a-haystack tasks from RULER. It boosts the accuracy of the base subquadratic model from 0.6% to 97.4% at 4K context lengths, with a 4.6x smaller cache than that of Llama-3.1 8B. LoLA demonstrates strong performance on zero-shot commonsense reasoning tasks among 1B and 8B parameter subquadratic models. Finally, LoLA is an extremely lightweight approach: Nearly all of our results can be reproduced on a single consumer GPU.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LoLA: Low-Rank Linear Attention With Sparse Caching":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LoLA, a novel inference-time strategy designed to enhance the performance of linear attention models, particularly in tasks requiring long-range dependencies and associative recall. LoLA addresses the limitations of linear attention, which suffers from memory collisions leading to inaccurate approximations of softmax attention. The core idea involves selectively caching important key-value (KV) pairs that are difficult for linear attention to memorize, storing them in a sparse, global cache in addition to the standard linear attention hidden state and a local sliding window. This selection is based on a "self-recall check" that identifies KV pairs that are poorly represented by the current hidden state. The approach is shown to significantly improve performance on passkey retrieval tasks (needle-in-a-haystack) and zero-shot commonsense reasoning, even with a smaller memory footprint compared to standard transformers. The authors also emphasize the lightweight nature of LoLA, making it reproducible on consumer-grade GPUs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the selective, sparse caching mechanism applied to linear attention. While sparse attention and linear attention are established areas of research, the combination with a targeted caching strategy based on a self-recall error metric is a relatively new contribution. The division of memory into local, sparse global, and recurrent hidden states also represents an innovation in memory management for linear attention. However, the individual components (sliding window, linear attention) are not novel on their own.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is multi-faceted:

    *   **Improved Associative Recall:** Demonstrably improves the ability of linear attention models to handle long-range dependencies and retrieve specific information. The improvement on the RULER benchmark is significant and shows that LoLA can overcome a major limitation of standard linear attention.
    *   **Memory Efficiency:** Achieving competitive performance with a smaller memory footprint is crucial for deploying large language models on resource-constrained devices.
    *   **Practicality:** The lightweight nature of the approach makes it accessible to researchers and practitioners without access to large-scale computing infrastructure.
    *   **Distillation Focus:** The focus on improving distilled models is a very relevant direction. Distillation is key to unlocking practical deployment in memory and compute-constrained settings.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of memory collisions in linear attention and their impact on associative recall.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The self-recall check provides a sensible criterion for selecting KV pairs to cache.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results on needle-in-a-haystack tasks and commonsense reasoning benchmarks provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of LoLA.
    *   **Ablation studies:** the score ablation studies were helpful in justifying the design.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The emphasis on reproducibility and the accessibility of the method are commendable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** LoLA is primarily an inference-time strategy, meaning that the model still needs to be trained using other techniques. The reliance on distillation also introduces some dependency on the quality of the teacher model. It would be even more impactful if the caching strategy can be part of the training procedure.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of LoLA likely depends on carefully tuning the cache size and sliding window size.
    *   **Computational Overhead:**  The overhead of the "self-recall check" and the maintenance of the sparse cache is not thoroughly examined in terms of runtime performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** LoLA has the potential to influence research on efficient attention mechanisms and memory management in language models. It could also enable the deployment of smaller, faster models in applications where memory and compute resources are limited. The idea of selectively caching "difficult-to-remember" memories could be adopted in other areas of machine learning. However, other approaches such as SSMs and their hybridizations with attention are quickly improving the situation.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7** to this paper. The core idea of sparse caching based on a self-recall metric is novel and addresses a key limitation of linear attention. The empirical results are compelling, and the practical aspects of the approach are well-emphasized. While the reliance on distillation and the limited scope of being only an inference-time method are weaknesses, the overall contribution is significant and warrants a high score. The benefits of faster inference, potential memory reduction in long contexts, and clear focus on the strengths of linear attention over softmax makes this a solid advance.

Score: 7

- **Score**: 7/10

### Fortune: Formula-Driven Reinforcement Learning for Symbolic Table Reasoning in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23667v1)
- **Authors**: Lang Cao, Jingxian Xu, Hanbing Liu, Jinyu Wang, Mengyu Zhou, Haoyu Dong, Shi Han, Dongmei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Tables are a fundamental structure for organizing and analyzing data, making effective table understanding a critical capability for intelligent systems. While large language models (LMs) demonstrate strong general reasoning abilities, they continue to struggle with accurate numerical or symbolic reasoning over tabular data, especially in complex scenarios. Spreadsheet formulas provide a powerful and expressive medium for representing executable symbolic operations, encoding rich reasoning patterns that remain largely underutilized. In this paper, we propose Formula Tuning (Fortune), a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that trains LMs to generate executable spreadsheet formulas for question answering over general tabular data. Formula Tuning reduces the reliance on supervised formula annotations by using binary answer correctness as a reward signal, guiding the model to learn formula derivation through reasoning. We provide a theoretical analysis of its advantages and demonstrate its effectiveness through extensive experiments on seven table reasoning benchmarks. Formula Tuning substantially enhances LM performance, particularly on multi-step numerical and symbolic reasoning tasks, enabling a 7B model to outperform O1 on table understanding. This highlights the potential of formula-driven RL to advance symbolic table reasoning in LMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FORTUNE: Formula-Driven Reinforcement Learning for Symbolic Table Reasoning in Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Formula Tuning (FORTUNE), a reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to improve the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform symbolic table reasoning. The core idea is to train LLMs to generate executable spreadsheet formulas that answer questions about tabular data.  FORTUNE uses the correctness of the formula's output as a reward signal, reducing the need for explicit formula annotations and encouraging reasoning-based formula derivation.  The paper includes a theoretical analysis comparing textual vs. symbolic reasoning and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) vs. RL.  The authors conduct extensive experiments on several table reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating that FORTUNE enhances LLM performance, especially on complex numerical and symbolic reasoning tasks.  They also show that initializing RL with SFT (cold-start RL) further improves performance.  The proposed method is shown to allow smaller models to outperform very large models that do not apply the techniques from the paper.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using spreadsheet formulas as an intermediate representation for table reasoning is not entirely new.  However, the authors' use of reinforcement learning with a correctness-based reward function to train LLMs to generate these formulas in a zero-shot or few-shot manner represents a significant advancement. The idea of using RL to steer models toward generating verifiable, executable outputs is novel and important. Prior works have predominantly relied on supervised learning with formula annotations or heuristic conversions, which have limitations in capturing the complexity required for diverse tasks and real-world application.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical limitation of LLMs: their struggle with accurate numerical and symbolic reasoning over tabular data. The ability to leverage spreadsheet formulas effectively opens up a powerful and expressive medium for representing symbolic operations and encoding rich reasoning patterns that LLMs have not been able to leverage before. This has the potential to significantly improve the performance of LLMs on table understanding tasks and other domains where structured reasoning is important. The fact that a 7B model can outperform much larger models is significant. The findings suggest that this approach democratizes the field, allowing smaller models to perform well.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a theoretical analysis that supports the benefits of symbolic reasoning over textual reasoning and RL over SFT, strengthening the claims and providing a solid foundation for the approach.
    *   **Extensive Experiments:** The experiments are conducted on a diverse set of table reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating the broad applicability of the proposed framework. This provides strong empirical evidence for the effectiveness of FORTUNE.
    *   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is well-structured and clearly written, making the approach and its advantages easy to understand.
    *   **Ablation Studies and Analysis:** The authors provide ablation studies and detailed analysis to investigate the contributions of different components of the framework, providing valuable insights into how the framework works.
    *   **Code Availability:** The authors release code to support their claims.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** RL training can be computationally expensive, which might limit the scalability of the framework to larger models and datasets.
    *   **Limited Dataset and Model Coverage:** The evaluation, while comprehensive, is limited to certain publicly available datasets and model architectures.
    *   **Reward Function Design:** The current reward function is based on execution accuracy, which might not capture other important factors like formula efficiency and token redundancy. However, the paper notes this limitation and suggests it as a direction for future work.
    *   **RL Instability:** RL training can be unstable. More work is needed to better stabilize the process, especially when a poor starting point or initial exploration phase means a model will rarely, if ever, generate a correct answer.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLMs and table understanding. The idea of using RL to train LLMs to generate executable formulas could be extended to other domains where structured reasoning is important, such as code generation and scientific discovery. The code release will also likely encourage further research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 8. The paper presents a novel and well-supported approach to improving symbolic table reasoning in LLMs. The theoretical analysis, extensive experiments, and clear writing contribute to the significance of the work. While the method has some limitations, the potential impact on the field and the thoroughness of the study justify a high score. The paper clearly advances the state-of-the-art.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### ImmunoDiff: A Diffusion Model for Immunotherapy Response Prediction in Lung Cancer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23675v1)
- **Authors**: Moinak Bhattacharya, Judy Huang, Amna F. Sher, Gagandeep Singh, Chao Chen, Prateek Prasanna
- **Abstract**: Accurately predicting immunotherapy response in Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer (NSCLC) remains a critical unmet need. Existing radiomics and deep learning-based predictive models rely primarily on pre-treatment imaging to predict categorical response outcomes, limiting their ability to capture the complex morphological and textural transformations induced by immunotherapy. This study introduces ImmunoDiff, an anatomy-aware diffusion model designed to synthesize post-treatment CT scans from baseline imaging while incorporating clinically relevant constraints. The proposed framework integrates anatomical priors, specifically lobar and vascular structures, to enhance fidelity in CT synthesis. Additionally, we introduce a novel cbi-Adapter, a conditioning module that ensures pairwise-consistent multimodal integration of imaging and clinical data embeddings, to refine the generative process. Additionally, a clinical variable conditioning mechanism is introduced, leveraging demographic data, blood-based biomarkers, and PD-L1 expression to refine the generative process. Evaluations on an in-house NSCLC cohort treated with immune checkpoint inhibitors demonstrate a 21.24% improvement in balanced accuracy for response prediction and a 0.03 increase in c-index for survival prediction. Code will be released soon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ImmunoDiff, an anatomy-aware diffusion model to predict immunotherapy response in Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer (NSCLC).  It synthesizes post-treatment CT scans from baseline imaging by incorporating anatomical priors (lobar and vascular structures) and clinical data (demographics, biomarkers, PD-L1 expression).  A novel cbi-Adapter is introduced to ensure consistent integration of imaging and clinical data.  Experiments on an in-house NSCLC cohort demonstrate improved accuracy in response and survival prediction compared to baseline models. The code is intended to be released.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The combination of anatomy-aware diffusion modeling *with* a cbi-Adapter for integrating diverse clinical variables is the primary source of novelty.  While diffusion models are being increasingly applied to medical imaging, the specific integration of lobar/vascular anatomy and the way clinical data is incorporated via the cbi-Adapter seem to be novel.  Existing work typically focuses on either anatomical guidance or clinical variable integration, but not their combined synergistic impact on post-treatment image synthesis *and* downstream prediction.

* **Significance:** Accurately predicting immunotherapy response is a critical unmet need.  If the reported improvements in balanced accuracy and c-index hold up under external validation, the work could be quite significant.  The ability to synthesize plausible post-treatment CT scans could also be beneficial for data augmentation and training more robust predictive models.  The integration of multiple data modalities (imaging, demographics, biomarkers) aligns with the growing trend in personalized medicine.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Approach:** The method integrates multiple data modalities (imaging, clinical variables, anatomical priors) in a coherent framework.
    * **Anatomical Awareness:**  The use of lobar and vascular structures is a valuable addition, reflecting the importance of these features in lung cancer.
    * **Improved Performance:** The reported improvements in balanced accuracy and c-index are promising.
    * **cbi-Adapter:**  The idea of the cbi-Adapter as a mechanism for consistently integrating imaging and clinical features appears to be innovative.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **In-House Dataset:** The evaluation is performed on a single, in-house dataset.  External validation on independent datasets is crucial to assess the generalizability of the findings.  The size and characteristics of the in-house dataset are crucial, but only summarized. Further details of the dataset (subtype of NSCLC, therapies used, etc.) are needed to understand if the results will generalize to a broader NSCLC population.
    * **Limited Comparison:** While various baselines are included, there's limited discussion or analysis of *why* ImmunoDiff outperforms them. A deeper dive into the failure modes of baselines and how ImmunoDiff addresses them would strengthen the paper.
    * **Code Release "Soon":** The lack of readily available code at the time of publication impacts reproducibility. The impact of the method cannot be readily verified or extended until released.
    * **Quantitative Image Quality:** The MMD and SSIM results are a bit puzzling. High structural similarity index (SSIM) is achieved even when stage-2 diffusion is applied, suggesting that more noise has been added. These measures should be compared to clinical-specific measurements to evaluate image quality.
    * **Clinical Significance:** The real impact of a 21% improvement in balanced accuracy should be examined in a clinical application. Would the results significantly alter the treatment plan?
* **Potential Impact:** If validated, ImmunoDiff could contribute to more accurate prediction of immunotherapy response, potentially leading to better patient selection and treatment strategies. The idea of synthesizing post-treatment scans is potentially valuable.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and potentially significant approach, the lack of external validation and some weaknesses in the presentation and analysis warrant a somewhat conservative score.  The reliance on an in-house dataset is a major limitation. The importance and impact depend greatly on validation via independent trials. Further analysis for how the diffusion model changes the images and if this change is clinically valid should be discussed.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Learning Compositional Functions with Transformers from Easy-to-Hard Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23683v1)
- **Authors**: Zixuan Wang, Eshaan Nichani, Alberto Bietti, Alex Damian, Daniel Hsu, Jason D. Lee, Denny Wu
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based language models have demonstrated impressive capabilities across a range of complex reasoning tasks. Prior theoretical work exploring the expressive power of transformers has shown that they can efficiently perform multi-step reasoning tasks involving parallelizable computations. However, the learnability of such constructions, particularly the conditions on the data distribution that enable efficient learning via gradient-based optimization, remains an open question. Towards answering this question, in this work we study the learnability of the $k$-fold composition task, which requires computing an interleaved composition of $k$ input permutations and $k$ hidden permutations, and can be expressed by a transformer with $O(\log k)$ layers. On the negative front, we prove a Statistical Query (SQ) lower bound showing that any SQ learner that makes only polynomially-many queries to an SQ oracle for the $k$-fold composition task distribution must have sample size exponential in $k$, thus establishing a statistical-computational gap. On the other hand, we show that this function class can be efficiently learned, with runtime and sample complexity polynomial in $k$, by gradient descent on an $O(\log k)$-depth transformer via two different curriculum learning strategies: one in which data consists of $k'$-fold composition functions with $k' \le k$ presented in increasing difficulty, and another in which all such data is presented simultaneously. Our work sheds light on the necessity and sufficiency of having both easy and hard examples in the data distribution for transformers to learn complex compositional tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the learnability of compositional functions by transformers, focusing on the k-fold composition task. This task requires a transformer to perform an interleaved composition of *k* input permutations and *k* hidden permutations. The authors prove a Statistical Query (SQ) lower bound demonstrating an exponential sample complexity for SQ learners. Conversely, they show that gradient descent on transformers can efficiently learn this function class with polynomial runtime and sample complexity using curriculum learning strategies. These strategies involve either presenting data with increasing difficulty (based on *k'*-fold compositions where *k'* ≤ *k*) or simultaneously training on all such data. The work highlights the importance of both easy and hard examples for transformers to learn complex compositional tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper tackles an important and relatively underexplored question: how can transformers learn complex compositional functions efficiently? While prior work has examined the expressiveness of transformers for multi-step reasoning, the focus on learnability with data distribution conditions (specifically curriculum learning) adds a new dimension. The combination of an SQ lower bound and upper bounds demonstrating the effectiveness of curriculum learning for transformers in this compositional task is novel. However, some components, such as using curriculum learning for optimization, build on existing knowledge.

*   **Significance:**  Understanding the learning dynamics of transformers on compositional tasks is crucial for building more robust and general-purpose AI systems. The paper provides a theoretical framework that offers insights into how data distribution impacts the learnability of these tasks. The identification of a statistical-computational gap and the demonstration that curriculum learning overcomes this gap are significant contributions to the theoretical understanding of transformer training.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides clear and mathematically sound proofs for both the lower and upper bounds.
    *   **Clear Task Definition:** The k-fold composition task is well-defined and allows for a focused analysis of compositional reasoning.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings on curriculum learning provide concrete guidance for training transformers on complex tasks.
    *   **Addresses an Important Question:** The work directly addresses a key question about the learnability of compositional reasoning in transformers, going beyond mere expressivity results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Task:** The k-fold composition task, while well-defined, is synthetic and may not perfectly capture the complexities of real-world compositional reasoning in natural language.
    *   **SQ Lower Bound Limitations:** While SQ lower bounds are valuable, they don't necessarily preclude other, more specialized learning algorithms from achieving polynomial sample complexity. Gradient descent isn't strictly an SQ learner, even if the heuristic suggests otherwise, meaning this lower bound isn't a hard limit on all learning.
    *   **Embedding Dimension:** Theorem 1 shows that embedding dimension d = Õ(Nk) is required to solve this task which is suboptimal, but a modification of the k-fold composition task with *m ≪ k* hidden permutations, and show that an embedding dimension of d = Õ(mN) suffices.
    *   **Dependence on Hyperparameters:** Theorem 3 assume that *k = 2<sup>L-1</sup>*, M > Ω(k<sup>4</sup>N<sup>6</sup>) and *η > (k<sup>2</sup>N<sup>3</sup> log(k)k)β<sub>0</sub>*.
    *   **Value Matrix Assumption:** The value matrix W at each layer is fixed to the desired block-sparse matrix which does not encode any information about the hidden permutations π in the target fr.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in transformer training strategies, particularly for tasks that require complex reasoning. The work encourages a deeper consideration of data distribution and curriculum design. It might also spur further theoretical investigation into the limitations and capabilities of transformers for compositional tasks.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the theoretical understanding of transformer learning, particularly in the context of compositional functions. The combination of lower and upper bounds provides a more complete picture of the problem, and the emphasis on curriculum learning offers practical guidance. The weaknesses related to the synthetic task and limitations of the analysis are legitimate but do not negate the paper's overall significance.

### DA-VPT: Semantic-Guided Visual Prompt Tuning for Vision Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23694v1)
- **Authors**: Li Ren, Chen Chen, Liqiang Wang, Kien Hua
- **Abstract**: Visual Prompt Tuning (VPT) has become a promising solution for Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) approach for Vision Transformer (ViT) models by partially fine-tuning learnable tokens while keeping most model parameters frozen. Recent research has explored modifying the connection structures of the prompts. However, the fundamental correlation and distribution between the prompts and image tokens remain unexplored. In this paper, we leverage metric learning techniques to investigate how the distribution of prompts affects fine-tuning performance. Specifically, we propose a novel framework, Distribution Aware Visual Prompt Tuning (DA-VPT), to guide the distributions of the prompts by learning the distance metric from their class-related semantic data. Our method demonstrates that the prompts can serve as an effective bridge to share semantic information between image patches and the class token. We extensively evaluated our approach on popular benchmarks in both recognition and segmentation tasks. The results demonstrate that our approach enables more effective and efficient fine-tuning of ViT models by leveraging semantic information to guide the learning of the prompts, leading to improved performance on various downstream vision tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Distribution-Aware Visual Prompt Tuning (DA-VPT), a novel framework for parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) of Vision Transformer (ViT) models. DA-VPT guides prompt learning by constructing semantic metrics between visual prompts, image patches, and class tokens in deep ViT layers. The method aims to leverage the attention mechanism to facilitate information flow by focusing on class-specific tokens and related prompts rather than considering arbitrary classes of information. The authors hypothesize that prompts can be guided to establish a bridge to connect class tokens and image patch semantic information through guided attention maps, leading to improved representation learning. They conduct extensive experiments on various image recognition and segmentation tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of their approach over standard VPT and related methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the guided prompt learning using a semantic metric, contrasting with previous works that mainly focused on manipulating prompt connections or initialization based on random data representations. The idea of establishing connections between visual tokens, prompts, and the class token using a learned metric is a solid and novel contribution, with the k-means clustering to set up these connections further emphasizing the semantic guidance. Specifically, the idea of learning the relationship between prompts and class-specific information is a departure from previous approaches and contributes novel insights into VPT optimization.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates significant performance improvements over existing VPT methods across a variety of visual tasks. The consistent gains achieved on both supervised and self-supervised pre-trained models highlight the generalizability and robustness of DA-VPT. Moreover, the study shows that improved results can be obtained with fewer learnable parameters than previous methods, reflecting efficiency.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The idea is well-motivated and addresses a gap in previous VPT research.
    *   The paper presents a clear and well-explained methodology.
    *   Extensive experiments validate the effectiveness of DA-VPT.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method introduces some additional hyperparameters (e.g., *β*, *λ*) that may require careful tuning for different tasks. It would be valuable to have more explicit guidelines for setting these hyperparameters.
    *   While the results are impressive, there isn't a deeply insightful qualitative analysis of how the learned prompts are functioning at a more granular level. Visualizations of attention maps are helpful, but a deeper dive into what types of features the prompts are capturing would strengthen the work.
    *   The computational costs, though not extreme, could be a barrier in specific resource-constrained scenarios, and further optimization might be required to mitigate this. The increased model capacity for multi-layer implementation could potentially pose limitations when applied to larger models.

*   **Potential Influence:** DA-VPT has the potential to influence future research in parameter-efficient fine-tuning for vision transformers. The semantic guidance approach could be applied to other PEFT methods and extended to other modalities. By enhancing the transfer learning capabilities of ViTs while maintaining parameter efficiency, DA-VPT can make sophisticated vision models more accessible and practical for a wider range of applications. The work encourages future work in better understanding token-prompt and token-token relationships through guidance, and offers a specific approach to do so.

*   **Rigor:** The experimental setup seems thorough, covering a large number of datasets and baselines. Ablation studies further support the claims by individually assessing components. However, a full statistical significance analysis (rather than just reporting means) across multiple runs of the experiments would make it even more rigorous.

*   **Consideration of Artifacts:** The paper does acknowledge the presence of artifacts, a recent critical topic in vision transformers, which adds credibility.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** DA-VPT presents a solid and novel contribution to the field of parameter-efficient fine-tuning for vision transformers. The performance gains are significant, and the methodology is well-explained and validated. While it has a few minor shortcomings (like needing more hyperparameter analysis), the novelty of the core idea and the significance of the results justify a strong score. The method has good potential to influence future research in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Can LLMs Reason Abstractly Over Math Word Problems Without CoT? Disentangling Abstract Formulation From Arithmetic Computation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23701v1)
- **Authors**: Ziling Cheng, Meng Cao, Leila Pishdad, Yanshuai Cao, Jackie Chi Kit Cheung
- **Abstract**: Final-answer-based metrics are commonly used for evaluating large language models (LLMs) on math word problems, often taken as proxies for reasoning ability. However, such metrics conflate two distinct sub-skills: abstract formulation (capturing mathematical relationships using expressions) and arithmetic computation (executing the calculations). Through a disentangled evaluation on GSM8K and SVAMP, we find that the final-answer accuracy of Llama-3 and Qwen2.5 (1B-32B) without CoT is overwhelmingly bottlenecked by the arithmetic computation step and not by the abstract formulation step. Contrary to the common belief, we show that CoT primarily aids in computation, with limited impact on abstract formulation. Mechanistically, we show that these two skills are composed conjunctively even in a single forward pass without any reasoning steps via an abstract-then-compute mechanism: models first capture problem abstractions, then handle computation. Causal patching confirms these abstractions are present, transferable, composable, and precede computation. These behavioural and mechanistic findings highlight the need for disentangled evaluation to accurately assess LLM reasoning and to guide future improvements.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) on math word problems, arguing that standard evaluation metrics like final-answer accuracy obscure two distinct sub-skills: abstract formulation (translating the problem into mathematical relationships) and arithmetic computation (executing those calculations). Through a disentangled evaluation framework applied to GSM8K and SVAMP datasets, using Llama-3 and Qwen-2.5 models of varying sizes, the authors demonstrate that without Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, LLMs are more bottlenecked by computation errors than by abstract formulation deficits.  They further show that CoT primarily aids in computation, and provide mechanistic evidence of an "abstract-then-compute" mechanism in single-pass inference using logit attribution and activation patching. They demonstrate transferability and composability of these abstractions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's main contribution lies in its disentangled evaluation framework for math word problems, which provides a more nuanced understanding of where LLMs struggle. While prior work has examined math reasoning, the explicit separation and targeted evaluation of abstract formulation vs. computation is a valuable methodological advancement.  The mechanistic interpretability findings are also novel, revealing an abstract-then-compute process within a single forward pass, and demonstrating the transferability of abstractions.

**Significance:** The findings have implications for how we evaluate and improve LLMs.  Highlighting the computational bottleneck suggests focusing on techniques that improve arithmetic accuracy. Furthermore, the "abstract-then-compute" mechanism opens avenues for research on better understanding and controlling the internal reasoning processes of LLMs.  This could ultimately lead to more robust and reliable AI systems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies a limitation of standard evaluation metrics.
*   **Rigorous methodology:**  The disentangled evaluation is well-designed, and the mechanistic interpretability analysis adds substantial support to the claims. The use of causal patching is appropriate and effectively demonstrates causal relationships.
*   **Comprehensive analysis:**  The study covers multiple datasets, models, and interpretability techniques, offering a robust set of results.
*   **Actionable insights:**  The findings provide clear directions for future research and development efforts (e.g., improving computation skills).

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited scope:** The study is restricted to English-language datasets and relatively simple arithmetic operations. Generalizability to more complex problems and languages is an open question.
*   **Model size limitations:** Analysis is restricted to models up to 32B. While informative, results on even larger models might reveal different behaviours.
*   **Reliance on GPT-4 for symbolic variant generation:** Although measures are taken for validation, gpt-40-mini is still a black box.
*   **Simplification of reasoning:** Decomposing the task into solely "abstraction" and "computation" may overlook other relevant reasoning skills.

**Potential Influence:**

This paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in LLM evaluation, particularly in tasks involving reasoning and problem-solving. It advocates for more fine-grained evaluation approaches that account for the interplay of different skills. It is also relevant to the mechanistic interpretability community seeking to understand how LLMs perform complex tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-supported analysis with practical implications. However, the relatively limited scope and reliance on specific models and datasets restrict its overall impact. The findings may not translate directly to much more complex multi-step problems or models drastically different in architecture.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SocialMaze: A Benchmark for Evaluating Social Reasoning in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23713v1)
- **Authors**: Zixiang Xu, Yanbo Wang, Yue Huang, Jiayi Ye, Haomin Zhuang, Zirui Song, Lang Gao, Chenxi Wang, Zhaorun Chen, Yujun Zhou, Sixian Li, Wang Pan, Yue Zhao, Jieyu Zhao, Xiangliang Zhang, Xiuying Chen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to socially grounded tasks, such as online community moderation, media content analysis, and social reasoning games. Success in these contexts depends on a model's social reasoning ability - the capacity to interpret social contexts, infer others' mental states, and assess the truthfulness of presented information. However, there is currently no systematic evaluation framework that comprehensively assesses the social reasoning capabilities of LLMs. Existing efforts often oversimplify real-world scenarios and consist of tasks that are too basic to challenge advanced models. To address this gap, we introduce SocialMaze, a new benchmark specifically designed to evaluate social reasoning. SocialMaze systematically incorporates three core challenges: deep reasoning, dynamic interaction, and information uncertainty. It provides six diverse tasks across three key settings: social reasoning games, daily-life interactions, and digital community platforms. Both automated and human validation are used to ensure data quality. Our evaluation reveals several key insights: models vary substantially in their ability to handle dynamic interactions and integrate temporally evolving information; models with strong chain-of-thought reasoning perform better on tasks requiring deeper inference beyond surface-level cues; and model reasoning degrades significantly under uncertainty. Furthermore, we show that targeted fine-tuning on curated reasoning examples can greatly improve model performance in complex social scenarios. The dataset is publicly available at: https://huggingface.co/datasets/MBZUAI/SocialMaze
